# 판정 주장 24건과 반박 검토

> 스포츠과학·코칭 두 종합을 합친 24개 주장(`synth:merge`)과, 주장마다 두 관점(evidence·applicability)의 반박 검토 결과다. 영어 원문.

## 종합 개요

Two independent evaluations were merged into 24 claims. Before merging, three spec facts were checked read-only in docs/ref5-program-spec.md:
- line 302: the 48 h claim reads "구조적으로 보장된다" ("structurally guaranteed");
- line 842: Coleman 2024 is described as "열위" ("inferior");
- line 792: the protein range is 1.6–2.2 g/kg; line 904: the audit criterion is days below 1.6 g/kg.

Both evaluations agree that REF5 v1.4 is a coherent, conservative, low-input strength program. Its core stimulus matches the strongest current evidence for 1RM strength:
- squat comes first in every session;
- hard squat runs about 2 times a week, at least 48 h apart;
- heavy 2–3-rep sets at about 82–88% of estimated 1RM;
- 2–3 work sets per lift per session, about 3–6 hard sets per main lift per week;
- no sets to failure;
- no scheduled deloads (the deload evidence is too weak to require them).

The session queue, density valve and noise-tolerant 2.5 kg windows suit an irregular 2–4/week schedule. The squat cadence ceiling (about 34–43 kg/yr of H3) is above even the best competitive yearly squat gains, so cadence will not limit long-run progress. Window outcome quality will, and it will bite squat first because squat has the longest window.

**Main weaknesses (both lenses).** These sit in measurement and documentation, not in the stimulus:
1. **Bodyweight is a default value.** It is 75.00 kg in 28 of 29 sessions, so the G1 KPI, PULL totals and the §23.1 recovery audit cannot be computed. Sleep, protein, climbing and pain are not recorded either.
2. **The 106/106 PASS record cannot yet test efficacy.** It is re-acquisition below the June 2026 3×3 loads. The first informative zone (H3 about 90–92.5 kg) is about 7–11 weeks away.
3. **Nothing re-anchors REF to true capacity.** The binary slowdown judgment is low-resolution at current loads. REF (107.2/104.1/111.1) is already above the last demonstrated maxima.
4. **Two spec errors.** §7.3's "structurally guaranteed" 48 h heavy-pull/OAP separation does not hold: 3 of 11 adjacencies were under 48 h. §22.2 calls the Coleman 2024 result "inferior", which overstates it.
5. **Implementation frictions:**
   - the zero-tolerance 168 h boundary cost 2 hard exposures, each by minutes;
   - plan cutovers erase window, stagnation and structureReview history;
   - the OAP rung dwell rule cannot be enforced;
   - the OAP rungs measure assisting-hand placement as much as strength.

**Costs of the user's own constraints.** These are reported as costs, not defects: little hypertrophy stimulus during a deficit; DL and OHP held at maintenance intensity by the C7/C8 caps; no maximal reality check.

**Where the lenses disagree:**
- **Verdict labels:**
  - frequency cap: tradeoff vs strength;
  - weekly volume: tradeoff vs strength;
  - window cadence: strength vs tradeoff;
  - hypertrophy: weakness vs tradeoff;
  - climbing: tradeoff vs risk.
- **Slowdown judgment bias:** B thinks the tie-break biases it toward PASS. A thinks the net direction is uncertain, because people also underpredict reps to failure.
- **Whether to put protocol candidates on the table now:**
  - A offers a ≥166 h lookback tolerance; B calls it unnecessary.
  - B offers OHP rep progression and moving OAP ahead of BP focus as §26 proposals; A says no change for the first and keeps the second conditional.
- **Confidence:** on intensity and proximity, A high, B medium; on G1, A medium, B low.

**One recommendation narrowed.** B suggested reading MAINTAIN windows at a 3–10%/yr benchmark as "predicted, not a signal to restructure". That would conflict with the §21.1 pre-registration that low acquisition is first read as insufficient stimulus or recovery. It was narrowed to a context-only note.

**Goals:**
- **G1 (175 kg high-bar at about 70 kg).** It needs about +70% on a ~103 kg reference. It is a rare, upper-percentile, multi-year direction that cannot be credibly dated or confirmed by REF.
- **G6 (one-arm chin-up).** The limit is bilateral weighted-pull strength relative to bodyweight, not ladder speed: about +47% of bodyweight now against +60–67% in practitioner heuristics.

**Highest-value actions.** None of these changes the protocol:
1. Measure bodyweight.
2. Fix spec §7.3 and §22.2.
3. Log climbing, elbow pain, knee pain and three recovery items, outside the judgment.
4. Standardize the OAP assisting-hand contact.
5. Add a "first slowed rep" field that the judgment does not read.
6. Do not judge adaptation rate until each lift's standard exceeds its June 2026 load.

Every protocol candidate in the claims is optional, changes a single element, and is conditional on the §21.1/§23.1 path. None relaxes FAIL 0, HOLD ≤1 or the volume veto, and none adds accessories, tests, RIR, AMRAP or scheduled deloads.

**Corrections to the brief:**
- The DB holds 29 REF5 sessions; the 10/14 session was added and logged bodyweight 76.
- DL (8/11) and OHP (9/24) each had one MAINTAIN window forced by their caps.

**Evidence caveats:**
- Nearly all trials ran 5–12 weeks, mostly in young and often less-trained men.
- No study validates REF5's binary slowdown judgment, its 6/4-exposure windows, or its 48 h/168 h gates.
- No trial covers weighted or one-arm pull-ups, climbing combined with heavy pulling, or quadriceps-tendon pain under high-bar loading.
- Program comparisons rest on program documents and secondary summaries.

## C1 · intensity · strength · 확신 high · 변경 등급 none

The hard and focus slots sit in the load band (≥~80% of 1RM) that the strongest evidence links to maximal-strength gains: SQ H3 3x3 at about 82-83%, SQ H2 3x2 at about 87-88%, BP focus at about 82-85% and PULL focus at about 82% of the best-estimate maxima. The SQ V sets (~70-71% x5), BP volume (~70%), DL (~64-67%) and OHP (~56-59%) are practice and volume work, not 1RM-specific stimuli. Because the strength-load curve flattens above about 65%, the ~70% sets are still not trivial.

**근거**

- Currier et al. 2023, Br J Sports Med 57(18):1211-1220, Bayesian network meta-analysis of 178 studies: higher-load (>80% 1RM) multi-set prescriptions ranked top for strength (HM3 SMD 1.60 vs control); the 3x vs 2x ordering was not robust. https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/57/18/1211
- ACSM Position Stand 2026 (Currier ... Phillips), Med Sci Sports Exerc 58(4):851-872: strength is enhanced by loads ≥~80% 1RM, 2-3 sets, placing the exercise first, and ≥2 sessions/wk. Much of the evidence is from less-trained people. https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897
- Robinson et al. 2024, Sports Med 54(9):2209-2231: load (%1RM) is the more influential variable for maximal strength. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-024-02069-2
- Lopez et al. 2021, MSSE 53(6):1206-1216: ≤8RM beat >15RM for strength (SMD ~0.6); heavy vs moderate was not significant (P=.068). https://jyx.jyu.fi/jyx/Record/jyx_123456789_75929
- Swinton et al. 2024, Sports Med 54(6):1579-1594: the strength-load curve is monotonic, with a reduced incline from 65% to 100%. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-024-02006-3
- Internal record, loads as of 10/14: H3 85, H2 90, V 72.5, BP focus 85 / volume 70, PULL focus total 90 (+15), DL 75, OHP 32.5. Reference maxima from pre-REF5 records: SQ ~102-103, BP ~100, PULL total 110, DL ~112-117, OHP ~55-58.

**추론**

Both lenses agree on direction and verdict. Direct evidence: meta-analytic and position-stand support for loads of ≥~80% 1RM in multi-set work, mostly from short trials in less-trained people. Extrapolation: every percentage rests on an Epley e1RM (~103 from 92.5x3 at RPE 9-10), uncertain by about ±3-5 kg. If capacity has already risen, the true percentages are lower. Lens A rated this high; lens B rated it medium because of that e1RM uncertainty. Merged: high confidence that the structure is sound, medium confidence in the exact percentages (see C10).

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: The SQ hard sets sit in the ≥80% 1RM category that meta-analyses (Currier 2023) and the ACSM 2026 position stand rank highest for strength. Taking an e1RM of ~102-105 kg from 92.5x3 at RPE 9-10, H3 is about 81-84% and H2 about 86-89%. PULL focus is about 82% of a measured 110 kg total single, assuming bodyweight is still ~75 kg. BP focus is probably in the same category at ~84-85%, but its ~100 kg anchor could not be verified.

This evidence is direct only for the general direction. It comes mostly from short trials in less-trained people doing effortful sets, and for weighted pull-ups it is wholly extrapolated. The ≥80% line is a coding cutoff, not a threshold:
- The load-strength curve is continuous, with a reduced incline from ~65% to 100% (Swinton 2024).
- Heavy vs moderate loads taken to failure did not differ significantly (Lopez 2021).
- A bench-press RCT in trained men found a 70-85% band best (Pareja-Blanco 2026, not re-verified here).

The evidence therefore does not favour H2 over H3, and it puts only a modest gap between the hard slots and ~70-79% work. V and BP volume (~70%), DL (~64-67%, anchor unverified) and OHP (~56-59%, anchor unverified) are lower-load volume and practice work. At ~70% x5, leaving ~8-10 reps in reserve, they are probably productive but less effective per set. That rests on extrapolation, chiefly Robinson 2025, not on load-curve data. Every percentage carries ±3-5 kg of anchor uncertainty and drifts down if capacity has risen; at a 1RM of ≥107, H3 falls below 80%.

Verdict: the direction holds, but the claim overstates two things: how precise the percentages are, and how much the "≥80% band" actually buys.

WHAT HOLDS (direct evidence, checked):
- Currier 2023 BJSM. I re-read the BMJ page: higher load is defined as ≥80% 1RM, and "top-ranked prescriptions for muscle strength were characterised by lifting heavier loads". At matched sets and frequency, HM2 vs LM2 gave a 95% probability that the heavier load wins. One small inconsistency: the evidence pack gives the cutoff as ">80%". Either way, H3 at ~82% clears it.
- The ACSM 2026 abstract says ≥80% 1RM and 2-3 sets, and that most of its evidence comes from inexperienced trainees.
- Robinson 2024 Key Points: load (%1RM) is the more influential variable for strength.
- Swinton 2024 (Springer page, re-read): "monotonic but non-linear ... reduced incline between 65 and 100%"; maximal strength is "likely to be maximised with the heaviest loads".
- Arithmetic checks out: SQ H3 85/102-103 = 82.5-83.3%; H2 90/102-103 = 87.4-88.2%; V 72.5 = 70.4-71.1%; PULL 90/110 = 81.8%; BP 85/100-101 = 84-85%; DL 75/112-117 = 64-67%; OHP 32.5/55-58 = 56-59%.

FIDELITY PROBLEMS:
1. "≥80%" is a coding cutoff in Currier and ACSM, not a physiological threshold. Swinton's continuous model shows the curve flattening from about 65% to 100%. Swinton's text gives no numeric predictions at 70/80/90%, so the gap between ~75% and ~82% cannot be quantified from it.
   - Lopez 2021: heavy vs moderate load was not significant (P=.068).
   - Pareja-Blanco 2026 (MSSE; 158 trained men, bench press, per the verified evidence pack) found the 70-85% band gave the greatest 1RM gain. I could not re-read that abstract myself: PubMed returned a reCAPTCHA and Europe PMC returned HTTP 429.
   - So the evidence does not favour H2 at 87-88% over H3 at 82%. It also separates the hard slots only modestly from ~70-79% work. "In the band the strongest evidence links to strength" is true as a category, but it implies a step the data do not show.
2. Population. Swinton's groups were 54% untrained, 41% recreational and 5% highly trained. ACSM says the same about its sources, and Currier was mixed (45% women). Swinton found training status did not improve its model, which helps, but trained-specific evidence for load bands is thin.
3. How effortful the sets were. Lopez 2021 and Schoenfeld 2017 used sets to failure. REF5 hard sets leave about 4-5 reps in reserve (RIR), from Nuzzo's main model: ~8-9 reps possible at 82%. Applying the heavy-load benefit to far-from-failure triples is an extrapolation. It is supported by Robinson 2024 (no RIR effect on strength once load is adjusted for) and by velocity-loss trials.
4. The percentage anchors.
   - SQ rests on an Epley e1RM from 92.5x3 at RPE 9-10. That gives about 101.75 if the set was RPE 10, and about 104.8 if it was RPE 9 (treated as a 4RM). So H3 is plausibly 81-84%. If capacity has risen to ≥107 (plausible, since every window so far ended INCREASE), H3 falls below 80% and V to ~68%.
   - PULL is the best-anchored lift. The spec (§19.6, docs/ref5-program-spec.md:778) records a measured single of 110 kg total (BW 75 + 35). But logged bodyweight appears to be a default, so ±2 kg of BW moves PULL to ~80-84%. No load or strength trial covers weighted pull-ups, so treating PULL at ~82% as in the strength band is extrapolation.
   - I could not trace the BP (~100), DL (~112-117) or OHP (~55-58) maxima anywhere in the repo docs. The spec's control REFs (BP 101, DL 100, OHP 50) are explicitly not 1RMs, and spec line 233 forbids reading REF ratios as %1RM. Those three percentages therefore rest on records I could not verify.
5. "The ~70% sets are still not trivial." This is a fair reading of Swinton's flattening curve, but it rests on typical-effort sets. REF5 V and BP-volume sets at ~70% x5 leave about 8-10 RIR (~15 reps possible at 70%, per Nuzzo), where proximity data are sparse. The best indirect support is Robinson 2025, whose 4-6 RIR squat group actually trained at about 8 RIR and still gained 13.8 kg. Treat the claim as directional inference, not a quantified finding.

CITATION ACCURACY: All six cited items say roughly what the claim attributes to them. Overall confidence should drop from high to medium, because the claim's own reasoning concedes anchor uncertainty and points 1, 4 and 5 add to it.

Limits: the web-search budget was already exhausted, so no new counter-evidence searches were possible. The Pareja-Blanco 2026 abstract could not be re-read (PubMed reCAPTCHA; Europe PMC rate-limited).

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: REF5's hard and focus slots sit at or just above the ~80% 1RM band that the meta-analyses and the ACSM 2026 position stand associate with maximal-strength gains. The other slots are lighter practice and volume work.

Hard and focus slots, as % of the user's own records (PULL is a measured 110 single; the rest are 4-5-month-old Epley estimates, mostly with RPE not logged, so these percentages are upper bounds):
- SQ H3 3x3 at 85: about 81-83%
- SQ H2 3x2 at 90: about 86-88%
- BP focus 3x3 at 85: about 84-86%
- PULL focus 3x3 at 90 total: about 82%

Lighter slots, which are practice and volume work rather than 1RM-specific stimuli:
- SQ V 72.5x5: about 69-71%
- BP volume 70x5: about 70%
- DL 75 2x4: about 64-68% of e1RM 110-117
- OHP 32.5 2x6: about 56-59% of e1RM 55-58

Because the strength-load curve flattens from about 65% upward, the ~70% sets are still not trivial.

Caveats:
- H3 is only 1-3 points above Currier's >80% cutoff. If capacity has risen since June, it may already sit at about 80%.
- The heavy-load advantage was shown with sets to failure. Applying it to REF5 sets that stop well short of failure is an extrapolation.
- These percentages must come from the user's records, never from the control REFs (spec §6.3).

Confidence is high for the split itself and medium for the exact percentages.

I checked the claim against the spec, the engine and a read-only SELECT on the prod DB. Its rule description is correct and its percentages hold within a point or two. A few numbers need tightening.

What matches:
- Prescriptions match the spec (§5.2, §6.2) and the engine (ref5.ts deriveRef5Standards): H3 3x3, H2 3x2 at H3+5, V 3x5 at floor2.5(H3*72.5/82.5), BP focus 3x3, BP volume 2x5 at floor2.5(focus*70/82.5), PULL focus 3x3 total load, DL 2x4, OHP 2x6.
- Current loads in the DB match the claim: H3 85, H2 90, V 72.5, BP 85 / 70, PULL +15 (total 90 at the logged 75 kg), DL 75, OHP 32.5.

Reference maxima, from the user's own records:
- **SQ:** 92.5x3 for 3 sets at RPE 9 on 6/16. Epley gives 101.8, or about 104-105 if the 1 rep in reserve is counted. So H3 is about 81-83.5%, H2 about 86-88.5% and V about 69-71%.
- **BP:** 90x3 on 6/16 and 6/20, RPE not logged. That gives at least 99, so BP focus is about 84-86%. The claim's 82-85% is slightly low at the top end. BP volume is about 70%.
- **PULL:** a measured single of 110 total (75 + 35, spec §19.6). PULL focus 90 is 81.8%. This is the only true 1RM; the other maxima are estimates.
- **DL:** 100x5 in late May (Epley 116.7) and 100x3 in June (110). DL 75 is about 64-68%, so the range should read 110-117, not 112-117.
- **OHP:** 50x5 (58.3) and 50x3 at RPE 10 (55). OHP is about 56-59%.

The split holds even with a ±3-5 kg error in the estimates. The hard and focus slots stay at about 79-88%. The V, volume, DL and OHP slots stay at 75% or below.

Caveats the claim should carry:
1. **The maxima are 4-5 months old.** Most sets have no RPE logged, so the Epley numbers are lower bounds and the true percentages are probably lower.
2. **H3 is close to the threshold.** Currier's cutoff is strictly above 80%, and H3 sits only 1-3 points over it. If true capacity has risen by about 3-4 kg, H3 would be at about 80%.
3. **The load-band evidence comes from sets to failure.** Lopez 2021 and Schoenfeld 2017 compared loads with sets taken to failure. REF5 hard sets end roughly 4-5 reps short of failure. Carrying the heavy-load advantage over to those sets relies on Robinson 2024's finding that proximity matters little once load is controlled, so it is an extrapolation.
4. **Spec compliance.** §6.3 forbids describing control REFs as %1RM, and §22.1 says study %1RM cannot be equated with REF. The claim complies because it uses record-based maxima. Anyone who swaps in the control REFs would get different figures: DL 100 gives 75% and OHP 50 gives 65%.

Minor record note: the DB has a session on 10/14 (H3 85 3x3, BP 85 3x3, OHP 32.5 2x6, OAP L 3+3, R 3+2). The "8-day gap" ended that day. Loads are unchanged, so C1 is unaffected.

The claim recommends no change (changeClass none), so it breaches no fixed constraint.

## C2 · proximity/autoregulation · strength · 확신 high · 변경 등급 observation-only

Ending every set before technique loss or at the first obvious slowdown, never to failure (§11.1), with session RPE around 7-8, is well supported for strength and for recovery between sessions. Its cost falls on hypertrophy, not strength. At current loads the load prescription, not the stop rule, sets effort: inferred RIR on hard sets is about 2.5-5. Neither lens finds evidence for the athlete's diagnosis that past stalls came from 'insufficient proximity'. Taking sets closer to failure is not the evidence-favoured first fix for a future strength stall; keeping load in step with capacity matters more.

**근거**

- Robinson et al. 2024, Sports Med 54(9):2209-2231: estimated-RIR slope for strength 0.003 (95% CI -0.012 to 0.018), i.e. negligible; for hypertrophy -0.019 (CI excludes 0). Load-adjusted and exploratory. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-024-02069-2
- Robinson et al. 2025, Int J Strength Cond 5(1), 38 trained men over 8 wk: bench 4-6 RIR +9.05 kg vs 0 RIR +0.71 kg. The nominal 4-6 RIR squat group actually trained near 8 RIR and still gained +13.8 kg, vs +18.1 kg at 1-3 RIR (CIs overlap). https://journal.iusca.org/index.php/Journal/article/view/393
- Grgic et al. 2022, J Sport Health Sci 11(2):202-211: failure vs non-failure for strength ES -0.09 (-0.22 to 0.05). https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jshs.2021.01.007
- Pareja-Blanco et al. 2017, Scand J Med Sci Sports 27(7):724-735: VL20 and VL40 gave similar squat strength gains with 40% fewer reps; VL20 preserved MHC IIX (Smith machine). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/27038416/
- Jukic et al. 2023, Sports Med: velocity-loss threshold did not affect strength gains. https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01754-4
- Moran-Navarro et al. 2017, Eur J Appl Physiol 117(12):2387-2399: non-failure squat and bench protocols recovered faster at 24-48 h than sets to failure. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28965198/
- Ruple et al. 2023, Physiol Rep 11(9):e15679: 0-1 vs 4-6 RIR on SQ/BP/DL over 5 wk showed no 1RM condition x time interaction. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37144554/
- Graham & Cleather 2021, JSCR 35(9):2451-2456: squat gains were larger when load kept pace with capacity (+10.8% vs +7.1%; mean 83.6% vs 80.4% 1RM). https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0000000000003164

**추론**

Direct evidence for strength comes from RCTs and meta-regressions over 5-12 weeks, mostly in young men. The rule also fits the athlete's C11 (no grinding) and REF5's 48 h spacing. Caveat: Robinson 2025's squat point estimate favoured 1-3 RIR by about 4 kg, so hard sets at very high RIR may cost a little. That gap closes on its own as standards rise. The REF5 RIR figures are inferred from Nuzzo 2024 population means plus one RPE 9-10 triple. Lens A rated this high, lens B medium; B's lower rating attaches to the inferred RIR figures, not to the direction.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Ending every set before technique loss, or at the first obvious slowdown, and never to failure (spec §11.1; session RPE around 7-8) is well supported for strength in 5-12-week trials. Supporting evidence: Robinson 2024 (load-adjusted RIR slope about 0, CI -0.012 to 0.018); Grgic 2022 (ES -0.09); Jukic 2023; Ruple 2023; and Robinson 2025, where bench gains at 4-6 RIR roughly matched 1-3 RIR and both beat 0 RIR. The rule also supports recovery: non-failure work recovers faster at 24-48 h than sets taken to failure (Moran-Navarro 2017, which includes a ~5-RIR 3x5(10) arm; Refalo 2023 SMO, bench only). Its clear cost is hypertrophy. A small strength cost is not ruled out when sets end very far from failure, at about 6-8 or more RIR. In Robinson 2025 the nominal 4-6 RIR squat group actually trained at about 8 RIR and gained about 4.3 kg (about 24%) less than the 1-3 RIR group, with overlapping CIs and about 9-10 per group. The velocity-loss literature points the same way: VL0 underperformed moderate VL (Pareja-Blanco 2026; Zhang 2023). This applies mainly to V squats at about 70% and possibly to early H3 sets. At current loads, the load prescription rather than the stop rule sets effort. Inferred hard-set RIR is roughly 2-5 with ±2-3 reps of uncertainty. Nuzzo 2024 population means alone give about 4-5, and the low end depends on a single RPE 9-10 triple. The evidence contradicts the idea that near-failure sets (0-1 RIR) are needed for strength. It does not refute the athlete's own Tactical Barbell diagnosis as stated ("TM drift up to real capacity unnoticed"). Graham & Cleather 2021 is direct RCT evidence that fixed loads lagging capacity, so that sets drift further from failure, cost squat strength (+7.1% vs +10.8%). With REF5's fixed reps, 'proximity' and 'load tracking capacity' are the same lever. The evidence-favoured response to a future stall is therefore the §23.1 recovery and realized-volume audit, with load kept in step with capacity, not grinding or adding reps toward failure. No log evidence exists to judge the 5x5 or 5/3/1 stalls either way.

The core holds: stopping short of failure is fine for strength and better for recovery. Three sub-claims overstate the evidence, and one conflicts with the claim's own citation.

1) The claim says "Neither lens finds evidence for the athlete's diagnosis." This is contradicted by Graham & Cleather 2021 (JSCR 35(9):2451-2456; https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0000000000003164), which the claim itself cites. In that RCT the RIR group held proximity roughly constant while loads rose. The fixed-load group drifted further from failure as it got stronger, trained at a lower mean intensity (80.4% vs 83.6%) and gained less (back squat +7.1% vs +10.8%). That is exactly the athlete's stated Tactical Barbell mechanism ("insufficient proximity; TM drift up to real capacity unnoticed"). The claim sets 'closer to failure' against 'load in step with capacity'. With fixed rep targets these are the same variable. What the evidence does refute is narrower: sets do not need to be at 0-1 RIR or ground out. For the 5x5 and 5/3/1 stalls there are no logs, so the honest status is insufficient evidence, not 'no evidence'.

2) The claim says the cost falls "on hypertrophy, not strength". This holds within roughly 0-5 RIR. Above that the evidence is sparse and leans the other way:
- Robinson 2025 abstract (re-fetched and confirmed: 38 men, 4 arms; squat 4-6 RIR +13.79 kg, CI 7.43-20.28, vs 1-3 RIR +18.05 kg, CI 12.12-23.88). The point estimate is about 24% smaller. The 'actually about 8 RIR' detail comes from the full text according to the verified evidence pack; it is not in the abstract.
- Pareja-Blanco 2026 MSSE: VL25 gave a greater 1RM gain than VL0.
- Zhang 2023: an inverted U, with VL0 lowest.
- The Robinson 2024 strength slope CI (-0.012 to 0.018 per RIR) spans about -0.06 to +0.09 SMC over 5 RIR, so a small effect cannot be excluded.
The sentence "the gap closes on its own as standards rise" is an inference, not evidence. It is plausible only because REF5's PASS-driven increments (about 37 kg/yr of H3 at the current pace) outrun plausible capacity growth.

3) The inferred RIR of 2.5-5: Nuzzo 2024 means alone imply about 5 RIR for H3 at 85 kg (~82%) and about 4 for H2 at 90 kg (~87%). Applying Epley to the athlete's 92.5x3 at RPE 9-10 gives about 3-4 for H3 and 2-3 for H2. A range of 2-5 with ±2-3 reps (between-person SD about 2.5 reps at 80%) is the honest version. If capacity has risen since then, current sets are further from failure. The causal statement "the load sets effort, not the stop rule" is still correct: with 0 HOLDs, the slowdown rule has not been binding.

4) Recovery support is moderate. It rests on small, acute samples (n=10-24), partly bench-only (Refalo 2023 SMO). The direction is consistent. Belcher 2019 shows squat velocity still down 8.6% at 72 h after sets to failure, which supports the benefit of non-failure work.

5) Population and timescale: nearly all trials are 5-12 weeks in mostly young men. The velocity-loss squat data (e.g. Pareja-Blanco 2017) used Smith machines. There is no multi-year, energy-deficit, high-bar or climbing context.

Process notes: the shared WebSearch budget was exhausted, so I could not search for 2025-26 counter-evidence. The PubMed page for Pareja-Blanco 2026 returned a CAPTCHA, so I relied on the verified evidence pack for it. I confirmed in docs/ref5-program-spec.md lines 465-473 that §11.1 bans intentional failure and extra reps, and that any ambiguous slowdown is not counted as a slowdown.

Recommendation: no protocol change, plus a pre-registration. This is observation-only, changes zero protocol elements, and fits all fixed principles (no failure, no RIR, no relaxing of judgment criteria). One suggested rewording: pre-register that a stall is not answered by grinding or extra reps, and is first answered by the §23.1 recovery and realized-volume audit. Do not frame load tracking as opposed to proximity.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: REF5 §11.1 ends a set before any rep that would lose ROM or technique. It counts the first obvious slowdown rep and then stops, treats an ambiguous slowdown as none, and forbids extra reps and intentional failure. REF5 sets and records no RPE or session-RPE target (§3.2, §11.1, §26.3), so "session RPE ~7–8" is not a REF5 rule.

For 1RM strength over 5–12 weeks, non-failure training is well supported: Grgic 2022, Robinson 2024 (load-adjusted RIR slope about 0), Jukic 2023, Ruple 2023 and Robinson 2025. Recovery at 24–48 h is faster after non-failure work (Moran-Navarro 2017). This matters because REF5 allows consecutive normal days, and only hard SQ is 48 h-gated.

At current loads the prescription, not the stop rule, sets effort. Assume the pre-program e1RM (about 98–105, from 92.5x3 at RPE 9–10) still holds. Then H3 85x3 leaves about 2–5 RIR and H2 90x2 about 1–4 RIR, with ±2–3 reps of uncertainty. If capacity has risen, sets are further from failure, which fits the zero main-lift HOLD/FAIL record. Once the PASS gate pushes load up to where slowdown appears, the stop rule will cap proximity near the first perceived slowdown, likely about 1–3 RIR.

At that proximity the stop rule's own hypertrophy cost is small. REF5's larger hypertrophy shortfall comes from the constraint-driven low volume and low-rep prescriptions. Over G1's multi-year horizon (about +70% squat while cutting), that shortfall may become a strength cost. No trial covers this; it is an extrapolation.

Once load is accounted for, the literature does not support "insufficient proximity" as the binding cause of past stalls. The athlete's own plateau record shows stall-era sets were already near failure (92.5x3 at RPE 9–10; the TB TM drifted up to capacity). The only contrary signal is weak: very-high-RIR work (6–8 or more RIR, as in TB's sub-80% weeks) may cost a little strength. That is mainly a load and intensity issue.

Within REF5 a stall already means HOLD/FAIL at the prescribed load. Pushing closer to failure would therefore require grinding, AMRAP or RIR, all of which are excluded. The evidence-favoured first response is the §23.1 order: recovery audit, then realized-volume audit. Load lagging capacity (Graham & Cleather 2021) is a risk for the current all-INCREASE catch-up phase, not a fix for a stall.

Recommendation, unchanged: no protocol change. Pre-register that a future stall will not be answered by taking sets closer to failure.

I checked the claim against /home/claude/workout-log/docs/ref5-program-spec.md (§3.2, §9, §11.1–11.3, §13, §19, §21.1, §22, §23.1, §26), the engine (packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts; REF5 sets are written with rpe: 0), the athlete's constraints sheet (/mnt/attach/project-files/uploads/hearth/df57dac8-a14b-4200-95d0-705cad8fe92e), the handoff, and /mnt/attach/project-files/notes/ref5-handoff-review-2026-10-14.md. The direction holds. Five points need revision.

What the claim gets right:
- §11.1 is described correctly: stop before a rep that would lose ROM or technique, count the first obvious slowdown rep and then end the set, no extra reps, no intentional failure.
- The strength evidence is real and confirmed in the pack: Grgic 2022, Robinson 2024 (load-adjusted), Jukic 2023, Ruple 2023, Robinson 2025 (including the ~8 RIR squat detail) and Pareja-Blanco 2017 (Smith machine).
- Moran-Navarro 2017 supports faster recovery at 24–48 h after non-failure work. This matters here because REF5 allows consecutive normal days: only hard SQ is 48 h-gated, and V and the upper-body slots are not.
- The recommendation is fine: no protocol change, one interpretive pre-registration, consistent with §21.1 and with the §23.1 order (recovery audit, then volume audit). It relaxes no judgment criterion and stays inside C5, C9, C10 and C11, and the no-AMRAP and no-intentional-failure rules.

Revisions:

(1) MISDESCRIBES REF5. There is no session-RPE ~7–8 target anywhere in the spec, the engine or the web code. The spec does the opposite. §3.2 (line 77) excludes AMRAP, RPE and RIR inputs. §11.1 (line 473) says no numeric velocity, RPE or RIR is entered. §26.3 (line 1048) treats any RPE meaning as a red flag. The only RPE figures in the athlete's files belong to the obsolete Hybrid v0.5 constraints sheet ("AMRAP only to RPE 9"), which the handoff review calls stale. Attaching "session RPE 7–8" to §11.1 should be removed. At most, label it as an outside estimate.

(2) RIR RANGE: THE LOWER BOUND IS TOO HIGH FOR H2. The pre-program e1RM comes from 92.5x3 at RPE 9–10. Epley and Brzycki put it at about 98–105. Current H3 85x3 is 81–87% of that, which leaves about 2–5 RIR. Current H2 90x2 is 86–92%, which leaves about 1–4 RIR (Epley at 98 gives about 1). The 2.5 lower bound only holds if capacity has risen. The zero main-lift HOLD record suggests it has, but squat slowdown may be under-perceived. So "2.5–5" should read "about 1.5–5, with H2 at the low end and ±2–3 reps of uncertainty". "Load, not the stop rule, sets effort" holds now, during catch-up. Once the PASS gate pushes load up to where slowdown starts (likely about 1–3 RIR), the stop rule will set the proximity ceiling.

(3) "COST FALLS ON HYPERTROPHY, NOT STRENGTH" HAS TWO PROBLEMS.
- The claim says load, not the stop rule, sets effort. If so, the stop rule's own hypertrophy cost is small. At the about 1–3 RIR the rule allows, Robinson 2024's slope of about -0.02 per RIR and Refalo 2024 (1–2 RIR about equal to failure) both imply little loss. REF5's larger hypertrophy shortfall comes from the constraint-driven low volume, low-rep prescriptions and no accessories.
- "Not strength" rests on 5–12-week trials. G1 needs about +70% squat strength while cutting to 70 kg. Over that horizon a hypertrophy shortfall may turn into a strength cost. No trial covers this, so it should be stated as an extrapolation limit.

(4) THE ATHLETE'S DIAGNOSIS. This part is strengthened, but "no evidence" is slightly overstated.
- Stronger: the athlete's own records cut against "insufficient proximity" as the cause of the stall. The plateau data point is 92.5x3 at RPE 9–10 (B1). The Tactical Barbell (TB) entry says the TM drifted up to real capacity: TM 102.5 while 92.5x3 was RPE 10. Stall-era sets were already near failure.
- Overstated: some weak evidence suggests sets very far from failure (6–8 or more RIR) cost a little strength. Robinson 2025's squat point estimate favoured 1–3 RIR by about 4 kg, and Pareja-Blanco 2026 found VL25 above VL0. TB Operator spent many weeks below 80% 1RM. With fixed reps, that is a load and intensity issue confounded with proximity. A better wording: "no evidence that going nearer than about 2–3 RIR adds strength; weak evidence that very high RIR costs a little."

(5) A STALL FIX THAT DOES NOT FIT REF5. "Keeping load in step with capacity matters more" uses Graham & Cleather 2021, which is about load lagging behind rising capacity. A REF5 stall is two maintain windows (§15). That requires HOLD or FAIL, meaning slowdown or rep deficit at the prescribed load, so load is already at capacity. "Pushing closer to failure" would then require grinding, AMRAP or RIR, all of which are excluded. That makes the pre-registration correct but nearly true by definition. The evidence-favoured first response to a stall is §23.1 steps 1–2. Load-lag belongs to the current all-INCREASE catch-up phase, not to stalls.

Confidence: the strength direction is high (multiple confirmed meta-analyses and RCTs). The RIR figures and the multi-year hypertrophy point are medium to low, and the revised claim labels them as inference.

## C3 · frequency · tradeoff · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 operational

Squatting every session, with hard H3/H2 limited to 2 starts per rolling 168 h at least 48 h apart, captures most of the frequency benefit the evidence supports. A 3rd or 4th weekly session adds only V or micro work, which volume-equated trials suggest costs little. The zero-tolerance 168 h boundary is a scheduling artefact: it cost 2 hard exposures, missed by 13.9 and 10.5 minutes, each about 1/6 of a squat window. The lenses DISAGREE on framing and remedy. A calls the cap a tradeoff: hard squat stays at 2 per week whatever the attendance, which limits progression cadence. A offers a small lookback tolerance as the single protocol candidate. B calls the cap a strength (cheap for strength), judges a boundary change unnecessary, and keeps the remedy display-only.

**근거**

- Pelland et al. 2026, Sports Med 56(2):481-505: volume-adjusted frequency effect on strength with diminishing returns; preprint values 12.7% at 1 vs 17.3% at 2 sessions/wk. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-025-02344-w
- Tao et al. 2026, Int J Sports Sci Coach 21(4):2628-2648 (47 studies): small independent frequency advantage for strength (|g|=0.17), no muscle-mass effect. https://ro.ecu.edu.au/ecuworks2022-2026/8044
- Volume-equated trials in trained lifters showed no strength difference: Colquhoun 2018 (3 vs 6/wk, squat p=0.845) https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29324578/ ; Saric 2019 (3 vs 6) https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30363041/ ; Johnsen & van den Tillaar 2021 (2 vs 4/wk, squat +13.25 vs +14.31 kg, p=0.64) https://peerj.com/articles/10781/ ; Hamarsland 2022 (2 vs 4).
- Grgic et al. 2018, Sports Med 48(5):1207-1220: no frequency effect when volume was equated (p=0.421); lower-body effect not significant (p=0.070). https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29470825/
- Raastad & Hallen 2000, Eur J Appl Physiol 82(3):206-214: in strength athletes, all variables were back to baseline by 33 h after 3RM-load squats. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s004210050673 . Belcher et al. 2019 (APNM 44(10):1033-1042): squat velocity still -8.6% at 72 h after sets to failure, so 48 h is adequate only while hard sets stay short of failure.
- Internal record: 18 hard and 7 V normal sessions. Five V sessions came from the 48 h gate (gaps 44, 24, 24, 23, 44 h); two came from the 168 h count, missed by 13.9 min (8/29) and 10.5 min (9/12). Plan B hard rate 1.58/wk.
- Spec §9: boundaries are exact with no tolerance; the UI may display the remaining time.

**추론**

Direct evidence: volume-equated frequency trials in trained lifters (6-9 weeks) and recovery time-course studies support at least 2 exposures per week, with little gained from more when volume is matched. The benefit runs mostly through volume. Extrapolation: those studies use regular calendars, not REF5's irregular clusters. The 168 h cap itself is unvalidated. Losing exposures by minutes has no physiological rationale, and both lenses agree on that. 'Squat first' shows up in session order, not in extra hard dose. The disagreement is about whether limiting cadence counts as a cost (A) or whether, for strength, it is effectively free (B).

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: REF5 squats in every session, so squat frequency is ≥2/wk whenever attendance is. That captures most of the frequency benefit that meta-analyses support: Pelland (preprint values 12.7% → 17.3% from 1 to 2 sessions/wk), Tao (|g|=0.17) and ACSM (≥2 sessions/wk).

The hard cap (2 starts per rolling 168 h, at least 48 h apart) does not lower frequency. It sets how many exposures are heavy (82-87% of estimated 1RM) and how many are V (~70%). Volume-equated frequency trials kept the load mix the same in both arms, so they do not test this. The view that capping heavy squat at about 2/wk costs little rests on extrapolation: diminishing returns beyond ~4 heavy fractional sets/wk (Pelland preprint; Schoenfeld 2019; Androulakis-Korakakis 2021 expert consensus). Load evidence leans weakly the other way (Currier 2023 >80% 1RM; Graham & Cleather 2021), so the cost is "probably small, unproven".

Recovery data do not justify the exact boundaries. Raastad 2000 found knee-extensor and jump measures back to baseline by 33 h after a full 3RM-load squat session. The 168 h count blocked two hard starts by 13.9 min (8/29) and 10.5 min (9/12), and replay shows each would have been one extra hard exposure. In practice the 8/29 miss changed no decision, because the v1.3 plan was replaced on 9/05 with its second window incomplete. The 9/12 miss delayed v1.4's first SQ window by about 5 days (9/24 → 9/29). The 48 h gate also had 44 h near-misses, but these cost nothing net.

Operational fix: add an "eligible at" time for the 168 h condition to the preview, which already shows the 48 h countdown and the 168 h count. If a protocol change is wanted later, the single candidate is a small lookback tolerance (e.g. ≥166 h).

I checked the internal record directly with a read-only SELECT on generated_session snapshots in prod (the user's plans 3f8e035e on v1.3 and 1688b592 on v1.4; plan c808dde3 belongs to a role='test' account and was excluded). I also read engine ref5.ts:1198-1203, spec §9 and §26.2, and the preview UI. The web-search budget for this turn was used up, so the literature checks rest on the verified evidence pack plus one Springer fetch.

What checks out
- The record matches. The 168 h misses were 13.87 min on 8/29 (window edge 8/22 09:14:35 vs hard start 09:28:27) and 10.52 min on 9/12 (edge 9/05 09:02:54 vs 09:13:25). There were 18 hard and 7 V normal sessions, counting the new 10/14 H3. The five 48 h-gate V sessions came after gaps of 44.1, 24.2, 24.2, 23.0 and 44.4 h.
- Replaying the gate shows each 168 h miss would have added exactly one hard exposure. Later sessions stay hard under both counterfactuals.
- Spec §9 matches the claim: the boundaries are exact, a tolerance counts as a protocol change (§26.2), and the UI may show remaining time.
- The cited trials and meta-analyses match the evidence pack: Colquhoun p=0.845; Johnsen +13.25 vs +14.31 kg, p=0.64; Saric; Hamarsland (moderately trained, small unequal groups); Pelland numbers correctly labelled as preprint values; Tao |g|=0.17; Grgic p=0.421; Belcher -8.61% at 72 h.

Where the claim overstates
1. The frequency evidence answers a different question. REF5 squats every session, so the hard cap never lowers squat frequency. Pelland, Tao and ACSM support "≥2 squat exposures/wk captures most of the frequency benefit", and that is true whatever the cap does. What the cap actually decides is whether the 3rd and 4th exposures are heavy (82-87%) or about 70%.
   - The volume-equated trials kept the load mix the same in both arms, so they cannot show that swapping heavy work for V "costs little".
   - The load evidence leans weakly the other way. Currier 2023 found loads above 80% 1RM maximised strength and ranked HM3 top (that rank is not robust), ACSM recommends ≥80%, and Graham & Cleather tie larger gains to higher average intensity.
   - "Cheap" is better supported by dose-response diminishing returns for extra heavy sets: Pelland (preprint, ~4 fractional sets), Schoenfeld 2019, and Androulakis-Korakakis 2021 (expert consensus). That makes it an extrapolation, not direct trial evidence.
2. The trials (n=21-28, 6-9 weeks) are underpowered for the small effect, g≈0.17-0.2, that Pelland and Tao report. "No difference" in them means "no large difference", not "no cost".
3. Grgic's lower-body p=0.070 comes from all studies, not only the volume-equated ones. The claim's wording runs the two results together.
4. Raastad & Hallén 2000 measured knee-extensor contractility and squat jump, not squat performance. Its protocol was a 90-min session of 3RM-load squats, front squats and 6RM knee extensions, a bigger dose than H3, and everything had recovered by 33 h. That supports a 48 h gap more strongly than the claim's caveat ("only while short of failure") allows. Belcher's slower recovery followed 4 higher-rep sets to failure at 80%, which is a different stimulus.
5. The "cost" is real but smaller in practice than "2 exposures, each 1/6 of a window" suggests.
   - The 8/29 miss happened in the v1.3 plan, which was replaced on 9/05 with its second SQ window only 3 of 6 complete. It changed no progression decision.
   - Only the 9/12 miss mattered: v1.4's first SQ window would have closed on 9/24 instead of 9/29, about 5 days earlier.
   - The 48 h gate has similar near-misses (44.1 and 44.4 h) that the same physiological argument would cover. The 9/23 one cost nothing net, because 9/24 became hard instead.
6. Part of the recommendation already exists. The preview panel (ref5-session-start-panel.tsx) already shows a 48 h countdown and the 168 h count ("N so far · limit <2"). It does not show when the oldest hard start leaves the 168 h window, so that "eligible at" time is the only missing piece.

Recommendation check
- The operational fix changes no protocol.
- The tolerance option (≥166 h) is a single change to the density gate, already flagged as a protocol change under §26.2. It leaves FAIL 0 / HOLD≤1 / veto, the lift set, the no-RIR/AMRAP/1RM rules and deloads untouched.
- It therefore stays within the fixed constraints.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: REF5 squats in every session. Hard H3/H2 work is gated at 48 h or more since the last hard start and fewer than 2 hard starts in the open 168 h window. This keeps total squat exposures at about 2 or more per week, where frequency meta-regressions put most of the strength benefit (Pelland 2025/26; Tao 2026). Extra sessions within any 168 h replace a heavy exposure (80-87% 1RM) with V at about 72%. That costs heavy-set dose, not frequency at matched volume. Heavy-dose evidence (Enes 2024: 6 ≈ 9 weekly heavy sets; Pelland: diminishing returns) suggests the cost is small but not zero. The 168 h cap itself is unvalidated.

In the record through 10/06 (28 sessions: 17 hard, 7 V-normal, 4 micro; an 18th hard came on 10/14), the 48 h gate blocked 5 V sessions. Four of those were also over the 168 h count. The 168 h cap was the sole binding constraint only on 8/29 and 9/12, missed by 13.9 and 10.5 min. Both were Saturday sessions that started slightly earlier than the previous Saturday's hard start. So the cap's entire realized cost is 2 hard exposures (each 1/6 of a SQ window), and both come from a weekly routine meeting an exactly-one-week boundary.

Operational remedy: the start panel already shows the 48 h countdown but only a count for the 168 h rule. Add a 'time until a 168 h slot frees' display; the API must expose the oldest in-window start. Record the true first-work-set time, since the start field defaults to panel-open time and does not refresh, rather than editing it to pass the gate. Optional single protocol candidate (§26-style, density gate only, judgment criteria untouched): treat hard starts 166 h or more old as outside the window. In this record that would have recovered the cap's whole cost. Whether to adopt it is a tradeoff (A) or unnecessary (B).

I checked the claim against three things: spec §8.1/§9 in /home/claude/workout-log/docs/ref5-program-spec.md, the engine (packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts lines 1189-1206), and the live DB. The DB check was a read-only SELECT on the generated_session decision snapshots for the user's plans 3f8e035e (old plan) and 1688b592 (v1.4 plan).

What checks out:
(1) The rules are described correctly. Hard is allowed only on NORMAL sessions, needs at least 48 h since the last hard start, and needs fewer than 2 hard starts in the open 168 h window. The boundaries are exact, and §9 says adding a tolerance is a protocol change. It also lets the UI display the remaining time.
(2) Both minute-level misses are exact. On 8/29 the session started at 09:14:35Z and the 8/22 09:28:27Z start was still inside the window, a miss of 13.87 min. On 9/12 the session started at 09:02:54Z against the 9/05 09:13:25Z start, a miss of 10.52 min. Both sessions had more than 48 h since the last hard start. Neither would have changed later decisions (9/01 and 9/15 stay hard), so the net cost is +2 hard exposures, each 1/6 of the 6-exposure SQ window.
(3) The 48 h gaps are 44.11, 24.16, 24.19, 23.00 and 44.39 h, as claimed.

What needs correcting:
(a) Counts. Through 10/06 the record has 28 sessions: 17 hard, 7 V-normal and 4 micro. The claim's figure of 18 hard includes a new session on 10/14 at 13:01Z (NORMAL, BP focus, H3, n168=0). That session falls outside the 28-session record and ends the '8-day gap'. 1.58/wk equals 9 hard starts from 9/05 to 10/15. The figure is 1.80/wk through 10/06 only.
(b) 'Five V from the 48 h gate' leaves something out. Four of those five (7/26, 8/06, 8/25, 9/08) also had n168=2. Only 9/23 was blocked by the 48 h gate alone. So the 168 h cap was over its count in 6 of the 7 V sessions, but it was the sole binding constraint only on 8/29 and 9/12. The whole realized cost of the cap, not just of its zero tolerance, is therefore those 2 near-miss exposures. That supports B's 'cheap' reading more than A's 'limits cadence' reading. The realized cadence is set mainly by attendance clustering plus the 48 h gate.
(c) Mechanism. Both misses were Saturday sessions that started 10-14 min earlier than the previous Saturday's hard start. Because 168 h is exactly one week, a weekly routine sits on the boundary by construction. This supports calling it an artefact, and a ~2 h tolerance absorbs that jitter.
(d) The evidence partly mismatches the claim. The cap does not redistribute matched volume. It swaps the 3rd exposure's load from about 80-87% to about 72% of 1RM, while V sets still add volume. So volume-equated frequency RCTs (Colquhoun, Saric, Johnsen, Hamarsland) are only indirect support. Closer support is heavy-dose dose-response: Enes 2024 IJSM found 6 = 9 weekly sets, and Pelland finds diminishing returns. ACSM 2026 and Currier 2023 link loads of 80% or more to strength, so the cost is small but not zero. The 168 h cap is unvalidated, as the claim says.
(e) Recommendation context. The UI (web/src/features/workout-log/ui/ref5-session-start-panel.tsx) already shows a hard-gate block with a 48 h countdown ('remaining to go'). For the 168 h rule it shows only the count ('N so far'). No time-until-slot-frees is shown, and the API returns only lastStartAt and the count, not the oldest in-window start. Also, 'Actual start time' is a user-editable datetime field. It defaults to when the panel opened and is not refreshed when Start is pressed. Waiting alone therefore does not change the decision: the user has to record the true first-work-set time, as the spec defines it. That same default timestamp adds minutes of measurement noise, about the size of the misses.

Constraint check. The operational remedy stays inside all fixed principles. The optional single protocol candidate (treat hard starts 166 h or more old as outside the window) changes one element, the density gate. It leaves FAIL 0, HOLD ≤1 and the volume veto untouched. It is correctly classed as a protocol change under spec §9. Had it been in place, it would have recovered the entire realized cost of the cap in this record. So recommendationOk is true. The display recommendation should be narrowed to the 168 h countdown that is actually missing, and should warn against editing the timestamp to pass the gate.

## C4 · volume · tradeoff · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 observation-only

The realized weekly dose meets the evidence-informed minimum effective dose for strength and sits near the point where extra sets stop producing detectable short-term gains. Plan B (9/05-10/14) averaged 2.45 sessions/wk with SQ 7.0 sets (4.7 hard), BP 5.6 (3.15 focus) and PULL 3.5 (3.15 focus). So volume is not the near-term limiter for squat. The dose is below the spec's own §19.3 table for 3 sessions/wk (SQ 9, BP 7-8, PULL 4-5), and BP focus and PULL, at about 3 heavy sets/wk, sit at the low edge. The lenses DISAGREE on the label: A calls it a tradeoff (whether ~3 sets/wk is enough over years is contested); B calls it a strength (adequate near term).

**근거**

- Pelland et al. 2026, Sports Med 56(2):481-505, with SportRxiv preprint values: strength rose ~0.21% per fractional set, and increments stayed above the 3.96% smallest detectable effect only up to ~4 fractional sets/wk. Returns diminish but do not reach zero. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-025-02344-w ; https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/view/460
- ACSM 2026: the strength dose-response plateaus beyond about 2-3 sets per exercise. https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000003897
- Androulakis-Korakakis et al. 2021, Front Sports Act Living 3:713655: powerlifter minimum effective dose ~3-6 sets/wk of 1-5 reps at >80% 1RM, RPE 7.5-9.5. Mainly expert consensus plus small trials. https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/sports-and-active-living/articles/10.3389/fspor.2021.713655/full
- Enes et al. 2024, Int J Sports Med 45(9):690-697: in strength-trained athletes, 6 and 9 weekly sets beat 3 for squat and bench; 9 was no better than 6. https://doi.org/10.1055/a-2312-5103
- Schoenfeld et al. 2019, MSSE 51(1):94-103: 1, 3 and 5 sets per exercise gave similar squat and bench 1RM gains in trained men. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30153194/
- Ralston et al. 2017, Sports Med 47(12):2585-2601: high vs low weekly sets ES 0.18; the 1RM-specific comparison was not significant (p=0.06). https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-017-0762-7
- Internal record: Plan B weekly totals ranged from 0 to 34 sets.

**추론**

Direct evidence: 2-3 sets per lift per session, and strength being relatively insensitive to volume beyond about 4 weekly sets in short trials. Enes 2024 points to a mild under-dose for BP focus and PULL at ~3 sets; Schoenfeld 2019 contradicts that. No trial covers weighted pull-ups. Extrapolation: the dose-response models are fitted to ~10-week trials, many failure-based or in less-trained people, so they may understate what an intermediate after a 1-year plateau needs over years. Every window so far ended INCREASE, so there is no internal signal for change.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Squat: the realized dose of about 7 sets/wk (about 4.7 at ≥80% of e1RM) meets the minimum effective dose (MED) for strength on set count and load. It sits at or past the point where, in short trials (mostly failure-based, about 10 weeks), extra weekly sets stop adding detectable strength (Pelland preprint: 5 or more fractional sets). Volume is therefore unlikely to be the near-term limiter for the squat. This rests on models; the all-INCREASE record cannot show it, because starting loads were conservative. Whether REF5's slowdown-terminated sets meet the MED's RPE 7.5-9.5 condition is unverified.

BP focus and PULL: heavy sets run at about 3.2-3.5 per week, which is at the MED floor. That falls in Pelland's intermediate tier, where 2-3 more sets would still be expected to add detectable short-term gains. Enes 2024 and Ralston 2017 (medium vs low volume) point to a mild under-dose. Schoenfeld 2019 is a weak counter, because its groups also did accessory work to failure and the trial was underpowered. No trial covers weighted pull-ups, and weighted PULL is trained only about 1.4 times per week.

Realized volume is roughly on-design for the realized 2.45 sessions/wk (interpolating §19.3). The gap against the 3x/wk row comes from session frequency, not from micro overuse. Label: tradeoff, not strength.

VERDICT: the squat part holds. The headline overstates the case for BP-focus and PULL, and the evidence weighting leans toward the "strength" label more than the evidence allows. "Tradeoff" is the defensible label.

1) Pelland numbers check out, but they say less than the claim implies. The SportRxiv preprint full text (https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/download/460/967/908) contains the 0.21%/set slope, but it is a linear summary slope "at the mean value of fractional volume" (study mean was about 8 sets/wk). It is not a per-set rate across the whole range, because the best-fit model is reciprocal. The tier table (2B) matters more:
- MED 1 set.
- 2 sets: higher efficiency.
- 3-4 sets: intermediate, where about 2.25 extra sets still give a detectable gain above the 3.96% SDES.
- 5+ sets: extra sets "do not consistently enhance strength gains > SDES".

So SQ (7.0 total sets, 4.7 hard) and total BP (5.6, about 6.6 fractional with OHP) are at or past the knee. Heavy BP focus (3.15) and PULL (3.5) sit in the intermediate tier, where the model still predicts detectable gains from 2-3 more sets. "Near the point where extra sets stop producing detectable gains" is therefore true for SQ and wrong for PULL. Pelland also cites, without re-analysing, that well-trained powerlifters found 1-3 direct sets/wk insufficient. The published Sports Med version could not be checked against these preprint numbers.

2) "Meets the MED" is conditional. The Androulakis-Korakakis 2021 MED of 3-6 sets/wk at >80% 1RM also requires RPE 7.5-9.5. It rests on expert interviews and surveys plus two quasi-randomized pilots (n=25). REF5 stops sets at the first slowdown. H3 at about 82% of an old e1RM has an estimated 2-5 RIR (Nuzzo 2024 plus the athlete's own 92.5x3), and V and BP-volume sets are at about 70%. So on load and set count:
- SQ meets the MED (4.7 sets at ≥80%).
- BP focus and PULL sit exactly at the 3-set floor.
- The effort condition is unverified for all three.

Robinson 2024 (no load-adjusted RIR effect on strength) softens this point but does not remove it. Pelland's 'MED' is only 1 set, so meeting it is a trivial bar.

3) The contrary evidence is weighted asymmetrically.
- Enes 2024 IJSM: trained athletes, 3 < 6 weekly sets for both SQ and BP, and 6 = 9; n=8 per group, so small. This is the most direct trial, and it says about 3 heavy sets is mildly under-dosed.
- Ralston 2017: the claim cites only the non-significant 1RM-specific p=0.06. It omits that medium vs low was significant (ES 0.15, p=0.04). Ralston's low tier is ≤5 sets/exercise/wk, which is where BP focus and PULL fall.
- Schoenfeld 2019 is a weaker counter than presented. The abstract (PubMed) confirms n=11/12/11 and no between-group 1RM differences, so the null is underpowered. From my recollection of the full text (not re-fetched), every group also did leg press, leg extension, military press, pulldown and row, all to failure at 8-12RM. The '1-set' group's quads and chest therefore got far more than 3 weekly sets, which cannot be mapped onto a program with no accessories.
- Enes 2024 MSSE: trained men's squat 1RM kept rising with added lower-limb volume. It is confounded and at very high volume, but the claim omits it.
- ACSM's '2-3 sets/exercise' is per session, comes from mostly untrained samples, and comes alongside ≥2 sessions/wk. Weighted PULL is trained about 1.4x/wk (focus about 1.05 plus micro) and BP focus about 1.05x/wk.

On balance, the evidence supports a mild under-dose for heavy BP and PULL. It does not support calling them adequate.

4) The internal record supports "no change signal" but not "dose adequate". The all-INCREASE run began from loads about 80% of e1RM. A catch-up phase cannot be told apart from an adequate stimulus, so the record is not evidence that volume suffices.

5) Arithmetic. The figures reproduce exactly with 14 sessions over 40 days (5.71 wk): 12 normal sessions (6 PULL-focus, 6 BP-focus) plus 2 micro.
- SQ 40 sets → 7.0/wk; hard 27 → 4.7/wk.
- BP 32 → 5.6/wk; BP focus 18 → 3.15/wk.
- PULL 20 → 3.5/wk.

The fact sheet, however, says 13 v1.4 sessions. With 13 sessions the rate is 2.28/wk and SQ is ≤39 sets (≤6.8/wk). This is minor (possibly a calibration session was counted) and does not change the conclusion.

More important: comparing against the §19.3 3x/wk row is misleading. Interpolating the table at the realized 2.45/wk gives SQ about 7.35, BP about 6, PULL about 3.5, so realized volume is roughly on-design for the frequency actually achieved. The shortfall comes from frequency (including the 8-day gap), not from micro overuse. That is relevant to §23.1 step 2.

RECOMMENDATION: within constraints. It is observation-only, keeps the pre-registered order (recovery, then realized-volume audit, then a +1 set §26 candidate), changes one element, and relaxes no judgment criterion.

Note: web search budget was exhausted mid-check, the Enes 2024 DOI page failed to load, and PubMed showed only the abstract. Enes details rely on the evidence pack's Thieme verification.

수정 주장: Plan B covers 9/05-10/14: 14 sessions, including the 10/14 22:01 KST session, or 2.45/wk. That rate includes the 10/07-10/13 gap; over 9/05-10/06 it is 2.84/wk.

Realized weekly sets:
- SQ 7.0, of which 4.7 were H3/H2
- BP 5.6, of which 3.15 were focus
- PULL 3.5, of which 3.15 were focus
- DL, OHP and OAP pairs about 2.1 each

This is essentially the §19.3 design dose interpolated to 2.45 sessions/wk. Being below the 3x/wk row reflects frequency, not a §23.1 step-2 volume shortfall.

The heavy-set count and approximate load meet the 3-6 sets/wk >80% minimum dose that is mainly expert consensus (Androulakis-Korakakis 2021). Its RPE 7.5-9.5 component is probably not met, because REF5 sets end at the first obvious slowdown at about 80-87% of an old e1RM. Robinson 2024 (strength insensitive to proximity when load is controlled) narrows but does not close that gap. Schoenfeld 2019's 3-set group, which matched higher volumes, trained to failure, so it transfers only weakly.

BP-focus and PULL heavy sets, at about 3/wk, sit at the low edge, and whether that is enough over years is contested. That makes it a tradeoff, not a deficit.
