- true PULL total load (each kg of bodyweight error is a kg of PULL-total error, up to ~6.7% for a 5 kg gap);
- the §23.1 weight-loss-rate audit;
- whether the planned 0.25%/wk cut is happening.
Sleep, protein, session RPE, climbing and pain are not stored either, so the pre-registered first step of §23.1 (the recovery audit) cannot currently be run. Both lenses rank this as the highest-leverage fix, with no cost to the protocol.

**근거**

- Internal DB, read-only check: logged bodyweight 75.00 in 28/29 REF5 sessions (76 on 10/14); 0 body_measurement rows for the user.
- Internal computation: logged PULL total = logged bodyweight + added load, so bodyweight error feeds the PULL REF and every PULL prescription.
- Spec §10.1, §20, §21 (REF ÷ 7-day bodyweight trend) and §23.1 step 1 (sleep, days under 1.6 g/kg protein, weight-loss rate over 0.25%/wk).
- Garthe et al. 2011, IJSNEM 21(2):97-104: the rate of weight loss mattered for lean mass (0.7%/wk: +2.1% LBM; the faster arm did not gain), so the rate has to be measured to be managed. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21558571/
- Murphy & Koehler 2022: lean-mass loss depends on the size of the deficit; a deficit that cannot be observed cannot be audited. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34623696/

**추론**

This is a data-quality fact and does not depend on the literature. The structural consequence is that the spec's audit-first logic, which is what protects the fixed judgment criteria, depends on data that are not being collected. The spec already uses today's bodyweight, so the fix is purely operational.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Bodyweight is effectively unmeasured. I verified that 28 of 29 REF5 session starts log 75 kg (76 on 10/14) and that the user has 0 body_measurement rows. The start panel pre-fills the settings value, which explains the constant 75. Notes and tags are empty, and all 278 sets have a placeholder RPE of 0.0. Sleep, protein, stress, climbing and pain are not recorded.

As a result, none of the following can be computed validly:
- the G1 KPI proxy, control REF ÷ 7-day bodyweight (§21);
- the true PULL total (about 5.5% error per 5 kg on a roughly 90 kg total);
- whether §20 is being followed, i.e. bodyweight held through the first gate, then a 0.25%/wk cut if one is chosen;
- the §23.1 step-1 recovery audit.

PASS/HOLD/FAIL judgments themselves stay valid. With a constant logged bodyweight, the engine effectively progresses added load, so it is the meaning of the PULL REF that is distorted, not the judgments.

Fixing this is the cheapest way to make the pre-registered audit-first logic runnable. The literature only supplies context for it: Garthe 2011 shows achieved weight-loss rates vary widely around the plan, and Murphy & Koehler 2022 shows deficits blunt lean-mass gain but not strength.

Operational fix:
- weigh on waking under the same conditions, ideally daily (at least 3 times a week);
- overwrite the pre-filled value with that weight at every session start, so §10.1's existing 7-day averaging applies;
- log the same value in body_measurement;
- use the 7-day mean only for the KPI and the §23.1 audit.

Day-to-day noise is about 0.5% of bodyweight (Khosla & Billewicz 1964), against a target of about 0.19 kg/wk. Over the 4-week audit window, near-daily weighing is therefore needed to judge the 0.25%/wk threshold. Feeding body_measurement into the PULL calculation would be a protocol change under §20 and would need a §26 proposal.

I re-checked the data myself with read-only SELECTs and found no conflict with the claim.
- generated_session for the 29-session user (2026-07-19 to 10-14) has todayBodyweightKg = 75 in 28 sessions and 76 on 10/14.
- body_measurement has 0 rows for this user. It holds 12 rows for other users, so the feature works and is simply not used.
- 29 workout_logs have empty notes and tags. All 278 sets carry rpe = 0.0, which is a placeholder rather than an RPE rating.

The code explains the 75. ref5-session-start-panel.tsx pre-fills "today's bodyweight" from the settings value (defaultBodyweightKg), so a stale 75 carries forward unless it is overwritten. The core point therefore holds: the §23.1 recovery audit and the §21 REF÷7-day-bodyweight metric depend on inputs that are not being collected.

The claim overstates what the evidence supports in five places.

(1) The literature is context, not proof.
- Garthe 2011 is confirmed, but it compares 0.7 vs 1.0-1.4%/wk, three to five times the user's rate. Its slow arm achieved 0.7 ± 0.8%/wk. That large spread around the planned rate is the real support for measuring.
- Murphy & Koehler 2022 is misstated. It shows that a deficit blunts lean-mass gain (about 500 kcal/day stopped gains), not lean-mass loss. It also found strength gains not impaired (ES -0.31, p=0.28). For the athlete's main strength goal, the stake is the KPI's denominator and the PULL calibration, not protection of strength itself. The "highest-leverage" ranking is a judgement, not a finding.

(2) The PULL arithmetic is framed loosely.
- 5 kg is 6.7% of bodyweight but about 5.5% of the roughly 90 kg PULL total.
- Under §10.3 the added load is locked for each window, so bodyweight affects prescriptions only at each relock.
- With a constant logged bodyweight, the engine in effect runs an added-load progression. PASS/HOLD/FAIL calls stay valid on performance. What is wrong is the absolute meaning of the PULL REF and its trend during a cut.

(3) "Cannot be computed" should read "cannot be computed validly". The G1 KPI is the §21 proxy REF÷bodyweight, and it can be calculated, but its denominator is unverified.

(4) The claim misreads §20.
- §20 schedules the 0.25%/wk cut only after the first evaluation gate, and only if needed. Before that it requires the 7-day mean to stay roughly constant.
- So the issue is not only whether the cut is happening. Neither maintenance nor the cut can be verified.

(5) Detecting the rate needs more weighings than the claim implies.
- Khosla & Billewicz 1964 (Br J Nutr 18:227, read from the Cambridge PDF) found a day-to-day SD of about 0.5% of bodyweight (mean CV 0.54%), roughly 0.4 kg at 75 kg.
- The 0.25%/wk target is about 0.19 kg/wk, less than half of one day's noise.
- My own calculation, assuming independent daily errors, is that over the 4-week §23.1 window:
  - daily weighing gives a slope SE of about 0.08%/wk;
  - 3 weighings/wk give about 0.12-0.13%/wk, a 95% interval of about ±0.25%/wk, which cannot tell 0.25 apart from 0 or 0.5.
- A weekly weekend/weekday rhythm (Orsama et al. 2014, Obes Facts, https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24504358/) adds systematic error when weigh days are irregular.
- So daily weighing should be the default for auditing the rate. Three a week is a floor for the KPI and PULL only.

The recommendation is also partly mislabelled as "no protocol change". The engine's calculation bodyweight (§10.1, readRecentBodyweight in ref5-integration.ts) reads only frozen session-start inputs. It averages them only when there are at least 3 in the last 7×24 h, and never reads body_measurement. §20 states that changing the PULL total-load calculation rule is a protocol change. Using the body_measurement 7-day mean for PULL is therefore one protocol-element change and would need a §26 proposal.

The fully operational version is:
- weigh on waking under the same conditions;
- overwrite the pre-filled value with that real number at every session start, so §10.1's own averaging applies;
- log the same value in body_measurement;
- use the 7-day mean only for the KPI and the §23.1 audit.

The suggestion to log sleep, protein and stress or pain through notes or a separate log is operational. It is consistent with C10, because session RPE and RIR are different things, and the spec already targets a session RPE of about 7-8.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: Bodyweight is not measured per session. All 29 REF5 sessions (7/19 to 10/14) carry 75.00 kg as the start-input bodyweight, except 76 on 10/14. That 75 is the user's settings value from 2026-07-04, carried forward and possibly reconfirmed through the stale-weight banner on 9/05. The user has 0 body_measurement rows. As a result: the §21 proxy (control REF ÷ 7-day bodyweight) is flat by construction; true PULL totals and the added-load prescriptions carry an unknown error (5 kg is about 6% of the total and about 33% of the +15 kg added load); neither the maintenance phase nor any later 0.25%/wk cut can be verified; and the weight-loss-rate and protein g/kg legs of §23.1 step 1 cannot be computed. Separately, sleep, protein intake, session RPE (the per-set field is a 0.0 placeholder), climbing and pain are not recorded anywhere, and workout notes are empty. Because §23.1 looks back 4 weeks, the recovery audit cannot be run when a structureReview eventually fires unless collection starts now. The G1 KPI (e1RM ÷ bodyweight) stays unavailable even with real bodyweight, because REF5 produces no e1RM (§6.3, §20). Recommendation, operational and with no protocol change: enter the real same-day bodyweight as the existing REF5 start input at every session, and the engine's existing §10.1 rule handles PULL averaging. Optionally also weigh on waking 3 to 7 days a week into body measurements for the trend; this does not feed REF5. Keep an off-protocol daily note of sleep hours and estimated protein. Do not change how PULL calculates bodyweight; that would be a §26 protocol change.

The data facts check out against the production DB, using SELECT-only queries on generated_session, body_measurement, user_setting, workout_log and workout_set. There are 29 REF5 sessions: 15 on the v1.3 plan (7/19 to 9/01) and 14 on the v1.4 plan (9/05 to 10/14). Every startInput.todayBodyweightKg is 75 except 76 on 10/14. calculationBodyweightKg follows the same values. The user has 0 body_measurement rows. All 29 logs have empty notes and tags. The per-set rpe field holds a 0.0 placeholder on all 278 sets, so no session RPE, sleep, protein, climbing or pain data exists anywhere. The 10/14 session is real, so the task brief's "28 sessions" is out of date and the claim's 29 is correct.

Revisions needed:

(1) The 75 is not a code default. The code default is null or 70. The 75 is the user's own prefs.bodyweight.kg, set on 2026-07-04. prefs.bodyweight.checkedAtMs was refreshed on 2026-09-05 at 09:12 UTC, just before the first v1.4 session, which means the stale-weight banner was answered with either "update" or "keep". The REF5 start input takes this value. So "effectively unmeasured" is too strong. The accurate wording is: entered once on 7/04 and carried into every session start, possibly reconfirmed on 9/05, with one fresh entry (76) on 10/14. The practical conclusion is the same: there is no per-session measurement and no trend can be computed.

(2) The G1 KPI is e1RM ÷ bodyweight. REF5 has no e1RM by design: §6.3 says REF is not 1RM, e1RM or TM, and §20 says REF÷BW cannot confirm 175. So real bodyweight is necessary for the KPI but not sufficient. Real bodyweight does make the §21 proxy (control REF ÷ 7-day bodyweight trend) meaningful.

(3) The "6.7%" figure is 5/75, i.e. relative to bodyweight. As a share of the PULL total (about 85 to 90 kg) a 5 kg error is about 5.6 to 5.9%. Against the +15 kg added load it is about 33%. Strictly, the PULL REF is the target total. What the bodyweight error corrupts is the added-load prescription (target minus calculated bodyweight, §10.2) and how true the actual lifted total is.

(4) Main applicability defect, in the recommendation. "Use the 7-day mean ... for PULL total load", labelled "no protocol change", conflicts with the spec. §10.1 already defines the calculated bodyweight. It averages only frozen session-start bodyweights within 7×24 h, and only when there are at least 3. Otherwise it uses today's value, and it explicitly forbids averaging two values. §10.2 defines the actual total as today's bodyweight plus the locked added load. §20 and §26.2 classify any change to PULL averaging or the total-load rule as a protocol change. The engine (ref5-integration.ts readRecentBodyweight) reads only generated_session startInput values, so morning weigh-ins stored in body_measurement never reach REF5. That clause is therefore either redundant or a protocol change hidden under an "operational" label.

(5) Bodyweight restores only one leg of the §23.1 step-1 recovery audit: the weight-loss rate, plus the denominator for protein in g/kg. Sleep and protein intake still go unrecorded. Adding them as REF5 inputs would itself be a §26.2 change ("items the user inputs"). Keeping them in an off-protocol log, such as the currently empty workout_log notes, is not.

(6) The case for starting now is actually stronger than stated. §23.1 looks back 4 weeks, so the data has to be collected before a structureReview fires, and none has fired yet because every window ended INCREASE. Also, under §20 the 0.25%/wk cut is meant to start only after the first evaluation gate, if needed. The bodyweight series is therefore needed now to confirm the maintenance phase, not to track a cut that has not begun.

(7) An operational side effect to expect: once real values are entered, the calculated bodyweight changes the PULL added load at the next relock, or at once if the direct target changes (§10.3). PULL totals logged before and after the correction are not comparable.

The literature (Garthe 2011, Murphy & Koehler 2022) is cited accurately but only supports the case indirectly. The case is mainly about data quality, as the claim itself says. The statement that both lenses rank this highest cannot be checked here.

## C12 · recovery/deficit · strength · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 operational

A ~0.25%/wk cut (~200 kcal/d at 75 kg) is compatible with continued strength gains, and §23.1's 'recovery audit before stimulus audit' order is well founded. The profile's protein start range (1.6-2.2 g/kg, spec line 792) and the §23.1 audit criterion (days under 1.6 g/kg, line 904) are mostly energy-balance figures. Data specific to a deficit point to the upper part of the range, expressed per kg of fat-free mass. Sleep and stress evidence favour patterns over several nights or weeks rather than single nights. Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Murphy & Koehler 2022: strength gains were not significantly impaired in a deficit (ES -0.31, p=0.28); lean-mass gains were (ES -0.57), with ~500 kcal/d preventing them. https://doi.org/10.1111/sms.14075
- Garthe et al. 2011: elite athletes losing ~0.7%/wk gained 2.1% lean mass; the faster arm did not. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/21558571/
- Morton et al. 2018, BJSM 52(6):376-384: fat-free-mass gains plateaued at ~1.62 g/kg/d, mostly in energy balance. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28698222/ . Helms et al. 2014, IJSNEM 24(2):127-138: lean athletes in a deficit likely need 2.3-3.1 g/kg fat-free mass. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24092765/
- Refalo, Trexler & Helms 2025, Strength Cond J: >97% probability of a linear protein dose-response per kg fat-free mass in a deficit (beta 0.06); the per-kg-bodyweight interval included zero. Exploratory. https://doi.org/10.1519/SSC.0000000000000888
- Knowles et al. 2018, J Sci Med Sport 21(9):959-968: consecutive nights of sleep restriction reduced multi-joint force; single nights had little effect. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/29422383/ . Craven et al. 2022, Sports Med 52(11):2669-2690: strength -2.85%, significant only after total deprivation. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-022-01706-y
- Bartholomew et al. 2008, JSCR 22(4):1215-1221: high life-event stress gave smaller squat and bench gains over 12 weeks. https://doi.org/10.1519/JSC.0b013e318173d0bf

**추론**

Direct evidence: the meta-analysis and RCT support a mild cut alongside strength work, though most deficit studies are short and use larger deficits in non-athletes. The size of the effect at 0.25%/wk is extrapolated, and converting 0.25%/wk to ~200 kcal/d assumes typical tissue energy density. The protein figure is per kg fat-free mass, so a g/kg-bodyweight target is an extrapolation. Given the athlete's work and social constraints (C2), recovery is a plausible first cause of any future HOLD or FAIL. That is why the audit needs its inputs (see C11).

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: A slow cut of ~0.25%/wk (~200–250 kcal/d at 75 kg) is plausibly compatible with continued strength gains, but this is extrapolated. The deficit meta-analysis (Murphy & Koehler 2022) found no significant strength cost, but in mostly sedentary or novice and older samples (Analysis A: 7 RCTs, mean age ~60). The only trained-population RCT (Garthe 2011, 0.7%/wk) measured lean mass, not per-lift 1RM. Putting the §23.1 recovery audit before the stimulus audit is a defensible cheapest-first ordering (it changes nothing in the program), not an evidence-tested sequence, and at this mild deficit the recovery audit may often find nothing. The 1.6–2.2 g/kg protein range comes entirely from energy-sufficient trials: Morton 2018 excluded energy-restricted studies, and its breakpoint was 1.62 g/kg with 95% CI 1.03–2.20. Deficit-specific syntheses (Helms 2014; Refalo, Trexler & Helms 2025, exploratory) favour higher intakes per kg of fat-free mass, scaling with deficit severity and leanness. So the upper half of the range is a reasonable precaution aimed at fat-free mass rather than strength. For partial sleep loss, effects on strength and squat bar speed appear mainly after consecutive short nights (Knowles 2018; Knowles 2022: 9 nights at 5 h slowed lower-body lifts up to 15%). A single night of total deprivation is the exception, with about a 3% strength loss (Craven 2022). Stress evidence comes from novice students and supports direction only.

Spec references check out: line 792 is "단백질 섭취 시작 범위 1.6–2.2 g/kg/day" and line 904 is the §23.1 step-1 recovery audit (4-week sleep, days under 1.6 g/kg, loss rate above 0.25%/wk). The arithmetic also holds: 0.1875 kg/wk is about 206 kcal/d at 7,700 kcal/kg, or about 250 at the 9,400 kcal/kg of fat mass Murphy used. Each citation exists and is described accurately at abstract level, but several are cited more strongly than the evidence allows.

(1) Murphy & Koehler 2022 is the main population mismatch. The ES -0.31 (p=0.28) strength and -0.57 lean-mass figures come from Analysis A: 7 RCTs, about 282 participants, mean age about 60. Secondary summaries quote the authors as saying almost all included studies used sedentary or novice participants, and that the only study of experienced lifters showed impaired strength. I could not confirm that from the primary text. Sources: https://sci-sport.com/en/impact-of-energy-deficiency-on-resistance-training-gains/ and https://www.mysportscience.com/post/can-i-gain-muscle-and-lose-weight-at-the-same-time ('jury is still out' for trained lifters). A non-significant ES of -0.31 across 7 trials is absence of evidence, not evidence of no cost. The ~500 kcal/d lean-mass threshold is a meta-regression zero-crossing, and it has been criticised as driven by between-group differences (https://buttondown.com/mdickens/archive/can-you-maintain-lean-mass-in-a-calorie-deficit/). For a trained lifter on a one-year plateau, 'compatible with continued strength gains' is therefore extrapolated. That makes it plausible at 0.25%/wk, but not directly shown.

(2) Garthe 2011 is the only trained-population RCT, and its endpoint is lean mass. Elite athletes losing 0.7 ± 0.8%/wk gained 2.1% lean mass. The abstract gives no per-lift 1RM results (https://lida.sport-iat.de/ta/Record/4020442?lng=en). It supports slow over fast loss, and 0.25%/wk being benign is extrapolation in a favourable direction.

(3) The protein point is correct, and stronger than the claim says. Morton 2018 explicitly excluded energy-restricted trials ('Only trials with humans who were healthy and not energy-restricted'). The 1.62 g/kg breakpoint had a 95% CI of 1.03–2.20, and the fit was not significant (p=0.079); full text at https://openrepository.aut.ac.nz/bitstreams/3e67b2a4-56bc-487f-b31c-5df0d84207af/download. So the spec's 1.6–2.2 range is the point estimate plus the upper CI bound, all from energy-sufficient adults. Refalo, Trexler & Helms 2025 is quoted accurately: 29 studies, >97% probability of a linear relationship, beta 0.06 per kg FFM, and the per-kg-bodyweight interval crosses zero. It is exploratory, and its moderators are lower body fat, more than 4 weeks, and men (https://openrepository.aut.ac.nz/items/1aa54baf-6885-4739-aba7-df0087c3eae8/full). Helms 2014 (2.3–3.1 g/kg FFM) scales the need with deficit severity and leanness, so at a mild 0.25%/wk deficit the upper half of 1.6–2.2 g/kg bodyweight is a precaution, not a demonstrated need. Both reviews measure fat-free mass, not 1RM.

(4) Sleep: 'patterns over single nights' holds for partial restriction but needs a carve-out. Craven 2022 found the strength effect (-2.85%) significant only after total deprivation, which is itself a single night. So one near-sleepless night is an exception. Knowles 2018 says total deprivation had little effect, so the two reviews partly disagree. A direct newer trial strengthens the multi-night reading and is especially relevant to REF5's slowdown judgment: Knowles et al. 2022, MSSE 54(12):2167-2177 (https://acuresearchbank.acu.edu.au/item/8z659/sustained-sleep-restriction-reduces-resistance-exercise-quality-and-quantity-in-females). In 10 resistance-trained women, 9 nights of 5 h time in bed slowed lower-body compound lifts by up to 15%, gave up to 7% greater intraset velocity loss in back squats, and raised session RPE by 11%, while volume load fell by under 1%. On the other side, Borba 2024 found that habitually sleeping 1–2 h short did not prevent resistance-training gains, though the sample was small and untrained (https://thieme-connect.de/products/ejournals/abstract/10.1055/s-0044-1787297). Mild chronic short sleep may therefore matter less than acute multi-night restriction.

(5) Stress: Bartholomew 2008 is a 12-week study of undergraduate students in weight-training classes, mostly novices. It supports the direction only.

(6) 'Recovery audit before stimulus audit is well founded' overstates the evidence. No study tests a diagnostic order. The evidence shows recovery factors can reduce performance and gains, but it gives no ranking of recovery against under-stimulation as the likelier cause of a HOLD or FAIL. At the planned deficit, the expected strength cost from the deficit itself is small. The order is defensible as a cheapest-first logic: step 1 changes nothing in the program, and the user has pre-registered both causes. That is a design rationale, not an evidence finding. The verdict should therefore be 'reasonable, extrapolated', not 'strength'.

Recommendation check: it holds. The 3-item note is operational logging that feeds an existing §23.1 step. It sits within §20, which bars sleep, protein and stress data from branching session prescriptions, and the user can already choose MICRO for any reason. It relaxes no FAIL/HOLD/veto criterion and adds no lifts, RIR or AMRAP. The protein suggestion stays inside the existing range, is presented as a profile note, and changes no protocol element. Suggested refinement: the <6 h threshold is lenient compared with the tested 5 h and 3 h doses, so treat the flag as a cue for a pattern, and separately note a near-sleepless night.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: A cut of about 0.25%/wk (about 200 kcal/d at 75 kg) is compatible with continued strength gains. This rests on direct evidence for strength in mild-to-moderate deficits; the size of the effect at this specific rate is extrapolated. §23.1's recovery-audit-first order is defensible, but mainly because the audit leaves the program unchanged, is reversible, fits C2 and fits one-change-at-a-time. The evidence does not show that recovery factors are the more likely cause of a HOLD or FAIL: effects of a mild deficit or partial sleep loss on strength are small and appear mainly with multi-night restriction or total deprivation. Requiring 2 windows after a correction costs about 7 weeks for SQ, so the audit should flag only multi-night or multi-week patterns. The §20 protein range (1.6–2.2 g/kg) and the §23.1 1.6 g/kg criterion come from energy-balance data. Deficit data (per kg fat-free mass, exploratory) point higher, roughly 1.8–2.6 g/kg body weight at plausible body fat. Aiming for the upper half of the existing range once the cut starts is a reasonable personal-profile note, not a protocol change. To make step 1 executable, three things are needed. (a) Bodyweight must actually be measured; it is already a §7.1 input, but 75 kg appears to be defaulted. (b) A short sleep, protein and stress log is needed, which counts as operational only if kept outside the app. Spec §7.1 limits the user inputs, and §26.2 treats any new user-input item as a protocol change needing version review. (c) The protein log should record days under about 1.6 g/kg, not a yes/no.

What checks out against the spec: line 792 (§20 personal profile) is the 1.6–2.2 g/kg/day protein start range, and line 904 (§23.1 step 1) is the audit of 4-week sleep, days under 1.6 g/kg protein, and weight-loss rate above 0.25%/wk. §23.1 runs the recovery audit first, then the volume audit, then the stimulus card. The arithmetic holds: 0.25% × 75 kg = 0.19 kg/wk, about 206 kcal/d. The cited sources match the verified evidence pack: Murphy & Koehler 2022, Garthe 2011, Morton 2018, Helms 2014, Refalo/Trexler/Helms 2025 (per-FFM β credible, per-BW interval crosses zero), Knowles 2018, Craven 2022 (strength −2.85%, significant only after total deprivation) and Bartholomew 2008. Framing the protein target as a §20 personal-profile note, not a protocol change, matches line 795. Leaving the §23.1 1.6 g/kg criterion and FAIL/HOLD/veto untouched respects the user's fixed constraints.

Points that need revision:
(1) Change class. Spec §7.1 says the user supplies only start time, today's bodyweight, a direct-micro boolean and an OAP-revert boolean. §26.2 treats "사용자가 입력하거나 앱이 자동 계산하는 항목" (items the user inputs or the app auto-calculates) as a protocol change. The 3-item pre-session note is therefore "operational" only as an off-app personal log, like the knee pain log managed outside the program. As an in-app start input it is a single protocol-element change and needs §26 review. The claim labels it operational without that qualifier.
(2) The note alone does not make §23.1 step 1 executable. The weight-loss-rate item needs real bodyweight, and the DB shows 75 kg logged every session, probably a default. Fixing that needs no protocol change because bodyweight is already a §7.1 input, but the user has to actually measure it. The yes/no protein item is also coarser than the spec's "days under 1.6 g/kg" criterion. The pre-session yes/no stress item also differs from what the cited evidence measured: life-event or chronic stress over weeks, not acute state.
(3) "Well founded" is overstated for the ordering. The evidence shows that recovery factors can blunt strength gains. Effect sizes at this athlete's likely exposure are small, though: a mild ~200 kcal/d deficit gave a non-significant strength effect from about 5 studies, and partial sleep restriction has little effect unless it is multi-night or total. So the literature does not establish recovery as the more probable cause of a HOLD or FAIL. The recovery-first order is defensible mainly because step 1 leaves the program unchanged, is cheap and reversible, fits the athlete's C2 constraint, and fits the one-change-at-a-time rule. It also has a cost the claim omits. Step 1 requires observing 2 windows after a correction, which is about 7 weeks for SQ at the observed ~24–25 days per window. That delays the volume and stimulus audits, so the trigger for "problem found" should require multi-night or multi-week patterns, which the claim's own advice to read sleep as a pattern supports.
(4) Protein conversion. Helms's 2.3–3.1 g/kg FFM range equals roughly 1.8–2.6 g/kg body weight at 15–20% body fat (body fat is unknown). The "upper half of 1.6–2.2" therefore sits at the low end of the deficit-specific range. That is reasonable for a mild deficit, since Helms scales need with deficit severity and leanness, but it is an extrapolation. Morton's "mostly in energy balance" detail was not verified from the abstract.

Recommendation check: it violates no fixed constraint (no RIR, AMRAP, 1RM test, accessories or judgment relaxation) and changes at most one element. It is acceptable once it is scoped as an off-app log or routed through §26.

## C13 · stagnation/decrease · tradeoff · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 operational

The decrease logic tolerates noise but is slow and cannot tell causes apart:
- An immediate -2.5 kg needs 2 consecutive FAILs in the same stream (SQ_H3 and SQ_H2 are separate streams). That is about one test-retest CV, gentler than the 5-10% resets common in coaching.
- Stagnation detection needs ~3 windows (~9-12 weeks for SQ) and cannot separate overload from underload (§22.4.3).
- The 'stagnation micro' is a single 4-set session: a pause before reassessment, not a recovery period.
- Any recalibration, new plan or version cutover erases in-progress windows, stagnation history, hard-start times and the structureReview flag. The 9/01 to 9/05 cutover discarded 13 exposures, ran H3 twice in a row, and stretched the BP-focus gap to 9 days.
A cutover can silently reset the §23 'second stagnation decrease at the same basis' trigger, including through §23.1 step 4. Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Grgic et al. 2020: CV 3.3%, so one FAIL pair at 2.5 kg resolution is a reasonable noise filter. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-020-00260-z
- Coaching contrast: Alter 2018 (Starting Strength) resets 5-10% after a second consecutive failure at the same load. https://startingstrength.com/article/the-reset-why-and-how . StrongLifts resets ~10% after 3 failures. https://stronglifts.com/stronglifts-5x5/failure/ . REF5's -2.5 kg is smaller, so a real deficit may need several FAIL pairs.
- Wendler 2024 (coaching): progress at a plateau takes 'several months or even longer'; stay at a lighter TM 'one cycle longer than you think'. https://www.jimwendler.com/blogs/jimwendler-com/building-strength-and-the-training-max
- Bell et al. 2023 Delphi: a deload is a period of reduced training stress. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-023-00633-0 . Rogerson et al. 2024: deloads last ~6.4 days. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-024-00691-y
- Pelland et al. 2026 and Enes et al. 2024 (MSSE 56(3):553-563): realized volume predicts strength gain, which supports §23.1 checking missed sessions and volume before lowering the standard. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/37796222/
- Internal record: the cutover discarded SQ 3/6, BP 2/4, PULL 3/4, DL 3/4 and OHP 2/4 exposures. Spec §15, §19.6, §24.3: the v1.4 decoder rejects v1.3 state, and a new plan resets hardStartTimes, startedSessions, the stagnation phase and history, and structureReview.

**추론**

The evidence says nothing about reset size or stall-detection rules in trained lifters, so the rule values are unvalidated design resting on arithmetic, reliability data and coaching convention. Calling the stagnation micro too small for recovery is an inference from deload definitions. The history reset is a code and spec fact. The spec's §23.1 order (recovery, then stimulus) is the right mitigation for not being able to tell causes apart.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: The decrease logic responds to clear FAIL pairs but moves conservatively and cannot separate causes. No study validates any of its values.
- An immediate -2.5 kg needs 2 consecutive valid FAILs in the same stream (§14; SQ_H3, SQ_H2 and SQ_V are separate streams, and every stream of that lift resets after any change). A 2.5 kg step is ~2.4-2.9% of the load, about 0.5-0.9 of the trained 1RM test-retest CV (3.3%; Grgic 2020). One step therefore sits inside measurement noise. That reliability figure says nothing about how often a rep-deficit/slowdown FAIL fires falsely. A coaching contrast at the level of principle: Starting Strength resets 5% after a stall (2nd failure at the same load) and StrongLifts ~10% after 3 failures, both in novice linear progression at near-limit loads. By the spec's own arithmetic (§19.4 'FAIL cascade'), a large real deficit needs several FAIL pairs.
- Stagnation: a 2nd MAINTAIN window queues one 4-set micro, and a 3rd MAINTAIN window lowers the standard. For SQ that takes ~8.3 weeks at the fastest the density cap allows and ~10-12 weeks at the observed ~1.75 hard starts/week. The rule cannot separate overload from underload (§22.4.3). The stagnation micro is a single reduced session before reassessment, not a deload as consensus and survey sources describe one (~1 week; Bell 2023, Rogerson 2024). No trial shows that a deload would resolve stagnation.
- Mitigation: the §23.1 order (recovery audit, then volume audit) is a reasonable heuristic, not an evidence-validated one. It is triggered late (only on structureReview), and the earlier recovery check is the §20 personal rule to pause the cut on any SQ decrease. Dose-response meta-regressions (Pelland 2026) support a volume audit only indirectly.
- Every new plan, whether a recalibration, a version cutover or §23.1 step 4, is replayed from a fresh initial state. That clears in-progress windows, hardStartTimes (so the 48 h/168 h gate is bypassed), the H3/H2 and focus queues (reset to H3/PULL), stagnation history and structureReview. A cutover therefore silently disarms the §23 trigger 'second stagnation decrease at the same basis'. The 9/01 to 9/05 cutover discarded 13 in-progress exposures and ran H3 twice in a row. It also helped stretch a BP-focus gap to 9 days, 4 of which were plain calendar gap. These record details were not re-verified in this check.

The code and spec facts hold. The literature is cited accurately, but a few of the bridges from that literature to REF5 are stronger than the sources allow.

VERIFIED IN CODE AND SPEC:
- §14: FAIL streams are separate (SQ_H3, SQ_H2, SQ_V_NORMAL, SQ_V_MICRO). A -2.5 kg change needs 2 consecutive valid FAILs in one stream. After any change of the direct standard, all streams of that lift reset (resetMainLiftAfterDirectChange).
- Stagnation (§15 and ref5.ts advanceMaintainedStagnation / REASSESSMENT path): the 1st MAINTAIN window is recorded; the 2nd queues a single 4-set micro; the 3rd MAINTAIN window gives -2.5 kg. structureReview turns on only when a 2nd stagnation decrease happens at the same basisKg.
- Plan reset: every plan is replayed from createInitialRef5State (ref5-auto-progression.ts:900). A new plan therefore starts with nextFocus=PULL, nextSquatHard=H3, empty hardStartTimes and windows, and fresh stagnation state with decreaseHistory=[] and structureReview=false. §23.1 step 4 (recalibrate into a new plan) wipes the very history that §23 relies on.
- §22.4.3 does say that stagnation cannot separate overload from underload.
- Because hardStartTimes is cleared at cutover, the 48 h / 168 h density gate is also bypassed. This supports the 'H3 twice in a row' mechanism. I did not re-query the DB for the 9/01 to 9/05 numbers. The 3+2+3+3+2 = 13 arithmetic is consistent, but 4 of the 9 days in the BP-focus gap were ordinary calendar gap, not caused by the cutover.

EVIDENCE FIDELITY PROBLEMS:
(1) Grgic 2020 (re-fetched). Median 1RM CV is 3.3% in trained lifters, 4.7% for lower body, with retest intervals of 1 to 10 days. A 2.5 kg step is 2.4% of the ~104 REF and 2.9% of the 85 H3, about 0.5 to 0.9 of one CV. That supports 'a single step sits inside 1RM test-retest noise'. It does NOT support 'a FAIL pair is a reasonable noise filter'. Grgic gives no data on how often a rep-deficit or slowdown judgment fires falsely at ~82% 1RM, nor on day-to-day readiness under poor sleep and nutrition. If H3 really leaves 2 to 5 reps in reserve, a single FAIL is far bigger than CV-level noise. That makes the size of the response the real issue, not the filter.
(2) Alter 2018 (re-fetched). 5-10% is the general definition of a reset, which also covers layoffs and injuries. The specific reset after a first stall (the same load failed twice) is 5%. Missing reps in one workout is explicitly not a reason to reset. Both Alter and StrongLifts address novice linear progression at near-limit loads, so the comparison of reset sizes is a principle-level contrast, not like for like. 'A real deficit may need several FAIL pairs' rests on the spec's own arithmetic and on the 'FAIL cascade' passage in §19.4, not on research.
(3) The calendar timing is roughly right but imprecise. The stagnation micro is queued after 2 windows, and the decrease comes after 3. Under the 2-per-168h cap the fastest possible path to the 18th hard start is ~58 days (~8.3 weeks). At the observed ~1.75 hard starts per week it is ~10.5 weeks.
(4) Bell 2023 and Rogerson 2024 are expert consensus and a survey, not outcome evidence. Calling the micro 'not a recovery period' is true by definition. Coleman 2024 does not show that a week-long deload would fix stagnation either.
(5) Pelland and Enes measure the dose that study protocols prescribe, not whether a person did the volume they planned. Enes 2024 MSSE is also confounded by total quad volume at very high doses. Their support for a 'missed-volume audit' is therefore indirect, and weaker for SQ, which already sits on Pelland's flatter part of the curve.
(6) 'The right mitigation' overstates the evidence. Recovery-then-stimulus is a reasonable heuristic. Murphy & Koehler 2022 actually found that an energy deficit did not significantly blunt strength gains, so the weight-loss-rate audit item is low-yield for strength. §23.1 also acts late, because it runs only after a 2nd stagnation decrease at the same basis, so it does not resolve the cause at the first decrease. The claim leaves out §20's earlier step: 'stop the cut and review recovery on any SQ decrease'.

RECOMMENDATION: OK. It is operational, stays within all fixed constraints, and does not change any protocol element. Two caveats:
- Hand-written notes do not re-arm the engine's §23 trigger, so the user has to apply that trigger manually.
- Timing a cutover right after an SQ window closes does not line up the BP/PULL/DL/OHP windows. The rule is moot for the new plans after 4-8+ week gaps that §19.4 recommends.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Verdict: tradeoff. REF5's decrease logic resists noise. It is slow, it cannot tell causes apart, and any new plan wipes its memory.

1. Immediate decrease (§14). A -2.5 kg drop needs 2 consecutive valid FAILs in the same stream. SQ_H3 and SQ_H2 are separate streams, and results in other streams neither add to nor break a streak. The fastest SQ decrease therefore takes at least 3 hard exposures (H3 FAIL, then H2, then H3 FAIL), about 1.2–1.7 weeks at the observed ~1.75 hard/week. All streams reset after each step, so a ~5% deficit needs at least 6 hard exposures and at least 4 FAILs. A 2.5 kg step is ~2.9% of the H3 standard, about one trained 1RM test-retest CV (Grgic 2020: 3.3%). That is an analogy, not a validated threshold. Coaching resets after repeated failure are larger (Starting Strength: ~5% after a second consecutive miss, within a general 5–10% range; StrongLifts: ~10% after 3), but those apply to near-limit linear progression, so the comparison is loose.

2. Stagnation path (§15; §22.4.3–4). The stagnation micro comes after 2 consecutive MAINTAIN windows at the same basis. The decrease comes only if the 3rd (reassessment) window is also MAINTAIN. Any INCREASE or immediate decrease restarts the count. For SQ that is about 8.5 weeks at most (maximum density) and about 10.5 weeks at the observed 24–25-day windows. The program cannot separate overload from underload. Mitigations exist: the §20 recovery review at any SQ decrease, §21.1's pre-registered reading, and §23.1 (recovery audit first, then volume).

3. The stagnation micro is one 4-set session (§7.4, §8.3). It is a marker before reassessment, not a recovery period. This is an inference from the deload definitions in Bell 2023 and Rogerson 2024.

4. New plans erase everything (engine: foldRef5ReplaySource starts every plan from createInitialRef5State). Any recalibration, new plan or version cutover erases:
- windows and FAIL streams
- stagnation phase and decrease history, and the structureReview flag
- hard-start times and the started-session density memory
- forced-micro tokens and the focus/H3 turn

Within a plan, §15 deliberately keeps the decrease history and the flag. The spec says only generally that progression state does not carry over (§24.3). A plan change, including §23.1 step 4 itself, therefore silently resets the §23 "second stagnation decrease at the same basis" trigger. Today nothing is lost: all stagnation histories are empty.

5. The 9/01→9/05 cutover, as recorded in the DB:
- It discarded 13 exposures: SQ 3/6, BP 2/4, PULL 3/4, DL 3/4, OHP 2/4.
- It ran H3 twice in a row and PULL focus twice in a row.
- It stretched the BP-focus gap to ~9 days, instead of 7.
- Most concretely, it bypassed the §9 cap. On 9/07 the engine prescribed H2 with startsIn168Hours=1, while continuous state (counting 9/01) would have prescribed V. The result was 3 hard SQ starts in about 6 days, which matters given the athlete's quadriceps-tendon pain.

Recommendation (operational, within §26.1, no protocol change): when the timing of a plan change is discretionary, put it right after a SQ window closes (§19.6), accepting that the other lifts' in-progress windows are still lost. Gap-forced new plans (§19.4) and step 4 after a BP/PULL structureReview are exempt. Before any plan change, record by hand in the plan notes:
- each stagnation decrease's basis and count
- any structureReview flag
- the last two hard-SQ start times

Then raise structureReview manually if a recorded basis stalls again. If the new plan's first sessions would break the 48 h/168 h gate, choose a manual micro (§8 item 1).

I checked the claim against the spec (docs/ref5-program-spec.md §8, §9, §14, §15, §19.4, §19.6, §21.1, §22.4, §23, §23.1, §24.3), the engine (packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts: createInitialRef5State, resetMainLiftAfterDirectChange, recordStagnationDecrease, advanceMaintainedStagnation, selectRef5SquatPrescription), the replay path (packages/core/src/progression/ref5-auto-progression.ts foldRef5ReplaySource, which starts every plan from createInitialRef5State and replays only that plan's generated_session rows), and the production DB using read-only SELECTs.

What is confirmed:
(1) Immediate decrease. §14 is correct: a decrease needs 2 consecutive valid FAILs in the same stream, and SQ_H3 and SQ_H2 are separate streams. Results in other streams do not break a streak. So the fastest SQ decrease takes at least 3 hard exposures (H3 FAIL, then H2, then H3 FAIL), about 1.2–1.7 weeks at the observed ~1.75 hard/week. Every SQ stream resets after a decrease, so a ~5% deficit (two steps) needs at least 6 hard exposures and at least 4 FAILs.
(2) Stagnation path. The stagnation micro is scheduled after 2 consecutive MAINTAIN windows at the same basis. The stagnation decrease comes only when the reassessment window (the 3rd) also ends MAINTAIN. Any INCREASE or immediate decrease resets the count (resetMainLiftAfterDirectChange). Observed SQ windows took 24 days (9/05–9/29) and ~25 days (7/19–8/13), so 3 windows is about 10.5 weeks. At maximum density (one hard every 3.5 days) it is about 8.5 weeks. "~9–12 weeks" is fair, and §21.1 itself says ~9 weeks. §22.4.3 does say the program cannot tell overload from underload.
(3) The stagnation micro is exactly one session. PENDING_MICRO forces a micro on the next actual session, START moves the lift to REASSESSMENT, and several pending lifts merge into one micro (§8.3). The micro is 4 sets (§7.4). Calling it "not a recovery period" is an inference from deload definitions (Bell 2023: a period; Rogerson 2024: ~6.4 days). It is fairly labeled as inference.
(4) A new plan wipes all state. foldRef5ReplaySource rebuilds state per plan from createInitialRef5State. So any recalibration, new plan or version cutover empties windows, fail streams, stagnation phase, decreaseHistory, structureReview, hardStartTimes, startedSessions (the memory of the density valve), forced-micro tokens, and the focus/H3 turn (reset to PULL/H3). Inside a plan, §15 and §10.3 deliberately keep the stagnation history and the structureReview flag across standard changes. §24.3 says only in general terms that progression state is not carried over. It never says that §23's "second stagnation decrease at the same basis" trigger is lost, or that §23.1 step 4 (recalibrate, then a new plan) wipes the very history that triggered it. "Silently" is therefore fair. Today nothing has been lost: the live state shows BASELINE stagnation and empty decreaseHistory for SQ, BP and PULL.
(5) DB check of the cutover. The old plan's in-progress exposures were: SQ 3/6 (8/22 H3, 8/24 H2, 9/01 H3), BP 2/4 (8/24, 8/29), PULL 3/4 (8/22, 8/25, 9/01), DL 3/4 and OHP 2/4, which is 13. The new plan's first hard was H3 on 9/05, right after H3 on 9/01. The last normal BP focus was 8/29 and the next was 9/07 (about 9.2 days). Without the queue reset it would have been 9/05 (7 days), and PULL focus also ran twice in a row (9/01, 9/05).

What the claim missed, and it is concrete: the reset of hardStartTimes actually overrode the §9 density gate once. On 9/07 13:10Z the engine logged startsIn168Hours=1 and prescribed H2. Counting the real 9/01 12:39Z hard start, there were 2 hard starts in the open 168 h window, so continuous state would have prescribed V. The cutover therefore produced 3 hard SQ starts in about 6 days (9/01, 9/05, 9/07). That matters for this athlete because of the undiagnosed quadriceps-tendon pain.

Smaller corrections:
- Alter 2018's specific rule after a second consecutive failure is a ~5% reset. 5–10% is his general definition of a reset, and the context is novice linear progression at near-limit loads. That makes "gentler than coaching resets" a loose, cross-context comparison, not like-for-like.
- 2.5 kg is ~2.9% of H3 85, about one trained 1RM CV (Grgic 2020: 3.3%). That is an analogy, not a validated threshold.
- The reset facts are engine and code facts. §15, §19.6 and §24.3 do not list hardStartTimes or structureReview.
- Pelland and Enes concern prescribed volume dose, not realized adherence. They support the §23.1 volume audit only indirectly.
- §20 already prompts a recovery review at the first SQ decrease of either kind, so the inability to tell causes apart is mitigated earlier than §23.1.

Recommendation: it is operational (§26.1) and violates no fixed constraint. It relaxes no judgment criterion, and adds no accessories, RIR, AMRAP, 1RM test or deload. It is not a bundle of protocol changes. Two caveats:
(a) "Only right after a SQ window closes" should read "when timing is discretionary". §19.4 forces a new plan after 4–8+ week gaps, §23.1 step 4 can follow a BP/PULL structureReview while a SQ window is still open, and the windows of the 5 lifts are generally not aligned. At this cutover, waiting for SQ would still have dropped the BP, PULL, DL and OHP windows in progress.
(b) A carried MAINTAIN count cannot be honored by the engine. Only the stagnation-decrease basis/count and the structureReview flag matter for the §23 trigger. The hand ledger should instead also hold the last two hard-SQ start times, so the user can protect the 48 h/168 h gate with a manual micro (§8 item 1). This stays inside the same operational element.

Sources I could not verify independently are the DB-derived session validity of the old plan's 13 exposures (structure was verified, outcome validity is inferred from the INCREASE events) and the coaching sources, which I took from the evidence pack.

## C14 · deload/fatigue · strength · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 doc-or-display

Leaving out scheduled deloads is consistent with the evidence, which is too weak to require them: one cessation RCT plus Delphi consensus and surveys. The user's 'strong evidence' bar for a §26 deload proposal is not met. Unplanned unloading already happens through:
- REF5's low per-session volume and non-failure sets;
- the density valve (a micro after back-to-back days or after ≥3 normal starts in 6 days; a forced micro after FAILs in 2 different lifts);
- the athlete's own irregular gaps (three 8-day breaks in 87 days; the 10/14 return session was all PASS).
Spec §22.2 (line 842) overstates Coleman 2024 when it calls lower-body strength gains '열위' ('inferior'). Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Coleman et al. 2024, PeerJ 12:e16777: one week of complete cessation in a high-volume, to-failure program changed squat 1RM by -3.6 kg (95% CrI -10.4 to +3.2; posterior 0.851 favouring continuous training). A reduced-load deload was not tested. https://peerj.com/articles/16777/
- Bell et al. 2023, Sports Med Open 9:87 (Delphi): a deload is a period of reduced training stress, may be pre-planned or autoregulated, and is 'under-researched' (expert opinion). https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-023-00633-0
- Rogerson et al. 2024, Sports Med Open 10:26: athletes deload every 5.6 ± 2.3 wk; 13.4% deload purely reactively; the authors note a 'clear lack of empirical research'. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-024-00691-y
- Bosquet et al. 2013, Scand J Med Sci Sports 23(3):e140-e149: losses from cessation grow with duration and are smaller in recreationally trained than in inactive people. https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/sms.12047
- Internal record: 4 micros (1 manual, 3 from the density valve); 8-day gaps 7/26 to 8/03, 8/13 to 8/21 and 10/06 to 10/14.

**추론**

The direct evidence is thin, so the decision is 'not contradicted' rather than proven. Reduced-load deloads in low-volume submaximal programs have not been tested. The spec's wording should match the actual effect size, whose credible interval crosses zero.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Leaving out scheduled deloads is consistent with the evidence and not contradicted by it. Two small randomized trials found no benefit from deloading:
- Coleman 2024 (trained adults; one week of full cessation inside a to-failure program of about 40 lower-body sets/wk; Smith-machine squat). The squat 1RM gain was 3.6 kg smaller than with continuous training (95% CrI -10.4 to +3.2; posterior 0.851 favouring continuous). This is a weak, uncertain tendency.
- Pancar 2026, Sci Rep 16:10299 (untrained men; reduced volume and frequency in weeks 4 and 8; single-joint exercises; 10RM). It found no difference.

Beyond these, the evidence is a Delphi consensus (Bell 2023: deloads may be planned or autoregulated, and are under-researched) and a survey (Rogerson 2024). No trial has tested a reduced-load deload in trained lifters or in a low-volume, non-failure program like REF5. The user's 'strong evidence' bar for a §26 deload proposal is not met.

REF5's low per-session volume and non-failure sets lower the rationale for deloads. This rests on recovery studies of failure vs non-failure sets and is extrapolation. Session-level reactive reductions (manual or density-valve micros) and the athlete's irregular gaps add further unplanned reductions in training stress. The gaps are effectively cessation, which Bosquet 2013 suggests costs little at about a week. Gap and micro counts are taken from the provided record and were not independently verified. A single all-PASS session after a gap carries no information, because no HOLD or FAIL has yet been recorded.

Spec §22.2 line 842 overstates Coleman 2024 in two places: it calls lower-body strength gains '열위' ('inferior') without the authors' 'appears to' hedge, and it says the trial 'supports' reactive micros over fixed deloads. The trial only compared cessation with continuous training.

Core conclusion holds: no trial shows that scheduled deloads improve strength, and the user's 'strong evidence' bar for a §26 deload proposal is not met. Several supporting details need correcting.

VERIFIED
1. Coleman 2024, PeerJ (https://peerj.com/articles/16777/), checked against the PeerJ page:
- 50 randomized, 39 completed.
- Lower body was 4 exercises x 5 sets, twice a week, about 40 lower-body sets/wk (about 90 total). Participants were encouraged to volitional failure, though some stopped short.
- Squat 1RM was tested on a Smith machine.
- Table 3 univariate squat difference is -3.6 kg (CrI -10.4 to 3.2); 1 - 0.149 = 0.851 favouring continuous training.
- The authors say the results are specific to cessation and that a reduced-load deload was not tested.
- The abstract itself is hedged: a deload 'appears to negatively influence' lower-body strength.

2. Spec line 842 reads '하체 근력 향상이 열위였고' ('lower-body strength gains were inferior'). This drops the authors' hedge even though the CrIs cross zero, so the claim of a mild overstatement is correct. The same sentence also says the result '지지한다' (supports) REF5's choice of reactive micros over fixed deloads. That is a second overstatement the recommendation does not mention: Coleman compared cessation with continuous training, not scheduled deloads with reactive micros.

3. Bell 2023 (Delphi), Rogerson 2024 (5.6 ± 2.3 wk; 13.4% autoregulated; 'clear lack of empirical research') and Bosquet 2013 (losses grow with duration and are smaller in recreationally trained people) are cited accurately at the stated strength, according to the evidence pack.

CORRECTIONS
(a) The evidence base is described as 'one cessation RCT plus Delphi and surveys'. That is out of date. Pancar et al. 2026, Sci Rep 16:10299 (doi:10.1038/s41598-026-40612-5; registry NCT06825052, completed), is a randomized within-subject trial in 19 untrained men. It used a reduced volume/frequency deload in weeks 4 and 8 (once a week, 2 sets) on single-joint exercises taken to failure, and found no difference in 10RM or muscle thickness. It strengthens the conclusion (no deload benefit), but the description should be updated. 'Reduced-load deloads untested' stays true only for load reductions, and for trained lifters.

(b) The phrase '-3.6 kg change' should say it is a between-group difference in gain, not a change.

(c) The three bullets under 'unplanned unloading' are reasoning, not evidence:
- Low per-session volume and non-failure sets are low baseline stress, not unloading. They lower the rationale for a deload (indirectly supported by faster recovery after non-failure sets: Moran-Navarro 2017, Pareja-Blanco 2017).
- A 4-set micro is a single-session reduction. Surveyed deloads last about 6.4 days.
- The 8-day gaps are de facto cessation weeks. That is the very intervention Coleman found weakly unfavourable, though Bosquet suggests it costs little. So the gaps remove any remaining need for unloading; they are not evidence that unloading helps.

(d) Internal-record items are unverified. DB access was denied to me. The record supplied for this task lists 28 sessions = 15 + 13, ending 10/06, with 'an 8-day gap through 10/14'. A '10/14 return session, all PASS' is not in that record. Even if it exists, it tells us nothing: every main-lift window so far ended INCREASE with no HOLD or FAIL, so an all-PASS session is the expected outcome. I could not check 'three 8-day breaks' or '4 micros (1 manual, 3 valve)'.

(e) Transfer is limited. Both trials used hypertrophy-style sets to failure (one Smith-machine squat, one single-joint in untrained men). REF5 is low-volume, heavy, non-failure and free-weight high-bar.

RECOMMENDATION
Documentation only, one element (§22.2), within all fixed constraints. It should also soften '지지한다' to 'is consistent with' and cite Pancar 2026.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Leaving out scheduled deloads is consistent with the evidence but not proven by it. The evidence is one RCT of a complete week off (Coleman 2024), a coach Delphi (Bell 2023) and a practice survey (Rogerson 2024), which is too weak to meet the user's 'strong evidence' bar for a §26 deload proposal.

What REF5 actually has instead (spec §8, §14, §15):
- A manual micro, which spec line 375 names as the self-recovery valve for a program without scheduled deloads.
- A forced micro after new FAILs in 2 different main lifts within the last 2 completed sessions.
- A stagnation micro.
- A -2.5 kg cut after 2 consecutive FAILs in the same stream.
- Density rules that cap clustering: a micro when both of the previous two calendar days were program days (the third straight day), or when there were 3 or more normal starts in the previous 6 days.

Low per-lift volume (2-3 sets) and non-failure sets probably build far less fatigue than Coleman's ~40 lower-body sets/wk to failure. That point is inference, not tested.

The athlete's ~8-day gaps (3 in 87 days, about one every 4 weeks) are complete rest, not deloads. They make an added scheduled deload redundant in practice. They should not be counted as a designed unloading mechanism, and Coleman's weak signal, if anything, points against weeks off.

An all-PASS session after the gap tells us nothing, because every kg-lift exposure so far has been PASS.

Spec §22.2 line 842 overstates Coleman 2024 in two places:
- '열위' (inferior): the result was a weak, uncertain tendency (squat -3.6 kg, 95% CrI -10.4 to +3.2, posterior 0.851).
- '지지한다' (supports): it should read 'does not contradict'. The trial tested neither reduced-load deloads nor reactive micros.

The '50명' (50 participants) figure can stand as enrolment (39 analysed).

What I checked: the spec (docs/ref5-program-spec.md §3.2, §8, §19.4, §22.2–22.4, §23.1, §26) and the engine (packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts, decideRef5SessionType at lines 1154–1183). Read-only.

CONFIRMED
- Spec line 842 says '중간 1주 완전 중단군은 연속 훈련군 대비 하체 근력 향상이 열위였고' (the mid-program 1-week full-stop group had inferior lower-body strength gains vs continuous training). That is an overstatement. Coleman's squat effect was -3.6 kg with a CrI crossing zero (posterior 0.851; the evidence pack confirms these values against Table 3). The same line also says the result '지지한다' (supports) REF5's choice of reactive micros and decreases. That wording also goes beyond the evidence: Coleman tested a week of complete rest inside a high-volume, to-failure Smith-machine program, not reactive micros. The claim's rewrite fixes '열위' but misses '지지한다'. Both sit in one bullet, so the fix is still one document element.
- §3.2 line 80 excludes fixed deload weeks. §8 item 1 (line 375) states that the manual micro doubles as the self-recovery valve. So "no scheduled deloads, use reactive valves" is the spec's actual rule, and the claim's framing matches it.
- The engine matches the claim's trigger list: MANUAL; CONSECUTIVE_PROGRAM_DAYS (both previous calendar days were program days); NORMAL_SESSION_DENSITY (3 or more NORMAL starts in the previous 6 dates); FORCED_PRIMARY_FAILS; STAGNATION_*. Precision fix: the claim's "a micro after back-to-back days" means a micro on the third consecutive day.
- Omission: the claim leaves out the stagnation micro and the -2.5 kg cut after 2 consecutive FAILs (§14, §15). These are the "감소" (decrease) mechanisms line 842 itself cites.
- The recommendation is documentation-only and a single element. §26.1 treats wording as non-protocol. It stays inside every fixed constraint and relaxes no judgment criterion. recommendationOk = true.
- Citations: Coleman, Bell 2023 (Delphi; pre-planned or autoregulated; 'under-researched'), Rogerson 2024 (5.6±2.3 wk; 13.4% autoregulated; 'clear lack of empirical research') and Bosquet 2013 (losses grow with duration and are smaller in recreationally trained people) are all marked confirmed in the recovery and comparison verification packs. The intensity pack's 'unverifiable' tags for Bell and Bosquet came from access limits.

APPLICABILITY PROBLEMS (why this is 'with revision')
1) Treating the athlete's irregular gaps as "unplanned unloading" is a category error. An 8-day gap is complete rest, not a reduced-load deload, which is exactly the condition Coleman found weakly worse. It is also not a REF5 mechanism. If C1 changes (a regular 3–4x/wk schedule), the gaps disappear and the spec's own valves are what remain.
   The gaps are still practically relevant. 87 days (7/19–10/14, arithmetic checked) with 3 gaps is about one gap every 4.1 weeks, inside the surveyed deload interval of 5.6±2.3 wk. So in practice they make an added scheduled deload redundant. That is extrapolation.
2) "The 10/14 return session was all PASS" carries no information about unloading. A previous run's applicability check (C8) found all 106 kg-lift exposures PASS, so an all-PASS session is the baseline, not a recovery signal.
   I also could not verify that the 10/14 session exists. My read-only DB query was blocked by the permission classifier in this session. The brief I was given says 28 sessions with an 8-day gap through 10/14, which suggests no session yet. A previous run's C4 applicability note says the brief is out of date, so the session plausibly exists, but it is unconfirmed here.
   For the same reason I could not confirm '4 micros (1 manual, 3 density)'.
3) "Low per-session volume and non-failure sets" lower fatigue accumulation. That is a reason a deload is less needed, not a form of unloading. The direction is supported by the failure-recovery literature (Morán-Navarro 2017, Pareja-Blanco 2017), but applying it to deload need is extrapolation and coaching opinion, since Bell's definition is fatigue-based.
4) The '50명' in line 842 can stand as enrolment (50 randomised, 39 analysed, per the recovery pack). The claim correctly does not attack it.

EVIDENCE TYPE
- Direct: Coleman, which is a week of complete rest, not a deload.
- Expert opinion: Bell 2023.
- Practice description: Rogerson 2024.
- Indirect: Bosquet 2013.
- No study tests a reduced-load deload in a low-volume, submaximal program. The bottom line, that no scheduled-deload proposal is warranted, therefore holds as "not contradicted", and medium confidence is appropriate.

## C15 · concurrent climbing · risk · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 operational

Climbing in separate sessions is unlikely to blunt SQ, BP or DL strength, so leaving it out of the kg logic is defensible. Its real cost is local overuse load on the same elbow, finger and shoulder tissues that heavy pull-ups and OAP load. The engine has no climbing input (removed in v1.2), and adherence to the §19.5 manual rules is not recorded. Those rules are 'no climbing the day before or of OAP' and 'no PULL focus within 48 h of hard climbing'. The lenses agree on substance; A labels it a tradeoff, B a risk. Merged as a risk because the exposure is unmonitored.

**근거**

- Schumann et al. 2022, Sports Med 52(3):601-612 (43 studies): concurrent aerobic training did not blunt maximal strength (SMD -0.06) or hypertrophy; explosive strength was attenuated only within the same session. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-021-01587-7
- Petre et al. 2021, Sports Med 51(5):991-1010: in trained lifters, lower-body 1RM interference was ES -0.66 within the same session and -0.10 (not significant) across sessions. https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-021-01426-9
- Schoffl et al. 2015, Wilderness Environ Med 26(1):62-67 (911 injuries): 58% were overstrain; shoulder 17.2%, forearm/elbow 9.1%. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.wem.2014.08.013 . Gronhaug 2018, BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med: elbows were 17.7% of chronic climbing injuries. https://bmjopensem.bmj.com/content/4/1/e000406
- Quarmby et al. 2023, Front Sports Act Living 5:1269870: bouldering, higher intensity and previous injury were risk factors; evidence on volume conflicted. https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/sports-and-active-living/articles/10.3389/fspor.2023.1269870/full
- Gaspari et al. 2024, Front Sports Act Living 6:1251047 (n=9 elite boulderers): grip recovered by 24 h; readiness normalised by ~48-60 h. https://www.frontiersin.org/journals/sports-and-active-living/articles/10.3389/fspor.2024.1251047/full
- Internal record: no climbing sessions or elbow notes are stored in the DB. Spec §19.4 and §19.5.1(a) are manual rules, not enforced.

**추론**

The no-interference point is an extrapolation from aerobic concurrent-training data; there are no climbing-specific trials. The elbow risk rests on climbing injury epidemiology, which is mostly cross-sectional. How much OAP plus weighted pull-ups adds on top of climbing cannot be quantified. Climbing frequency is also inconsistent across sources: 1-2 times a month on the constraints sheet, 'ongoing' elsewhere.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Climbing in separate sessions is unlikely to blunt SQ, BP or DL strength. This is an extrapolation: the concurrent-training data come from running and cycling, not climbing. Interference appears to be short-lived (about 8 h or less) and local to the muscles used, and climbing is mainly upper-body pulling and grip work done at low frequency. Leaving climbing out of the SQ, BP and DL kg logic is therefore defensible.

PULL is the exception. Climbing can leave local fatigue in the same muscles. In the worst case, climbing within 48 h of a PULL focus can produce false HOLD or FAIL calls: they void that window's increase, and two in a row would trigger -2.5 kg. The resulting error is conservative and pushes loads down; it does not make them unsafe.

Separately, there is a plausible but unquantified overuse exposure at the elbow (brachialis, distal biceps, medial epicondyle) and the shoulder. Heavy weighted pull-ups and especially OAP load the same structures. Finger overlap is minor, because bar work does not load the pulleys the way crimping does. This exposure is inferred from cross-sectional injury-site epidemiology and coaching sources. It has not been shown to be additive, and one RCT suggests structured strength training lowers elbow injury in climbers.

The engine has no climbing input (removed in v1.2). Adherence to the manual rules is not recorded: no climbing the day before or the day of OAP (§19.5.1(a)), and no PULL focus within 48 h of hard climbing (§19.4). Classify this as a risk because the exposure is unmonitored, not because harm has been demonstrated.

FACTS CHECKED IN THE REPO (read-only).
(1) The engine has no climbing input. Confirmed: spec §3.2 bans external-activity prescription changes, the §28 changelog row for v1.2 removes climbing inputs, and the climb/climbing/climbingWithin48h/strongClimbing keys appear in packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts:884-892 only as rejection markers.
(2) Both manual rules exist, but the claim's section labels are slightly off. "Strong climbing must not fall within 48 h before a PULL focus" is §19.4 (docs/ref5-program-spec.md:742). "No climbing the day before or the day of OAP" is §19.5.1(a) (line 765). The claim text attributes both to §19.5; its evidence line gets them right. §19.4 (line 743) also already recommends a weekly elbow pain memo.
(3) I did not query the DB, so "no climbing sessions or elbow notes stored" is taken from the brief, not re-verified. It is consistent with the engine having no input.

EVIDENCE FIDELITY: what holds.
- The numbers for Schumann 2022, Petré 2021, Schöffl 2015, Grønhaug 2018, Quarmby 2023 and Gáspari 2024 match the verified pack.
- The no-interference point for SQ, BP and DL is honestly labelled as extrapolation. It is actually stronger than the aerobic meta-analyses alone suggest. Sporer's UVic thesis (2000; published as Sporer & Wenger 2003, JSCR 17(4):638-644; https://dspace.library.uvic.ca/items/d9c6f404-d74b-453e-99b0-816a42a46e45) found that interference after aerobic work lasted at most about 8 h and was limited to the muscles used: leg press reps fell, bench was unaffected.
- Climbing loads mainly the forearm flexors, elbow flexors, lats and shoulders, so a chronic effect on SQ is mechanistically implausible. At the sheet's 1-2 sessions a month, the dose is trivial.

EVIDENCE FIDELITY: problems.
(a) Weak proxy. Schumann and Petré studied running and cycling endurance training, mostly lower body. Climbing is intermittent, isometric, upper-body strength-endurance work, so these trials are a weak stand-in. The better basis is the localisation principle plus dose.
(b) The scope hides the one lift where interference is local. Climbing fatigue lands on PULL, and on DL grip in the hours after a session. Unmonitored climbing can therefore bias the PULL kg logic. It can cause false HOLD or FAIL calls, which void the window, and two contaminated consecutive FAILs would trigger -2.5 kg. "Leaving it out of the kg logic is defensible" is true for SQ, BP and DL. For PULL it carries a conservative, downward-biased cost that the claim mentions only through the 48 h rule.
(c) The "finger" overlap is overstated. In Schöffl, fingers are 52% of injuries, mostly pulley injuries from crimping on small holds. Bar pull-ups and OAP use a full-hand grip, so pulley strain is low. The tissues plausibly shared are the elbow flexors (brachialis, distal biceps), the common flexor-pronator origin (medial epicondyle) and the shoulder. Even that overlap is mechanistic inference, not observed data.
(d) "Real cost" asserts an additive harm the evidence does not show. The epidemiology is a clinic case series (Schöffl) and a retrospective survey (Grønhaug). It describes where climbers get injured, not whether extra pulling strength work adds risk. Quarmby rates evidence on training volume as conflicting and lists reduced finger strength as a risk factor. Its one RCT (Kozin 2022, n=84, a closed-chain, eccentric and PNF strength programme; https://physioquart.awf.wroc.pl/Use-of-closed-chain-exercises-eccentric-exercises-and-proprioceptive-muscle-facilitation,143832,0,2.html) found fewer moderate and severe elbow injuries with structured strength training. So heavy pulling could be neutral or even protective. The specific worry is OAP (high-intensity, unilateral, supinated), and that rests on coaching sources such as Antranik and Hooper.
(e) Gáspari is n=9 elite climbers after a competition. In that study tiredness normalised at 48 h and readiness at 60 h. The claim's "~48-60 h" merges these two markers. It is acceptable as a range but is the weakest possible anchor.

The core judgement survives once (b)-(d) are tightened. Climbing is a plausible but unquantified overuse exposure, concentrated at the elbow and shoulder and specifically in OAP. It is unmonitored by design. Its kg-logic effect is limited to PULL, where errors push loads down.

RECOMMENDATION. It changes zero protocol elements and violates no fixed constraint. A 0-10 pain score is not RIR. Logging fits the §19.4 memo practice.
- The next-morning pain check has indirect support from tendinopathy pain-monitoring models: Silbernagel 2007, AJSM 35(6):897-906 (https://search.pedro.org.au/search-results/record-detail/18389), and Breda 2021. Those are rehab populations, so using the check as a preventive monitor in an asymptomatic elbow is extrapolation.
- One caveat: "keep hard bouldering at least ~48 h before OAP days" should be worded as an addition to the existing §19.5.1(a) calendar rule (no climbing at all the day before or the day of OAP). Otherwise it could read as loosening that rule for easy climbing.
- The logging should also tag the PULL focus within 48 h case, since that is where climbing can actually distort the kg logic.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Climbing on separate days is unlikely to blunt SQ, BP or DL strength. This is extrapolated from aerobic concurrent-training data (Schumann 2022; Petré 2021). At the sheet's 1–2 sessions a month, any systemic effect is negligible, so leaving climbing out of the kg logic for those lifts is defensible.

Climbing has two costs that nothing monitors:
1. Cumulative overuse load on the elbow, finger and shoulder tissues that heavy PULL and OAP also load (climbing epidemiology: Schöffl 2015, Grønhaug 2018, Quarmby 2023).
2. Contamination of the PULL-focus and OAP judgment streams, which §19.4 itself names. A HOLD or FAIL caused by hard climbing can void a PULL window's increase or count toward OAP demotion. The §23.1 audit cannot tell that apart from too little stimulus or recovery.

The engine has no climbing input. It was removed in v1.2 (§3.2), and the decoder rejects climbing keys. Two manual rules stand in for it:
- §19.4: no PULL focus within 48 h after hard climbing.
- §19.5.1(a): no climbing on the calendar day before or the day of an OAP slot.

Both rules only protect the lifting session from climbing done before it. Neither limits climbing after heavy PULL or OAP, so following them does not cap elbow load, and adherence is not recorded. The spec already recommends a weekly elbow pain memo (§19.4) and pain-triggered reverts (§7.5, §19.5.1(d)). Both need a pain record that does not exist yet.

Recommendation (operational, outside the protocol): in the next session's workout_log notes, record each climbing day (date, bouldering or routes, hard or easy) and a next-morning elbow pain score (0–10). In addition to §19.5.1(a), keep hard bouldering at least ~48 h before OAP days (coaching-level evidence; Gáspari 2024, n=9).

I checked the claim against docs/ref5-program-spec.md and packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts.

What is correct:
- The engine has no climbing input. §3.2 forbids any prescription change based on climbing. The §28 v1.2 row records the removal. The engine keeps REF5_V12_REMOVED_STATE_KEYS ("climb", "climbing", "climbingWithin48h", "strongClimbing", "climbingReplacement") and containsRef5V12RemovedField, which reject those keys.
- Both manual rules are quoted correctly:
  - §19.4: after hard climbing, a PULL focus turn must not come within 48 h.
  - §19.5.1(a): no climbing the day before or the day of an OAP slot, described as "the same 48-hour discipline as heavy pull-ups".
  - The claim text calls both "§19.5 rules". Only the OAP rule is in §19.5; the PULL rule is in §19.4. The evidence line cites them correctly.
- Every cited source matches the verified pack: Schumann SMD -0.06, with the explosive-strength loss only within the same session; Petré -0.66 within a session vs -0.10 across sessions; Schöffl 17.2% shoulder, 9.1% forearm/elbow, 58% overstrain; Grønhaug 17.7% elbow; Quarmby's risk factors; Gáspari n=9, 24 h and 48–60 h.
- The claim correctly calls the no-interference point an extrapolation from aerobic data. At 1–2 climbing sessions a month (the constraints sheet), a systemic effect on SQ, BP or DL would be negligible whatever the evidence quality, so that conclusion holds.

What needs revising:
1. It leaves out a second cost that the spec itself names. §19.4's stated reason for the 48 h rule is judgment contamination: "a contaminated FAIL ... invalidates that window's increase". So climbing also confounds the PULL-focus and OAP outcome streams. A climbing-induced FAIL can count toward OAP demotion (2 consecutive FAILs). The early OAP FAILs on 9/07 and 9/12 cannot be checked against climbing, because none was logged. The user has pre-registered that a low success rate is read first as too little stimulus or recovery, and unlogged climbing confounds that audit (§23.1). So "the real cost is local overuse" is incomplete.
2. Both manual rules only run one way. They protect the lifting session from climbing done before it. Nothing limits climbing after a heavy PULL or OAP session. With about 2.9 sessions a week, the rules push climbing into the days right after lifting, which is the direction that piles up elbow load. Following the rules therefore does not cap elbow load, and checking adherence alone would not check the elbow ledger. The proposed log (climbing dates plus a next-morning pain score) does capture both directions.
3. The recommendation is mostly already in the spec. §19.4 already recommends a weekly elbow pain memo. §7.5 (revert if pain lasts 2 weeks or more) and §19.5.1(d) (review reverting after pain at 2 consecutive exposures) already depend on a pain record. The proposal makes those existing guidelines concrete; it is not new.

Recommendation check: it stays inside the fixed constraints. It changes no lift, accessory, test, RIR, AMRAP, deload or judgment criterion, and adds no engine branch, so it respects §3.2. Free text in workout_log.notes or tags (schema.ts) does not trip the removed-key decoder, which checks only state keys. In practice, climbing on non-program days would be written into the next lifting session's notes after the fact.

The 48 h bouldering item should be written as an addition to §19.5.1(a), not a replacement. Read as a replacement, it would loosen the rule from "any climbing" to "hard bouldering only", although it tightens calendar days to clock hours. Neither item is a protocol-element change, so the recommendation does not bundle several changes.

## C16 · exercise order · tradeoff · 확신 low · 변경 등급 observation-only

Squat first in every session, and focus 3x3 before volume work, is directly supported by exercise-order evidence. The cost: OAP sits third, after SQ and the BP-focus 3x3. OAP is now the #1 upper-body goal and a near-limit unilateral skill and lock-off task, and in that slot fatigue may blunt both performance and skill learning. The OAP-slot concern rests on extrapolation and coaching opinion, and 12 arm-exposures cannot separate slot fatigue from a strength shortfall (C23) or rung-definition noise (C18). The lenses converge on the same conditional single change. They DISAGREE on certainty (A medium, B low) and on framing: B frames it now as a §26 proposal, A keeps it observation-only.

**근거**

- Nunes JP et al. 2021, Eur J Sport Sci 21(2):149-157 (11 studies): multi-joint strength favoured performing the exercise first (ES 0.32); gains are largest for exercises at the start of a session. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32077380/
- ACSM 2026: strength is enhanced by placing the exercise at the beginning of the session. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41843416/
- Branscheidt et al. 2019, eLife 8:e40578: practising under fatigue impaired later motor-skill learning, partly through central mechanisms (lab finger task; the link to OAP is extrapolation). https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.40578
- Lopez-Rivera 2013 (coaching): lock-off training 'always at the beginning of the workout and after a good warm-up'. http://en-eva-lopez.blogspot.com/2013/05/lock-off-training-for-climbers-v-goal.html
- Internal record: 7 of 12 OAP arm-exposures were not PASS; net rung change after 5.7 weeks is -1 left, 0 right. Spec §7.3 order: SQ, BP focus, OAP, OHP.

**추론**

Squat-first rests on meta-analytic and position-stand evidence (high confidence for that part). The OAP-placement cost is low-certainty: there is no OAP-specific trial, and moving OAP earlier would push BP focus later, with a predictable BP cost under the same order evidence. Confidence is set to the lower rating because the contested part is the OAP slot.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 low · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: Exercise-order evidence supports squat first and focus 3x3 before volume work in principle (Nunes 2021: multi-joint vs single-joint order; ACSM 2026).
