Squat before bench or pull is an application of that principle, not a direct test. The cost to OAP of sitting third is probably small, because SQ and BP do not load OAP's prime movers. Antagonist pre-loading maintains or raises later reps (Zhang 2025 meta-analysis, SMD 0.68). Non-local fatigue has trivial effects on strength and power (Behm 2021). Branscheidt 2019 fatigued the practised effector itself, so it does not model this situation. The same evidence predicts that moving OAP ahead of BP would cost BP little and gain OAP little. The record does not suggest slot fatigue. There were 10 arm-exposures, not 12. Six were not PASS, and 4 of those were the rung-2 calibration FAILs that spec §19.5.6 intends. After calibration, 2 of 6 arm-exposures were not PASS (both left-arm HOLDs), and the right arm was promoted. The more plausible fatigue source, left out of the claim, is heavy PULL focus about 24 h before OAP (it happened twice). No change now. If OAP rung progress stalls after assisting-hand contact is standardised (C18), first compare outcomes against the PULL-to-OAP gap and the strength gap (C23). Slot order is the least-supported candidate cause.

VERDICT: the conclusion (no change now) holds. Several evidence statements are overstated, and the internal record is misreported.

1) "Directly supported" for squat-first is too strong.
- Nunes 2021 compared multi-joint-first against single-joint-first order, mostly on overlapping muscles: MJ ES 0.32, p=0.034; SJ ES -0.58; overall ES -0.11, p=0.306 (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32077380/).
- ACSM 2026 restates the general principle (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/41843416/).
- No study tested squat before bench or pull, which use different muscles. Squat-first is a sound application of the principle, not a direct test. The same applies to "focus before volume".

2) The OAP slot cost is weaker than framed. Neither SQ nor BP shares prime movers with OAP, and BP is the antagonist.
- Branscheidt 2019 fatigued the very pinch muscles used in the learned task, with a sustained MVC to a ~60% force drop. A low-force sequence task showed no fatigue effect (https://cdn.elifesciences.org/articles/40578/elife-40578-v2.pdf). It does not model fatigue from squat or bench before an elbow-flexor/lat skill.
- Lopez-Rivera's "beginning of the workout" advice comes with no stated reason beyond being rested. It sits inside lock-off strength sessions, not after leg or push work.
- More proximal counter-evidence, though still indirect for REF5's rests and loads:
  - Zhang, Weakley et al. 2025, Sports Med meta-analysis: agonist-antagonist supersets increased completed reps vs traditional sets (SMD 0.68, p=0.01) (https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-025-02176-8; https://digibug.ugr.es/handle/10481/102755).
  - Behm 2021, Sports Med 51:1893: non-local fatigue had trivial effects on strength (0.11) and power (-0.01). Only time-to-task-failure endurance was affected (-0.54), with no upper/lower moderator (https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-021-01456-3).
- So the BP-before-OAP cost is probably small. By the same evidence, the "predictable BP cost" of the swap is also probably near zero. The swap would therefore gain OAP little: the squat still precedes OAP, and BP is antagonist.

3) The internal record is misreported.
- The handoff lists 5 OAP sessions, which is 10 arm-exposures, not 12. Of those, 6 were not PASS, not 7.
- 4 of those 6 are the 9/07 and 9/12 rung-2 FAILs. Spec §19.5.6 pre-registers these as calibration: "첫 2회가 연속 FAIL이면 ... 1단으로 자동 재배치한다 — 그것이 의도다" ("if the first two are consecutive FAILs, it is automatically reassigned to rung 1 — that is the intent").
- After calibration, 2 of 6 arm-exposures were not PASS (left HOLD, HOLD; all others PASS), and the right arm was promoted.
- The "-1 left / 0 right" framing therefore counts by-design demotions as underperformance. OAP exposures spanned about 4 weeks (9/07 to 10/04), not 5.7.

4) The claim omits a confound. Twice, heavy PULL focus came about 24 h before OAP. That loads the same muscles and is a far more plausible fatigue source than within-session slot position. The evidence pack's OAP dimension already flags the gap-vs-FAIL check.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Squat first in every session (including MICRO) and focus before volume are supported by order evidence (Nunes 2021; ACSM 2026). OAP sits third on BP-focus days (SQ, BP focus 3x3, OAP, OHP; DB-confirmed). That costs it some systemic or central fatigue, but little fatigue in the muscles OAP uses, because neither SQ nor bench loads OAP's prime movers much. The expected benefit of moving OAP earlier, and the cost to BP of doing so, are both probably small and rest on extrapolation and coaching opinion. The record does not show a slot problem. Of 12 arm-exposures, 7 were not PASS, but 4 of those are the calibration FAILs at the default rung 2 that spec §19.5(6) designs in. After calibration there were 0 FAILs and 3 HOLDs in 8 exposures. Left is at rung 1 on a 2-PASS streak; right has been promoted to rung 2. These data cannot separate slot fatigue from strength shortfall, rung-definition noise, 24 h spacing after PULL sessions, or the 8-day gap. Recommendation: no change now. If there is still no net rung gain after about 8 standardized exposures per arm, counted from the post-calibration baseline, the single §26 candidate is SQ, OAP, BP focus, OHP. It should start at a BP-focus window boundary and accept a probably small BP cost.

How I checked: I read spec §7.2–7.6, §19.5, §21, §22.4, §23, §23.1 and §26, plus docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md. I also ran read-only SELECTs on workout_log and workout_set for user 6f47ec53.

WHAT HOLDS
(1) The session order is described correctly.
- Spec §7.2–7.4: SQ is first in normal PULL-focus, normal BP-focus and MICRO sessions.
- §7.3 and §7.5.1 fix the BP-focus order as SQ → BP focus 3x3 → OAP → OHP.
- The DB matches in all 15 sessions since 9/01: SQ is at sort_order 0, and OAP is at positions 2–3 after Bench in every OAP session.
(2) The "12 arm-exposures, 7 not PASS" count is correct. The DB has a 6th OAP session on 2026-10-14 22:01 KST (L 3,3; R 3,2) that the provided record summary leaves out (it describes an 8-day gap through 10/14).
- Non-PASS exposures: 9/07 L and R FAIL, 9/12 L and R FAIL, 9/21 L HOLD, 9/24 L HOLD, 10/14 R HOLD.
- Net rung change is −1 left and 0 right.
(3) The recommendation complies with the user's principles.
- It is observation-only now.
- The conditional candidate is one element, order. §26.2 lists 순서 (order) as a protocol change, so framing it as a §26 proposal is correct.
- Squat stays first, it stays within 5 lifts + OAP, and no judgment criterion is relaxed.
- Gating it after C18 is sequential, not bundled.

REVISIONS NEEDED
(a) The internal evidence is presented misleadingly. Four of the seven non-PASS exposures (9/07 and 9/12, both arms) are the calibration FAILs at the default start rung 2. Spec §19.5(6) says these are intended: two consecutive FAILs at the start rung automatically move the arm down to rung 1, "그것이 의도다" (that is the intent).
- After calibration (9/21 onward) there are 8 arm-exposures: 3 HOLD, 0 FAIL.
- Left at rung 1 went HOLD, HOLD, PASS, PASS, so it now has a 2-PASS streak. Right went PASS, PASS, PASS, was promoted, then had one HOLD at its new rung.
- Measured from the post-calibration baseline, net rung change is 0 left and +1 right. "−1 left" is the designed re-placement, not a regression. The trigger is therefore unlikely to fire soon, which supports "no change now".
(b) The fatigue mechanism is overstated for this slot.
- The lifts before OAP (SQ, and BP, which is a push and antagonist to OAP) load little of OAP's prime movers (lats, elbow flexors, forearm/grip).
- Nunes 2021 order effects (MJ vs SJ) and Branscheidt 2019 (fatigue of the same hand muscles used for the task) are largely about fatigue in the same muscles.
- Lopez-Rivera's advice comes from climbing sessions, which load the same tissues.
- What remains plausible is systemic or central fatigue and the time into the session. Both the OAP gain from reordering and the BP-focus cost are probably small.
(c) Missing internal context. The user's own v1.4 proposal (§1) blamed the previous 4-month OAP stall partly on doing it as a session finisher ("매 세션 마무리(피로 상태)", i.e. last in every session, in a fatigued state). v1.4 already moved OAP from last to third, so the remaining question is only third versus second.
(d) Confounds the claim does not list:
- Inter-session spacing: 9/24 L HOLD came about 24 h after the 9/23 heavy PULL focus; 9/12 came about 21 h after the 9/11 micro PULL 1x6.
- The 8-day gap before the 10/14 R HOLD, which was also R's first exposure at a new rung.
- Spacing does not explain the pattern either: 9/21 L HOLD came 6 days after a PULL session.
(e) Minor points:
- §21.1 sets the periodic review at three judgment windows per lift (about 9 weeks for SQ) and defines no OAP window. The "~8 exposures per arm" trigger is a new interpretive pre-registration that sits alongside the existing §23 OAP oscillation trigger.
- If the reorder is ever adopted, it should start at a BP-focus window boundary so it does not contaminate an open 4-exposure BP window.

## C17 · OAP · strength · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 none

The core design of the OAP slot is consistent with the evidence:
- a dedicated unilateral slot, because strength is task-specific and dynamic training transfers poorly to the isometric lock-off;
- independent per-arm state, because cross-education transfers only about a third of the trained-limb gain;
- complete isolation from kg progression;
- rule-based demotion;
- one-arm negatives gated behind rung 4 with 5-8 s lowering.
The 9/12 automatic demotion of both arms to rung 1 worked as the spec intended (§19.5.6). Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Kassiano et al. 2025, Sports Med 55:923-936: unilateral strength was favoured by unilateral training (ES -0.65). https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-024-02169-z
- Spitz et al. 2023: d 1.84 in the trained task vs 0.8 in untrained tests. https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-022-01790-0
- Saeterbakken et al. 2025: dynamic training transferred only SMD 0.42 to isometric strength. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12296774/
- Munn, Herbert & Gandevia 2004, J Appl Physiol 96:1861-1866: the contralateral limb gained +7.8%, ~35% of the trained-limb effect. https://journals.physiology.org/doi/full/10.1152/japplphysiol.00541.2003 . Manca et al. 2017, Eur J Appl Physiol 117(11):2335-2354: upper-limb cross-education +9.4% (high risk of bias). https://doi.org/10.1007/s00421-017-3720-z
- Roig et al. 2009, BJSM 43:556-568: the eccentric advantage appeared only at higher intensity and was mode-specific, which supports negatives as a gated add-on. https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/43/8/556
- Coaching: Hooper's Beta, 5 s one-arm lowers; dropping fast into extension strains the brachialis. https://www.hoopersbeta.com/library/one-arm-pull-up-progressions . Antranik 2018: rushed one-arm work is notorious for elbow pain. https://antranik.org/one-arm-pullup/
- Internal record: the 9/07 and 9/12 FAILs on both arms led to automatic demotion to rung 1.

**추론**

Specificity and cross-education evidence is direct, but it comes mostly from lower-limb or single-joint tasks, so transfer to elbow flexors and lats is extrapolation. No trial exists on one-arm pull-up acquisition, so the ladder mechanics rest on coaching practice.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: The OAP slot's core design is consistent with the evidence. Most of it is not derived from the evidence. (a) A dedicated one-arm slot is supported, by extrapolation, through the general task-specificity principle. That evidence is Kassiano 2025 (unilateral strength ES -0.65, 7 of 9 studies lower-limb), Spitz 2023 (d 1.84 vs 0.8, specificity of contraction mode and device) and Saeterbakken 2025 (dynamic training transfers SMD 0.42 to isometric strength). Saeterbakken does not argue for the slot: the slot is dynamic too, so it would also transfer only partly to an isometric lock-off. (b) Independent per-arm state is justified by measurement logic and the athlete's observed asymmetry at rung 1 (left HOLD, HOLD, PASS; right PASS, PASS, PASS). It is not justified by cross-education, because cross-education concerns an untrained limb and REF5 trains both arms. (c) Isolation from kg progression and rule-based demotion are design choices the evidence does not address and does not contradict. (d) Gating one-arm negatives behind rung 4 with 5-8 s lowering is coaching practice (Hooper's Beta, Low, JASC 2019, Antranik). Roig 2009 supports negatives as an add-on through mode specificity, but says nothing about the gate. (e) The 9/07 and 9/12 FAILs that moved both arms to rung 1 matched spec §19.5.6's stated intent exactly. Rung 2 was probably above capacity, given the earlier 4-month plateau there at 1-2 reps. Whether either FAIL exposure came about 24 h after heavy pulling was not checked.

The numbers in the claim check out, but most of the reasons given for each design element do not follow from the cited papers. The design itself holds; the evidence behind it is weaker and less direct than the claim says.

1. Kassiano 2025 (Sports Med 55:923-936). Confirmed: 9 studies; unilateral strength ES -0.65 (-0.93 to -0.37); bilateral strength ES 0.56; moderate risk of bias. The full-text table changes how much weight it can carry. Seven of the 9 studies trained the lower limb only, mostly back squat versus split squat. The one upper-limb study (Lee) compared dumbbell with barbell chest press and curls. The other (Janzen) used split-lever machines with untrained postmenopausal women. Most unilateral tests were the trained unilateral lift itself. So the paper shows test specificity in leg exercises. It does not show anything about a single-arm hang-and-pull, so applying it to the OAP is an extrapolation.

2. Spitz 2023. Confirmed: 12 studies; d 1.84 versus 0.8. The "untrained" tests were isometric or isokinetic tests on a different device. The paper is about specificity of contraction mode and device, not unilateral versus bilateral training. It supports the general specificity principle and nothing more.

3. Saeterbakken 2025 (Sports Med 55(7):1651-1676; checked through the LIDA index because PMC returned a CAPTCHA and Springer rate-limited the request). Confirmed: 43 studies; dynamic SMD 0.98; transfer to isometric SMD 0.42. The claim uses it the wrong way round. The OAP slot is itself dynamic training (2x3 reps), and G6 is a dynamic one-arm pull-up. Applied literally, this paper says the slot will also transfer only partly to any isometric lock-off. It supports mode specificity, not the slot. "Transfers poorly" is also too strong: the transfer is small but significant, about 43% of the task-specific effect. The v1.4 proposal itself names the bottleneck as asymmetric force transfer, elbow-tucked position and anti-rotation, not isometric lock-off strength.

4. Munn 2004 (+7.8%, 35.1% of the trained-limb effect) and Manca 2017 (upper limb +9.4%, high risk of bias). Both are confirmed, but they measure gains in an untrained contralateral limb. In REF5 both arms are trained. Cross-education therefore does not justify independent per-arm state. What does justify it is measurement logic (each rung is compared against the same arm's history) plus the observed asymmetry: at rung 1 the left arm went HOLD, HOLD, PASS while the right arm went PASS three times. Munn's pooled studies were also mostly untrained participants doing single-joint exercises.

5. Roig 2009 (20 RCTs). Confirmed: eccentric training beat concentric only at higher intensity, which the authors attribute to the higher loads, and the gains were more mode- and velocity-specific. The authors call for research on transfer to complex movements. Mode specificity supports negatives as an add-on rather than the main tool. Nothing in Roig supports gating them. If anything, its load-driven finding is the usual argument for why negatives are potent. The rung-4 gate and the 5-8 s lowering are coaching and safety choices: Hooper's Beta (at least 5 s, do not drop into extension), Antranik, Low (3-5 s progressing to 7-10 s) and the JASC 2019 model (5-10 s). These coaching sources gate negatives on a strength prerequisite, such as a 3RM at +60% BW (JASC) or +2/3 BW (DeMarco). The athlete's added load is roughly 20-47% BW. Rung 4 is a different gate, and no evidence says which gate is safer.

6. Isolation from kg progression and rule-based demotion. No evidence addresses either. "Consistent with the evidence" should read "not contradicted (evidence-silent design choices)". The spec's own §22.4 notes a cost of isolation: the PULL volume veto now fires rarely.

7. Internal record. Confirmed. Spec §19.5.6 says verbatim that if the first two exposures at the starting rung 2 are consecutive FAILs, the demotion rule moves the arm to rung 1, and "that is the intent". The 9/07 and 9/12 counts were 4, 3, 3 and 3 of 6 reps, all at least 2 short, so all FAIL; two consecutive FAILs trigger demotion. Rung 2 was probably beyond capacity. The proposal records a 4-month plateau at 1-2 reps at rung 2 before v1.4, and the left arm still logged HOLD twice at rung 1. One caveat I did not check: spec line 302 claims a 48 h separation between heavy pulling and OAP is "structurally guaranteed", which is false. If either FAIL session came about 24 h after a heavy PULL session, recovery is a possible confound. Given how far short the reps were, it is unlikely to change the conclusion.

8. "Both lenses agree" is not evidence.

Overall: the direct, peer-reviewed support is for the general task-specificity principle, mostly from lower-limb and machine data. Per-arm independence, the gate and tempo of negatives, isolation and the demotion rule rest on measurement logic, coaching practice and the athlete's own data, not on the cited trials. No trial of one-arm pull-up acquisition exists. The verdict "strength" with changeClass "none" is fine.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: The claim describes the OAP slot correctly as specified in §7.5 and implemented in ref5.ts. The slot is unilateral and sits only in slot 3 of BP-focus sessions. Each arm has its own state, and OAP has no effect on any kg standard, judgment window, stream, queue or forced-micro event. The ladder promotes after 3 consecutive PASS and demotes after 2 consecutive FAIL, with a floor at rung 1. Negatives (1×2, 5–8 s lowering) unlock at rung 4 and do not move the ladder.

Evidence supports two elements, by extrapolation: a dedicated unilateral slot (Kassiano 2025; Spitz 2023) and per-arm state (Munn 2004; Manca 2017, which has high risk of bias). The athlete's own left/right asymmetry from 9/21 to 10/04 supports per-arm state directly. The other elements are coaching or logic choices that no study tests: isolation from kg progression, the 3/2 promotion/demotion rule and the position-based rung-4 gate. The finding that dynamic training transfers poorly to isometric strength does not specifically support this dynamic-only slot.

The 9/12 demotion of both arms to rung 1 was the intended response under §19.5.6 to two FAILs during calibration. Later results (R promoted on 10/04, L at HOLD, HOLD, PASS) suggest rung 1 was the right level.

Three caveats apply:
- Assist force is not standardized, only hand position, so judgments at a given rung are noisy.
- The premise that 48 h between heavy PULL and OAP is "structurally guaranteed" (spec line 302) is false in the record: two gaps were about 24 h. Whether the demoting FAILs fell on those days is unchecked.
- The rung-4 negative gate is not tied to strength, so the athlete may reach it below the coaching strength thresholds for starting negatives.

Verdict: the design is a reasonable, mostly evidence-silent structure with two extrapolated evidential anchors. It is not an evidence-validated strength.

I checked the claim against docs/ref5-program-spec.md (§7.5.1–7.5.5 at lines 314–362, §19.5 at lines 761–770, line 302) and the engine (packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts, applyRef5OapOutcome at lines 2074–2146).

WHAT THE CLAIM GETS RIGHT ABOUT THE SPEC
- Dedicated slot: OAP takes slot 3 of normal BP-focus sessions only (§7.5.1).
- Per-arm state: "한 팔의 결과는 다른 팔의 어떤 상태도 바꾸지 않는다" — one arm's result never changes the other arm's state (line 338).
- Isolation from kg progression: complete (line 358). The engine function reads and writes only state.oap.
- Ladder rules: promote after 3 consecutive PASS, demote after 2 consecutive FAIL, floor at rung 1, HOLD breaks both streaks, INVALID is neutral. The code matches this exactly.
- Negatives: 1×2 per arm with a 5–8 s controlled lowering. They unlock on first reaching rung 4 by promotion and do not move the ladder (§7.5.3, line 346).
- The 9/12 demotion: §19.5.6 says outright that if the first two exposures at start rung 2 are both FAIL, the demotion rule moves the arm to rung 1, and that this is intended. The record matches the rules. On 9/07, L got 4/6 and R got 3/6; on 9/12, both got 3/6. Each is 2 or more reps short, so each is a FAIL.
- Later results fit the demotion being appropriate. At rung 1, R went PASS ×3 and was promoted on 10/04. L went HOLD, HOLD, PASS, so its streak is 1. The engine reproduces this.
- The same record gives direct evidence for per-arm independence: the left/right asymmetry. This is stronger support than the cross-education extrapolation.
- No recommendation is made (changeClass none), so no fixed constraint is touched.

NEEDED REVISIONS
(1) Only two of the five elements have literature support: the dedicated unilateral slot (Kassiano 2025, Spitz 2023) and per-arm state (Munn 2004, Manca 2017 — the latter at high risk of bias). That support is extrapolated from mostly lower-limb or single-joint data. The other three have no study behind them: isolation from kg progression, the 3-PASS/2-FAIL ladder rules and the rung-4 negative gate. They are bookkeeping or coaching choices. "Consistent with" is acceptable only if read as "not contradicted".

(2) The Saeterbakken argument (dynamic training transfers poorly to isometric strength) does not specifically back the slot as designed. The slot is itself dynamic 2×3 work with no isometric lock-off component. The finding supports general task specificity, not this prescription.

(3) The claim leaves out a measurement weakness in the evidence pack. §7.5.2 fixes where the assist hand goes, but not how hard it pulls. leverage.fit says assist is "hard to measure", and Sas-Nowosielski 2022 says the size of the relief is hard to control. So PASS/FAIL comparisons at a given rung are noisier than the rules assume.

(4) "Isolation" holds only in bookkeeping. The spec's premise that 48 h between heavy PULL and OAP is "structurally guaranteed" (line 302) is false in this athlete's record: twice the gap was about 24 h. Nobody has checked whether the 9/07 or 9/12 FAILs fell on those days. If they did, the demotion still "worked as intended" mechanically, but the FAILs may partly reflect residual fatigue rather than capacity. §19.5.6 also makes the first two exposures calibration sessions where form comes before reps.

(5) The rung-4 gate depends on ladder position, not strength. The coaching thresholds for starting one-arm negatives (JASC 2019: 3RM at +60% BW; DeMarco: about +2/3 BW) are unvalidated heuristics, and the athlete (~+47% BW at 1RM) currently sits below them. The spec also does not say how to control the bottom of the lowering, which Hooper and Antranik flag as the elbow-risk point.

(6) Rung 1 is the floor and nothing easier exists below it. If an arm FAILs repeatedly at rung 1 (L is borderline there), the rule only resets the counter. This is a design gap, not a refutation.

"Both lenses agree" cannot be checked from here. The DB was not reachable (no .env.local), so the session data could not be re-queried.

## C18 · OAP · weakness · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 operational

Measurement is the OAP slot's weak point:
- The rungs are ordinal and the assisting-hand force is uncontrolled.
- The default start (rung 2) was too hard: 4 FAILs in the first 4 arm-exposures.
- After 5.7 weeks the net change is -1 rung on the left and 0 on the right, and 7 of 12 arm-exposures were not PASS.
- The 9/21 left-arm memo blames a HOLD on the awkward rung-1 grip ('may have had strength left').
- Exposure runs at ~1.05 per week, against the spec's ~1.5.
The §19.5c '≥2 weeks per rung' guideline has no compliant way to be enforced: promotion can happen within ~8 days, and neither INVALID nor revert is a compliant way to hold a rung. The lenses differ on how much that matters: A treats it as a gap; B notes that at ~1 exposure a week the dwell is usually met by accident.

**근거**

- Internal record, left arm: 9/07 r2 FAIL, 9/12 r2 FAIL (to r1), 9/21 HOLD (grip memo), 9/24 HOLD, 10/04 PASS, 10/14 PASS. Right arm: 9/07 and 9/12 FAIL (to r1); 9/21, 9/24 and 10/04 PASS (to r2); 10/14 HOLD.
- Spec §22.4.11 (ordinal scale) and §19.5.1(c) (2-week dwell, not enforced).
- Coaching: leverage.fit says assistance 'is hard to measure'; Brookshire 2013 and Hybrid Calisthenics found positions past the elbow awkward. https://www.hybridcalisthenics.com/advanced-onehand-pullups
- Bohm et al. 2015, Sports Med Open 1:7: tendon adaptation was studied only in interventions of ≥8 weeks, so 2 weeks is untested as an adaptation window. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-015-0009-9

**추론**

The record facts are direct. That assist force and grip ergonomics, not strength, drive part of the variance is an inference supported by the memo and by coaching sources. This matters because demotion follows 2 FAILs, so measurement noise can produce FAIL/HOLD streaks unrelated to strength. Five or six exposures per arm cannot separate insufficient stimulus from insufficient recovery.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 low · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: The OAP ladder has a structural measurement limitation, which the spec acknowledges in §22.4.11: rungs are ordinal, and self-assist force is uncontrolled, so PASS depends partly on how much the athlete holds back the assisting hand. The current record does not show this limitation distorting outcomes. The opening FAIL, FAIL at rung 2 on both arms is the calibration and automatic demotion the spec designed (§19.5.6). After that both arms improve monotonically, and the right arm went from 3/6 to at least 5/6 at rung 2. The 10/14 exposures and the 9/21 grip memo still need checking against the DB. The §19.5.1(c) two-week dwell is internally inconsistent: honest performance at the rung cannot avoid promotion. Revert and user-chosen micro can legitimately skip exposures, but no rule ties them to the dwell. At ~1 exposure a week the dwell has so far been met incidentally. Any change to contact mode (e.g. open palm) is a rung-definition change and belongs in a §26 proposal. Adding a per-exposure deviation or assist tag is measurement-only.

What holds. The rung scale is ordinal and the assisting-hand force is uncontrolled by design. The spec admits this in §22.4.11 (/home/claude/workout-log/docs/ref5-program-spec.md:877). With self-assist the free hand can always add force, so PASS depends partly on the athlete holding back. Hybrid Calisthenics supports that assist force varies: "If your supporting hand leaves bruises on your forearm, you may be supporting too much." Bohm 2015 is cited accurately (fetched): it required interventions of at least 8 weeks and covered only Achilles and patellar tendons. So 2 weeks is untested, though applying any of it to elbow tendons is an extrapolation. The ~8-day promotion arithmetic checks against §8 density rules: normal sessions can fall on days 0, 1, 3, 7 and 8, which with alternation gives BP-focus days 0, 3 and 8. The §23 oscillation trigger exists (line 895).

What fails or is overstated.
(1) The 4 opening FAILs are not evidence of a measurement defect. §19.5.6 (line 770) makes the first 2 exposures calibration (form over completion). It states that if both are FAIL at rung 2, the automatic move to rung 1 is "the intent". That is designed behaviour. A recovery confound, the ~24 h PULL-to-OAP gaps, was also never ruled out.
(2) The record does not show measurement noise. Both arms improve monotonically. Left: F, F, H, H, P, P. Right: F, F, P, P, P, then HOLD after promotion. On the right arm at rung 2, reps rose from 3/6 (9/07, 9/12) to at least 5/6, so "net 0 rungs" hides real gain the ladder did detect. Without the 4 calibration exposures, only 3 of 8 are not PASS.
(3) The 10/14 exposures (L PASS, R HOLD) are not in the verified record, which ends OAP at 10/04 and then an 8-day gap. "7 of 12", "net -1/0" and "~1.05/wk" all depend on them. Without them the figures are 6 of 10 and ~0.88/wk. I could not check the DB (read access was denied), so the 9/21 memo quote is also unverified.
(4) The spec's ~1.5/wk is stated for 3 sessions/week (line 302). The realized frequency, gap included, was lower, so the comparison is not like for like.
(5) Coaching citations are misattributed or off-target. The fetched Hybrid Calisthenics page never mentions positions past the elbow; only Brookshire does. Past-the-elbow awkwardness concerns rungs 4 and up, not the rung-1 wrist grip in the memo. The leverage.fit "hard to measure" quote is, per the evidence pack, about finger-assisted reps, not hand-on-arm.
(6) "Neither INVALID nor revert is a compliant way to hold a rung" is overstated. Line 24 says revert can be used anytime, and its §7.6 conditions are only recommended. §7.5.5 says revert preserves ladder state. Choosing micro for any reason (line 375) also legitimately skips an exposure. The narrower true point: §19.5.1(c)'s own instruction (do the exposure at that rung but don't meet the promotion condition) cannot be followed without sandbagging, which §11 forbids.

The recommendation is not OK. Fixed landmarks are already mandatory under §7.5.2, with EXTERNAL→INVALID for deviation. "Open palm, no gripping" contradicts rung 1's mandatory definition ("감쌈", wrap around; line 331). Per Hybrid Calisthenics, softening to a flat palm is a difficulty progression. Imposing it would make every rung harder, change the stimulus and break comparability with existing exposures. That is a protocol change mislabelled as operational. The "support only as needed" cue also leaves assist force as the free variable, so it does not fix the stated problem. A compliant measurement-only alternative: keep the rung definitions and log, per arm per exposure, any contact deviation or an "assist felt heavy" tag.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 low · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: The OAP ladder controls the assisting hand's position but not its force, and its rungs are ordinal (spec §7.5.2, §22.4.11, both already acknowledged). The record has 12 arm-exposures.
- Four were the designed §19.5.6 calibration FAILs, which moved both arms from rung 2 to rung 1 as intended.
- After calibration, 3 of 8 exposures were HOLDs and none were FAILs. Two came on the first exposure at a new rung: left 9/21 on r1, with a memo blaming the awkward rung-1 grip, and right 10/14 on r2. One came about 24 h after heavy pulling (left 9/24).
- Current state: left r1 with 2 of 3 PASS toward promotion; right r2.
This fits familiarisation and recovery noise. It does not yet show measurement noise driving demotions, and six exposures per arm cannot separate the causes.

Exposure at ~1.05/wk reflects attendance (about 2.1 normal sessions/wk), not slot design.

The §19.5.1(c) two-week dwell is met by structure. The engine's density valve (≥3 normal starts in the previous six calendar days forces MICRO) puts at least 14 calendar days between reaching a rung and the next possible promotion, unless two normal sessions are done on the same day. Only the guideline's wording is incoherent.

Measurement-only response, with no change to the ladder: log the actual contact per arm and per exposure, plus a first-exposure-on-rung flag, and read HOLDs at the first exposure on a new rung with that flag in mind. Any standardisation of how the hand makes contact would change the §7.5.2 rung definitions. Rung 1 is defined as a wrap, so it must go through §26 as a single change, take effect only at a rung boundary, and specify a constant contact rather than "as needed".

I checked docs/ref5-program-spec.md (§7.5.2–7.5.5, §7.6, §8, §19.5, §22.3–22.4, §23), the density rule in packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts:1154-1183, and the prod DB with read-only SELECTs (Dev user, plan 'REF5 Adaptive Strength 프로그램 2').

What holds:
1. The record facts are correct. The brief is out of date: a 10/14 session does exist.
   - Left: 9/07 r2 4/6 FAIL, 9/12 r2 3/6 FAIL, demoted to r1. Then 9/21 5/6 HOLD, 9/24 5/6 HOLD, 10/04 PASS, 10/14 PASS. Runtime state: r1, passStreak 2.
   - Right: 9/07 3/6 FAIL, 9/12 3/6 FAIL, demoted to r1. Then 9/21, 9/24 and 10/04 PASS, promoted to r2. 10/14 r2 5/6 HOLD.
   - So 7 of 12 arm-exposures were not PASS, net change is -1 left and 0 right, and exposure rate is 6/5.7 wk ≈ 1.05/wk.
   - The 9/21 left memo is verbatim: "1단 잡는 자세가 뭔가 왼쪽에서 많이 어색했다. 힘은 남았을지도" ("the rung-1 grip posture felt very awkward on the left; I may have had strength left").
2. The ladder fixes hand position, not assist force (§7.5.2), and the scale is ordinal (§22.4.11). Both are true, and the spec already acknowledges both.

What does not hold or is overstated:
1. "Default start too hard" / "4 FAILs" ignores §19.5.6. The spec designates the first 2 exposures as calibration and calls the auto-demotion from start rung 2 after 2 consecutive FAILs "the intent". So 4 of the 7 non-PASS exposures were the designed calibration.
   - After calibration, 3 of 8 exposures were non-PASS. All three were HOLDs; there were no FAILs.
   - Two of those HOLDs were the first exposure on a new rung: left 9/21 on r1 (the memo) and right 10/14 on r2. That points to familiarisation.
   - The third, 9/24 left, came about 24 h after heavy PULL focus on 9/23. That points to recovery.
   - Assist-force noise is therefore only one of three candidate causes, and no noise-driven demotion has occurred.
2. The ~1.05/wk vs ~1.5/wk comparison is not a slot-design gap. The spec's 1.5 assumes 3×/wk. The actual record was 12 normal and 2 micro sessions in 5.7 weeks, and BP-focus is exactly every other normal session (6/12).
3. The §19.5c "gap" is refuted.
   - The engine makes a session MICRO if there are ≥3 normal starts in the previous six calendar days (ref5.ts:1170-1176). That caps normal sessions at 3 in any 7-day span.
   - I brute-forced every 22-day schedule with at most one session per day. The minimum from the exposure that reaches a rung to the next possible promotion is 14 calendar days.
   - The "~8 days" figure is only first-to-third exposure on a rung, which applies from plan start, not from reaching a rung.
   - So the 2-week dwell is met by the program's structure, not by accident. The one exception is two normal sessions on the same calendar day, because the engine excludes today from the six-day count.
   - Revert is also a compliant way to hold a rung: §7.6 makes it a user decision the engine does not judge, and §7.5.5 preserves ladder state. Choosing MICRO manually (§8.1) also works.
   - What remains is a wording problem: §19.5.1(c) is self-contradictory, but the guideline never actually binds.

Recommendation fails:
1. It is labelled "operational", but it is a protocol change.
   - The fixed landmark is already mandatory (§7.5.2: an off-definition exposure is closed EXTERNAL and counted INVALID).
   - Rung 1 is defined as "손목 주름 바로 아래를 감쌈" ("wrap just below the wrist crease"), which is a grip. "Open palm, no gripping" contradicts the definition of the rung the left arm is on now, 2/3 of the way to promotion.
   - The ladder's position definitions are listed in §22.3 as protocol hypotheses. Changing them is a §26 change. This also contradicts the recommendation's own clause that a rung-definition refinement should wait for the §23 oscillation trigger.
2. Contact mode changes difficulty. Coaching sources in the evidence pack (Hybrid Calisthenics) use softening the grip to a flat palm as a progression. Switching mid-streak would mix stimuli inside the left arm's current streak and the right arm's rung-2 baseline, and would create a new familiarisation episode, which is the very noise source the claim cites.
3. The "support only as needed" cue lets assist force float to whatever is needed to finish the rep. That biases outcomes toward PASS and weakens what PASS/HOLD/FAIL can tell you. In effect this loosens the judgment, against the user's pre-registered rule.

A compliant alternative is logging only: record, per arm per exposure, the contact actually used and a "first exposure on this rung" flag, and leave the ladder unchanged. Any contact rule should be a single §26 candidate that takes effect at a rung boundary and specifies a constant contact, not "as needed".

## C19 · OAP · tradeoff · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 doc-or-display

The v1.4 swap removed little pulling stimulus but added high-tension elbow-flexor load. The replaced PULL volume 2x6 was always bodyweight +0 kg (~68% of the pull total, roughly 8-10 RIR), whereas OAP sets are near-limit unilateral pulls. The PULL volume veto now rests only on the micro's bodyweight x6, so it is effectively inert, and in practice PULL judgment reduces to FAIL 0 / HOLD ≤1 on focus exposures. More generally, the volume vetoes are inert at current loads. That is a description only, not a proposal to change the criteria. Spec §22.4.9 frames v1.4 mainly as a loss of PULL volume; the records suggest the risk to watch is elbow load. Lens A raised this. Lens B concurs that the removed sets carried little stimulus, and separately lists the PULL set reduction as a minor headwind for G6 (C23).

**근거**

- Internal record: every PULL volume prescription has been bodyweight +0, because the target total (70-75) is at or below the logged bodyweight of 75. OAP: 7 of 12 arm-exposures were HOLD or FAIL, indicating near-limit effort.
- Nuzzo et al. 2024: about 15 reps to failure at 70% 1RM, consistent with bodyweight x6 being far from failure. https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10933212/
- Coaching: Antranik 2018 calls one-arm pull-ups and chin-ups 'NOTORIOUS for causing elbow pain if the exercise is rushed through'. https://antranik.org/one-arm-pullup/ . DeMarco reports elbow tendonitis on a rushed one-arm chin attempt (n=1). https://divergentstrengthacademy.com/blog/one-arm-chinups
- Gronhaug 2018: elbows were 17.7% of chronic climbing injuries, which adds to the same tissue load. https://bmjopensem.bmj.com/content/4/1/e000406

**추론**

Inference from the records: no trial measures OAP tissue load. The RIR estimate for the removed sets uses population means. Calling the veto inert is a description of current behaviour and does not touch the user's criteria. Note that the 'below logged bodyweight' fact depends on the default bodyweight value (C11).

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: At current loads and the logged 75 kg default bodyweight, every PULL volume prescription (micro 1×6, revert 2×6) has been bodyweight +0. That is about 68% of the historical 110 kg total, and population rep data (Nuzzo 2024: main model and bench, no pull-up data, SD about 4 reps) put it well short of failure. The PULL volume veto is therefore close to inert: its inputs are PULL_VOLUME_MICRO and revert sessions only. The SQ V and BP volume vetoes are similarly near-inert. PULL judgment now in practice reduces to FAIL 0 / HOLD ≤1 on focus exposures. This is description, not a proposal to change the criteria. It stops being true once focus reaches 92.5 (volume becomes +2.5) or when real bodyweight is logged (C11).

The OAP slot that replaced the normal 2×6 has produced HOLD or FAIL on 6 of 10 arm-exposures. That indicates near-limit unilateral efforts, so v1.4 plausibly added high-tension elbow-flexor loading. This is unmeasured inference plus coaching opinion; no study measures OAP tissue load.

The strength value of the removed bodyweight sets is uncertain, not shown to be negligible. Strength appears insensitive to proximity to failure (Robinson 2024/2025), and set-count models (Pelland) would count them, so the 5→3 weekly direct PULL sets at 2 sessions/week may still slow weighted pull-up progress.

Documentation-only revision: keep §22.4.9's volume-loss statement and add that v1.4 also adds near-limit unilateral elbow-flexor loading, tying it to the existing §19.5 elbow protocol and the §23 revert trigger. Both risks are watched; neither replaces the other.

The descriptive core is right: the PULL volume veto is close to inert. The "little stimulus" part goes further than the evidence, the OAP count is wrong, and the doc recommendation would delete a risk the evidence still supports.

WHAT HOLDS (checked in the engine and the spec)
(1) Bodyweight +0 kg. In packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts, line 334 sets the volume target to floor2.5(focus × 75/87.5), and line 1273 sets added load to nearest2.5(max(0, target − logged bodyweight)). At focus totals up to 90 the target is at most 75, so at the logged 75 kg every PULL volume prescription has been bodyweight +0. That covers both the micro 1×6 and a revert-session 2×6.
(2) The veto inputs are as described. Spec §13.2 confirms the only veto inputs are PULL_VOLUME_MICRO plus a revert session's PULL_VOLUME_NORMAL. The claim leaves out the revert path, which is a minor omission. Spec §22.4.10 already concedes that the veto rarely fires.
(3) Far from failure. Nuzzo 2024 (PMC10933212 is the correct paper) gives about 15 reps at 70% in the main model and 14.1 at 70% for bench, so bodyweight ×6 at about 68% is far from failure. FAIL (2 or more reps short) is implausible short of illness or injury. The SQ V and BP volume vetoes are similarly near-inert at current loads.

WHAT NEEDS REVISION
(a) The OAP count. The record as supplied lists 5 OAP sessions, which is 10 arm-exposures. Of those, 6 were HOLD or FAIL, not 7 of 12, unless there is an exposure the record does not list.
(b) "Always +0" depends on current state. When focus reaches 92.5, the target becomes 77.5 and the prescription becomes +2.5. A measured bodyweight below about 73.75 kg also gives +2.5 (C11). "Inert" therefore means inert at current loads and the default bodyweight, not structurally inert.
(c) The 8–10 RIR figure is extrapolated. Nuzzo has no pull-up or pulldown data; the authors list the lat pulldown as data-poor. The figure rests on the old 110 kg 1RM total, and the between-person SD is about 4 reps at 60–70%. It is a population point estimate with a wide range.
(d) "Removed little stimulus" is contested for strength. Robinson 2024 (load-adjusted) found strength gains insensitive to RIR. Robinson 2025 found that squat sets actually ending around 8 RIR still gained about 13.8 kg. Swinton 2024 shows the strength-load curve flattening above about 65%. Pelland's fractional counting does not discount sets for distance from failure; in the preprint, extra weekly sets give detectable gains up to about 4 sets. At 2 sessions/week, v1.4 cuts direct PULL sets from 5 to 3, which crosses that range. Two things favour the claim: the removed sets were below the >80% load that maximises strength (Currier 2023), and there are few data on sets ending at 8 RIR or more. The removed block was therefore probably a smaller strength stimulus per set, but it has not been shown to be negligible. The "small" reading is better supported for hypertrophy (Robinson 2024 hypertrophy slope).
(e) "High-tension elbow-flexor load" is inference plus coaching opinion. No study measures OAP tissue load; my search found only bilateral chin-up EMG studies. The assist force is unknown. Antranik's warning and DeMarco's tendonitis (from towel-method work begun at only 15 bodyweight chins) are coaching opinion and n=1. Gronhaug 2018 (elbows 17.7% of chronic injuries) describes climbing, not OAP. The DB records contain no elbow-symptom data, so "the records suggest elbow risk" really rests on near-limit OAP outcomes plus the roughly 24 h PULL-to-OAP spacing.
(f) The spec already frames the trade this way. Spec §19.3 (line 737) already calls v1.4 low-intensity volume exchanged for "high-tension unilateral pull stimulus". §19.5 already has an elbow protocol, and §23 already makes a second elbow-pain revert a review trigger. Only §22.4.9 frames v1.4 mainly as volume loss.

WHY THE RECOMMENDATION IS NOT OK AS WRITTEN
It is doc-only and changes one element, but "elbow monitoring, not PULL volume loss" would remove §22.4.9's statement that weighted pull-up progress may slow. Point (d) does not support dropping that statement, and it conflicts with the claim's own note that Lens B (C23) lists it as a headwind. An additive reframe would be acceptable.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: In the record so far, the PULL volume 2×6 that v1.4 replaced was always prescribed at bodyweight +0. The target was 70–75 kg against a logged, probably default, 75 kg, which is about 68% of the 110 kg measured pull total and several reps from failure. This is not structural: the next PULL increase (focus 92.5) makes it +2.5, as would a real calc bodyweight of 73.75 kg or less.

The PULL volume veto now draws only on the micro's bodyweight 1×6, plus any revert session. That makes it practically inert, so PULL judgment in practice reduces to FAIL 0 / HOLD ≤1 on focus exposures. The v1.3 2×6 bodyweight veto was equally near-inert, so the extra leniency the spec lists in §22.4.10 is about nil at current loads. The SQ V and BP volume vetoes are rarely-firing tripwires, not inert. This is a description only, not a proposal to change the criteria.

The spec already frames the swap as trading low-intensity lat volume for high-tension unilateral pulling (§19.3, §22.4.9) and already treats the elbow as the watched risk (§19.4, §19.5, §7.6, §23). v1.4 also absorbed a 4-month, pain-free, every-session assisted-OAP finisher and dropped finisher dips. So against the athlete's actual training, the net change in elbow-flexor load is a restructuring of unknown direction, not a clear addition.

The records' clearest elbow-relevant point is that heavy PULL and OAP fell about 24 h apart twice. That contradicts §7.3's claim that 48 h is "structurally guaranteed".

OAP outcomes: 6 of 10 arm-exposures were HOLD or FAIL. Four of those were the two designated calibration exposures at rung 2. This is consistent with near-limit sets for the left arm and at rung 2.

The only documentation gap is that §22.4 has no elbow-tissue item. That item belongs alongside the PULL-volume item, not in place of it, because PULL strength still matters for OAP readiness.

The core description is right. The framing, one count, and the "added load" premise need fixing.

What holds (checked against the spec and engine):
- **Removed sets were bodyweight +0.** The PULL volume target is floor2.5(focus total × 75/87.5) and added load is nearest2.5(max(0, target − calc BW)) (spec §6.2, §10.2; ref5.ts:334, :1273).
  - At every focus total so far (82.5, 85, 87.5, 90) the target was 70–75 kg. At the logged 75 kg that gives +0, so every PULL volume prescription has been a bodyweight set.
  - This is historical, not structural. The next PULL increase (92.5) gives a 77.5 target, so +2.5. Any calc BW ≤ 73.75 would already give +2.5. The "+0" fact therefore depends on the default bodyweight entry (C11), as the claim says.
- **About 68% of the pull total.** 75/110 = 68% of the measured 1RM total. Several RIR is plausible from Nuzzo 2024 (~15 reps at 70%), but that model has no pull-up data, so this is extrapolation.
- **PULL veto is practically inert.** Spec §7.4 (line 312) and §13.2 (line 557) confirm the only volume inputs are PULL_VOLUME_MICRO plus a reverted session's PULL_VOLUME_NORMAL. The claim omits the revert path (§7.6). A FAIL on bodyweight 1×6 means 4 or fewer bodyweight pull-ups, so the veto barely fires.
  - Unstated in the claim: the v1.3 2×6 bodyweight veto was equally near-inert (FAIL needed a total deficit of 2 or more across 12 easy reps). So the "judgment somewhat more lenient" cost the spec lists (§22.4.10) is about nil at these loads. That supports the claim.
- **Other volume vetoes are overstated as "inert".** SQ V (3×5 at about 70% of estimated 1RM) and BP volume (2×5 at about 70%) are fixed ratios of the focus or hard loads, sitting at several RIR. They are rarely-firing tripwires, not inert.

What needs revision:
1. **The spec already frames v1.4 this way.** The claim says §22.4.9 treats v1.4 mainly as a PULL volume loss. But §22.4.9 (line 875) already calls the removed sets "low-intensity lat hypertrophy volume". §19.3 (line 737) already says "low-intensity lat volume decreases and high-tension unilateral pulling stimulus replaces it". Elbow load is already the watched risk in several places:
   - §19.5 (line 763): OAP has high elbow (biceps tendon, brachialis) and rotator-cuff injury rates, and tendon adapts slower than muscle.
   - Elbow protocol §19.5(a)–(d), monitoring note §19.4 (line 743), revert trigger §7.6 (line 369), structure-review trigger §23 (line 896).
   - The proposed reframe is therefore mostly already present. The only real gap is that §22.4 has no elbow item.
2. **"Added" elbow load is not established against actual training.** The OAP proposal (docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md lines 24, 97) and spec §19.5.2 show v1.4 absorbed a 4-month every-session finisher of assisted OAP (1–2 reps at rung 2), with "no double exposure". It also removed finisher dips. The 4 months were pain-free (proposal line 26).
   - Against the v1.3 protocol, OAP is added. Against what the athlete was actually doing, it is a restructuring: more reps per exposure (2×3 per arm), about 1.5 exposures/week, done fresher. Since demotion it has mostly been at the most-assisted rung. The net change in elbow-flexor tension is unknown.
3. **The OAP count is off.** The record summary gives 5 OAP sessions (9/07, 9/12, 9/21, 9/24, 10/04), which is 10 arm-exposures with 6 HOLD/FAIL, not "7 of 12". I could not check the DB for a sixth exposure.
   - 4 of those 6 were the two exposures that §19.5.6 designates calibration, where form came before completion and auto-demotion was intended.
   - At rung 1 the record is 4 PASS and 2 HOLD (left arm). "Near-limit" holds for the left arm and for rung-2 work. It is weaker as a general description.
4. **The strongest records-based elbow point is missing.** Spec §7.3 (line 302) says 48 h between heavy PULL and OAP is "structurally guaranteed". The micro rules (§8) do not enforce that, and the records show two gaps of about 24 h. The claim cites the gaps in its context but its recommendation does not touch this false spec sentence.

Recommendation: it is doc-only and within the fixed constraints, but as worded it is partly unsupported:
- It rests on a mischaracterisation of the spec, and largely duplicates §19.3/§19.5.
- "More high-tension elbow-flexor loading" ignores the absorbed finisher.
- "Not PULL volume loss" drops a watched risk without support. The proposal (line 23) calls weighted-pull strength borderline for OAP readiness, and Lens B calls the set reduction a G6 headwind.

A narrower doc-only version would be fine: add an elbow-tissue item to §22.4 alongside item 9, not replacing it.

Evidence: Antranik and DeMarco are coaching sources (DeMarco is n=1, posted 2012, updated 2018). Gronhaug 2018 (elbow 17.7%) is confirmed. None measures OAP tissue load.

## C20 · OAP · weakness · 확신 high · 변경 등급 doc-or-display

Spec §7.3 (line 302) says 48 h separation between heavy pull-ups and OAP is 'structurally guaranteed' (구조적으로 보장된다). It is not. The engine only guarantees that they fall in different sessions. Because focus alternates every normal session, any two normal sessions less than 48 h apart pair a heavy PULL day with an OAP day. In the records:
- 3 of 11 heavy-PULL/OAP adjacencies were under 48 h (23, 44 and 24 h);
- micro bodyweight pull-ups fell ~21-24 h from OAP twice;
- 7 of 28 inter-session gaps were 1 day.
The documentation error is certain. The physiological case for 48 h rather than ~24 h is weak. Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Spec line 302 (verified): '헤비 풀업(PULL 집중)이 없는 날에만 OAP가 오므로 두 고장력 당기기 사이의 48시간 분리는 구조적으로 보장된다' ('OAP comes only on days without heavy pull, so 48 h separation between the two high-tension pulls is structurally guaranteed'). §12: focus alternates on every valid normal session.
- Internal record: 9/07 OAP to 9/08 PULL focus (23 h); 9/21 OAP to 9/23 PULL (44 h); 9/23 PULL to 9/24 OAP (24 h).
- Magnusson, Langberg & Kjaer 2010, Nat Rev Rheumatol 6:262-268: net collagen loss for ~18-36 h after loading, then net synthesis; the key point calls 24 h of rest 'sufficient'. Data are from lower-limb tendons after running. https://www.nature.com/articles/nrrheum.2010.43
- Lopez-Rivera 2013 (coaching): rest 24-72 h between lock-off sessions, and stop if the elbow hurts the next day. http://en-eva-lopez.blogspot.com/2013/05/lock-off-training-for-climbers-v-goal.html
- Bohm et al. 2015: tendon stiffness adapts to high-strain loading (SMD 0.90) in interventions of ≥8 weeks; there are no data on short windows or elbow tendons. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-015-0009-9

**추론**

The factual error is a logic and record fact (high confidence). The physiological consequence is uncertain: all tendon timing data are lower-limb or in vitro, the cited sources accept 24 h, no study covers weighted pull-up or OAP recovery, and no performance effect is visible at n=11. So the documentation fix is certain, while the case for an engine gate is low to moderate.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Spec §7.3 (line 302) says the 48 h separation between heavy pull-ups and OAP is "structurally guaranteed". That is false: the engine (ref5.ts) only guarantees that they fall in different sessions. The OAP slot is gated solely by NORMAL && nextFocus==="BP", and the density valve allows normal sessions on consecutive days. So any two normal sessions less than 48 h apart pair a heavy PULL day with an OAP day, in either order. The internal record reportedly shows three such pairs (23 h, 44 h, 24 h). Two of them are OAP→PULL and one is PULL→OAP. These were not re-verified against the DB. The same false premise appears in §19.5(a) (line 765). Physiologically, the evidence does not discriminate between ~24 h and 48 h for elbow-flexor/OAP loading. The tendon timing data come from lower-limb running or kicking. They come either from peritendinous microdialysis with no 24-48 h samples (Langberg 1999) or from tendon synthesis without degradation measures (Miller 2005). Magnusson 2010's "(24 h)" is a parenthetical whose own described time course puts 24 h inside the net-loss window. Neither the spec's 48 h nor a "24 h is enough" reading is evidence-based. Fix: a documentation correction at lines 302 and 765 (different sessions, not 48 h; spacing is user discipline per §19.5). Any engine gate is a low-certainty single §26 candidate. The proposed PULL→OAP revert gate would cover only one direction, which is 1 of the 3 recorded cases.

FACTUAL CORE: confirmed, and the code alone is enough to establish it.
- docs/ref5-program-spec.md:302 contains the sentence exactly as quoted.
- §12 (line 515ff) alternates focus after every valid normal session.
- In the engine (packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts), the OAP slot is gated only by `carriesOapSlot = sessionType === "NORMAL" && state.nextFocus === "BP"` (~line 1587). No elapsed-time check exists between a PULL-focus start and an OAP session. The only 48 h gate in the engine is the SQ hard gate (line 1198).
- `decideRef5SessionType` (lines 1154-1183) makes micro mandatory only when BOTH previous calendar days were program days, or when there were 3 or more normals in the prior 6 days. So a PULL-focus normal followed by a BP/OAP normal about 24 h later is legal by design.
- The "structurally guaranteed" sentence is therefore false regardless of the training log. The same false premise appears at spec line 765 (§19.5(a), "헤비 풀업과 같은 48시간 규율", i.e. "the same 48-hour discipline as heavy pull-ups") and in the non-normative docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md:34. Both need the same correction.

RECORD FIGURES: not independently verifiable here. There is no DB access: no web/.env.local, and the claim's checks were read-only anyway. Three minor issues:
- With 28 sessions there are 27 inter-session gaps, so "7 of 28" should probably read "7 of 27".
- The 5 listed OAP sessions give at most 10 nearest-neighbour heavy-PULL/OAP pairs, so the denominator of 11 needs a definition.
- The micro 1x6 is roughly bodyweight (90x75/87.5 floored to 75 kg total at a logged 75 kg BW). It is not one of the "two high-tension pulls" the spec sentence refers to, so it says little about that sentence's accuracy.
None of these changes the conclusion: a single sub-48 h instance, or the code logic alone, falsifies "guaranteed".

PHYSIOLOGY: the direction holds, but the cited evidence is overstated. The evidence doesn't favour 24 h; it can't tell 24 h from 48 h.
(1) Magnusson, Langberg & Kjaer 2010 (https://www.nature.com/articles/nrrheum.2010.43). The key point actually reads: "Without sufficient rest (24 h) after exercise, net loss of collagen might occur that leaves the tendon vulnerable to injury". The 24 h is a parenthetical, not a tested threshold. The abstract says synthesis "peaks around 24 h" and degradation "seems to peak earlier". The evidence pack's full-text reading puts net loss at about 18-36 h, which places 24 h inside the putative net-loss window. Reading this source as "24 h is sufficient" is an overread.
(2) The human time-course data behind it are sparse and not upper-limb. Langberg 1999 had 7 trained runners after a 36 km run, with peritendinous Achilles microdialysis sampled only before, immediately after, 2 h and 72 h; there were no 24/36/48 h samples, and ICTP "decreased transiently" (https://test01.ku.dk/:obvius/pureproxy/3974268/en/publications/type-i-collagen-synthesis-and-degradation-in-peritendinous-tissue). Miller 2005 measured patellar tendon collagen FSR after 1 h of one-legged kicking at 67% Wmax: it peaked at 24 h, was still elevated at 72 h, and degradation was not measured (https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16002437). Neither study isolates net balance at 24 vs 48 h, and neither involves heavy resistance or elbow tendons.
(3) Lopez-Rivera 2013 is coaching advice for spacing between lock-off sessions (24-72 h). It is not evidence that 24 h is enough between heavy weighted pulls and OAP, and its range extends to 72 h.
(4) Bohm 2015 is correctly described as silent: Achilles/patellar only, all interventions ≥8 weeks, chronic adaptation rather than acute recovery.
Correct framing: the spec's 48 h figure has no direct support, and neither does "~24 h is fine". The physiological consequence is indeterminate, not "weak for 48 h". This supports the claim's verdict (a documentation fix is certain; an engine gate is not established) but not its wording that "the cited sources accept 24 h".

RECOMMENDATION: it stays within the fixed constraints. The documentation correction is not a protocol change. The gate is offered only as a single future §26 candidate that uses the existing revert (v1.3 PULL volume, one of the 5 lifts), with advice to wait for data. It relaxes no judgment criteria and adds no accessories. One caveat should be added: a gate that reverts the OAP slot when a PULL-focus start was under 48 h ago covers only the PULL→OAP direction. It would have caught 1 of the 3 recorded cases (9/23→9/24). The other two (9/07 OAP→9/08 PULL, 23 h; 9/21 OAP→9/23 PULL, 44 h) run OAP→PULL, and preventing those would require gating the PULL-focus session instead, which is a different element.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 high · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: Spec §7.3 line 302, and the same sentence in docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md:34, says 48 h separation between heavy pull-ups and OAP is "structurally guaranteed". That is false. The engine (ref5.ts:1587-1589) creates the OAP slot whenever the session is NORMAL and the focus queue says BP, with no time check. The density valve (ref5.ts:1162-1167) allows normal sessions on consecutive calendar days and does not block a second session on the same day. So PULL focus and OAP are guaranteed only to fall in different sessions, and their spacing in either direction depends entirely on the user's schedule. Exceptions: an INVALID focus result does not alternate the queue, and a reverted BP session has no OAP. The provided record shows three sub-48 h PULL/OAP adjacencies (23, 44 and 24 h; not re-verified here). Micro bodyweight pull-ups are not "heavy pull" under the spec and are elbow-ledger context only.

The documentation fix is certain. Reword the sentence to "different sessions, not a time guarantee", and add a user-discipline note in §19.5 alongside the existing climbing 48 h discipline. The physiological case for 48 h over ~24 h is weak (low-to-moderate).

Any future engine gate is a §26.2 protocol change. The single candidate, reverting OAP when a PULL-focus start was under 48 h ago, would cover only the PULL→OAP direction (1 of the 3 cited cases). The OAP→PULL direction could only be covered by gating the heavy PULL session, which is a separate and larger change and should not be bundled.

Core claim confirmed from the spec and engine code, without the DB.

(1) Spec line 302 says verbatim that 48 h separation is "구조적으로 보장된다" (structurally guaranteed). The same sentence appears in docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md:34, so the doc fix must cover both files.

(2) The engine does not enforce 48 h. In packages/core/src/program-engine/ref5.ts:1587-1589, the OAP slot depends only on `sessionType === "NORMAL" && state.nextFocus === "BP"`; nothing checks the time since the last PULL-focus start. Focus flips on every normal session whose focus result is not INVALID (ref5.ts:2216-2219). Micro sessions do not consume the queue.

(3) The density valve allows back-to-back normal sessions. decideRef5SessionType (ref5.ts:1162-1167) forces MICRO only when both of the previous two calendar days were program days, or when the 6-day count is 3 or more. So a PULL-focus session on day D followed by an OAP session on day D+1 (about 24 h) is allowed. A second normal session on the same calendar day is not blocked either, so the gap can be only a few hours. The 48 h rule in §9 applies only to the SQ hard/V choice, not to session type or the OAP slot. The documentation error is therefore certain.

Revisions needed:

(a) "Any two normal sessions under 48 h apart pair PULL with OAP" has two exceptions: a focus result of INVALID (no alternation, §12), and a reverted BP-focus session (§7.6).

(b) The micro PULL volume 1x6 (about bodyweight) is not "heavy pull" in the spec's own terms. It does not contradict line 302 and is only context for the elbow risk ledger.

(c) "7 of 28 inter-session gaps" is internally inconsistent: 28 sessions give 27 gaps. About half of those gaps also fall in the pre-v1.4 plan, which had no OAP.

(d) I did not re-query the DB. The 23/44/24 h figures and "3 of 11" are as provided, not verified by this lens.

(e) Recommendation check. The doc-only change is one element, stays inside the fixed constraints, and does not touch FAIL 0 / HOLD≤1 / veto, so it is OK. The optional §26 gate is correctly framed as a conditional, low-to-moderate-certainty proposal. It would count as a §26.2 protocol change (48/168 h boundaries; app-computed items), so it needs a version review. As worded ("PULL focus start under 48 h ago → revert"), it covers only the PULL→OAP direction: 1 of the 3 cited cases (9/23→9/24). The other two (9/07 OAP→9/08 PULL, 9/21→9/23) run OAP→PULL. Closing those would mean gating the heavy PULL-focus session, which touches kg windows and the queue: a different and larger change that should not be bundled. The revert slot (PULL volume 2x6 at about bodyweight) still loads the elbow flexors, only at lower tension. The physiology point (24 h vs 48 h weakly supported) is consistent with the evidence pack: Magnusson 2010 calls 24 h sufficient, and Lopez gives 24-72 h.

## C21 · knee/elbow · risk · 확신 low · 변경 등급 operational

The undiagnosed suprapatellar (quadriceps-tendon-insertion) knee pain is the most likely limiter of G1 that lies outside the program, and the program has no structured pain signal by design. Twice-weekly heavy, non-failure high-bar squats are plausibly a tendon-friendly kind of loading. The athlete's out-of-program management resembles patellar-tendinopathy loading practice: an isometric warm-up at pain 0-3/10, plus depth and stance control. But stance has changed at least twice within the program (7/24 wider for knee pain; 10/04 narrower), which also makes exposures less comparable. Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- King et al. 2019, Ann Transl Med 7(4):72: there is a 'paucity of studies' on quadriceps tendinopathy; non-operative care comes first. https://atm.amegroups.org/article/view/23948/html
- Breda et al. 2021, BJSM 55(9):501-509 (JUMPER): progressive tendon loading, with progression gated by pain ≤3/10 on a single-leg squat test, beat eccentric training (VISA-P +28 vs +18). https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/55/9/501
- Agergaard et al. 2021, Am J Sports Med 49(4):982-993: heavy (90% 1RM) and moderate (55%) slow resistance gave similar 52-week results in patellar tendinopathy. https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0363546520988741
- Rio et al. 2015, BJSM 49:1277-1283 (n=6): isometric loading gave acute analgesia, still present at 45 min; small and short-term. https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/49/19/1277
- Crossley et al. 2016, BJSM 50:839-843: patellofemoral pain is aggravated by squatting and stairs, so the presentation overlaps; pain during a squat is the best available test. https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/50/14/839
- Bohm et al. 2015: tendon stiffness adapts to high-intensity (>70%) loading. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-015-0009-9
- Internal record memos: 7/24 'wide stance, knees-out cue is improving the knee pain'; 10/04 'narrowed stance slightly'. Brief: pain on stairs and sit-to-stand, managed outside the program.

**추론**

Evidence specific to quadriceps tendinopathy is sparse and mostly extrapolated from the patellar tendon. Without a diagnosis, patellofemoral pain is also plausible, and depth and load guidance differ between the two. That REF5's load profile suits tendon adaptation is an extrapolation. Keeping pain outside the judgment system follows the user's own design. But without a fixed pain metric, a knee-driven HOLD/FAIL cannot be told apart from a strength-driven one.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: The undiagnosed suprapatellar knee pain is a plausible but unquantified limiter of G1 outside the program. No evidence ranks it as the most likely one.

REF5 deliberately has no graded pain signal. A set stopped by pain can be closed as SAFETY→INVALID. Subthreshold pain that slows reps or inhibits force would still be scored as a strength HOLD/FAIL.

Tendinopathy trials show heavy load per se is not contraindicated: heavy and moderate slow resistance gave equivalent results (Agergaard 2021). But deep, repeated knee flexion is the implicated aggravator for both quadriceps tendinopathy (Simpson & Smith 2011) and patellofemoral pain (Crossley 2016). High-bar squats being "tendon-friendly" is therefore unproven, and depth and stance are the variables that matter. Knee loading also happens every session (about 2.9/week, sometimes on consecutive days), not just on the two hard days.

The isometric warm-up (45 s × 4–5 at pain 0–3) mirrors JUMPER stage 1. Its analgesic benefit, however, is small, short-lived and not superior to dynamic loading (Holden 2020; Clifford 2020).

Stance changed on 7/24 and 10/04, both within REF5 versions, which reduces how comparable SQ exposures are.

Recommendation, operational only: log a fixed provocation test before and the morning after hard SQ days, chosen to load the suspected structure. Log stance changes alongside it. Get a clinical assessment.

Checked against the spec, the cited sources, and counter-evidence.

The cited sources are mostly quoted accurately:
- King 2019 does say evidence is sparse.
- Breda 2021 (JUMPER) does report PTLE vs eccentric training, VISA-P 56→84 vs 57→75, adjusted difference 9 (1–16). Progression was gated by pain ≤3/10 on a single-leg squat. Its stage 1 is 5×45 s isometrics at 70% MVC, so the athlete's 45 s × 4–5 warm-up really does resemble patellar-tendinopathy practice.
- Agergaard 2021: 90% vs 55% 1RM slow resistance, volume-equated, n=44. Neither was superior at 52 weeks.
- Rio 2015 (n=6) is correctly described as small and short-term.
- Crossley 2016: squatting and stairs aggravate PFP, and pain on squatting is the best available test.
- Bohm 2015: stiffness responds to >70% loading in healthy Achilles and patellar tendons.

The spec confirms "no structured pain signal by design":
- §3.2 (docs/ref5-program-spec.md:83) excludes any medical or rehab branch for knee state or pain.
- §23.1 (line 909) sends knee pain outside the program.
- The elbow gets a recommended pain memo (line 743). The knee gets none.

Where the claim overreaches or leaves things out:

1. "Most likely limiter of G1 outside the program" has no supporting evidence. No cited source ranks the knee against other limiters such as C2 recovery and nutrition, the deficit, or individual ceilings. That ranking is opinion. "A plausible, unquantified limiter" is what the evidence supports.

2. "Plausibly tendon-friendly" is extrapolation, and some evidence cuts against it.
   - Bohm used healthy tendons. Agergaard compared two slow-tempo rehab loads against each other, not against normal-tempo deep barbell squats.
   - Simpson & Smith 2011 (Phys Ther Rev 16(6):455-461, https://ueaeprints.uea.ac.uk/id/eprint/35788/) links quadriceps tendinopathy to repetitive squatting and prolonged knee flexion. Crossley 2016 names squatting as the PFP aggravator.
   - So the evidence supports "heavy load per se is not contraindicated". It does not support "high-bar squatting is tendon-friendly". Deep flexion is the variable most likely to matter, which is why depth and stance control are the right levers.
   - "Twice-weekly heavy" also understates knee loading. SQ appears in every session, including V and micro, at about 2.9 per week, and squat sessions can fall on consecutive days.

3. The isometric warm-up's evidence is weaker than citing Rio implies.
   - Holden 2020 (J Sci Med Sport 23(3):208-214, n=21 randomized crossover, https://vbn.aau.dk/en/publications/isometric-exercise-and-pain-in-patellar-tendinopathy-a-randomized): pain fell only 0.9 NRS (95% CI 0.1–1.7). The effect was gone by 45 min, and isometric was no better than dynamic exercise.
   - Clifford 2020 (BMJ Open SEM, 10 RCTs, https://doaj.org/article/d325da3c588f4bed824e9ce334763bbb): isometric exercise was not superior to isotonic for chronic tendinopathy.
   - So the resemblance to standard practice is real, but the analgesic rationale is a small, short-lived effect that has not replicated well.

4. "Cannot tell a knee-driven HOLD/FAIL from a strength-driven one" is partly overstated. A set stopped by pain can be closed as SAFETY→INVALID (§11.2, §11.4), and the user can choose a micro session for any reason. The real gap is subthreshold pain that slows reps or inhibits force without stopping the set. Rio 2015 found MVC rose 18.7% once pain dropped, which implies pain was suppressing force. In that case a strength HOLD/FAIL would be recorded.

5. Stance memos (7/24 wider, 10/04 narrower): I did not verify these against the DB. Both dates fall within REF5 (v1.3 dated 7/24, v1.4 from 9/02). The comparability point is logical, not empirical.

Recommendation: it is operational, outside the judgment system, a single logging element, and relaxes no criterion, so it is OK.
- Gating on a provocation test matches Breda.
- Logging the next-morning response is in the spirit of pain-monitored loading. Silbernagel 2007 (AJSM 35(6):897-906, Achilles, n=38) found no harm from continued pain-monitored activity. I could not verify its specific next-morning rules.
- One refinement: keep the test fixed, and make it load the suspected structure. For quadriceps tendinopathy, Simpson & Smith list tenderness at the quadriceps–patella junction and pain on resisted extension in deep knee flexion. A single-leg squat mainly tests PFP and the patellar tendon.
- A clinical assessment to separate quadriceps tendinopathy from PFP is well justified.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 low · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: The undiagnosed suprapatellar (quadriceps-tendon-insertion) knee pain is a plausible but unquantified out-of-program risk to G1. It has not been shown to be the most likely limiter. There are no HOLD or FAIL results in 28 sessions while SQ H3 rose from 80 to 85, and recovery, the calorie deficit and climbing compete as limiters.

By design, REF5 has no pain input and no pain-driven branching (§3.2, §23.1). Its only knee-relevant levers are binary and manual: a user-chosen MICRO, where pain is an allowed reason (§8), and closing a set stopped by pain as SAFETY, which makes it INVALID (§11.2-11.4). Unlike the elbow (§19.4 and §19.5(b)), the knee has no written operational guideline. This creates two problems:
- Pain-modulated slowdowns or missed reps can be logged as strength HOLD/FAIL.
- Pain-stopped sets closed as SAFETY are excluded silently. They lengthen the ~24-day SQ window, still use up hard density, and never trigger stagnation or structureReview.

The knee is loaded in every session, not only twice a week. The observed rate is about 2.9 squat exposures per week (about 1.75 hard plus V/MICRO), and consecutive days are allowed. Heavy (>70%) non-failure loading fits tendon-loading principles (Bohm 2015; Agergaard 2021). The gap is that REF5 raises load on performance alone, without the symptom gating that tendinopathy loading programs use (Breda 2021; Silbernagel 2007).

The athlete's isometric warm-up resembles Rio 2015, but its analgesic advantage has not been replicated (Clifford 2020 systematic review: isometrics no better than isotonic exercise). Treat it as a convenience. Stance changes on 7/24 and 10/04 (reported memos, not independently verified) reduce how comparable squat exposures are; the engine does not record stance.

Recommendation (operational only):
- Score one fixed provocation test from 0 to 10. Take it before the isometric warm-up and again the morning after every squat session, and log it together with any stance change.
- Pre-register that the score never reclassifies an outcome after the fact. Only a stop for knee pain at the moment of the set, recorded as SAFETY, excludes an exposure.
- Get a clinical assessment to separate quadriceps tendinopathy from patellofemoral pain.

I checked the claim against REF5's rules in /home/claude/workout-log/docs/ref5-program-spec.md, the athlete's records, and the evidence. The core holds: the knee is a real out-of-program risk, and REF5 deliberately has no graded pain signal. Four parts need revision.

WHAT HOLDS
(1) No pain signal by design is accurate. §3.2 (line 83) excludes any medical or rehab branching that involves knee state or pain. §23.1 (line 909) says knee and pain problems are solved outside the program. The engine takes no pain input, so keeping the pain metric outside the judgment system matches the spec.
(2) The out-of-program management does resemble patellar-tendinopathy loading practice. The athlete's warm-up of 45 s x 4-5 isometrics is close to Rio 2015's 5 x 45 s at 70% MVC. The 0-3/10 pain ceiling matches Breda 2021's ≤3/10 progression gate.
(3) The evidence for heavy, non-failure loading on tendons is extrapolation, and the claim says so. Bohm 2015 (>70% intensity) and Agergaard 2021 (heavy and moderate slow resistance gave similar results) support the direction. Quadriceps-specific evidence is sparse (King 2019).
(4) The patellofemoral overlap (Crossley 2016) is correctly flagged.

WHAT NEEDS REVISION
(a) 'The most likely limiter of G1 outside the program' is unsupported opinion. The records show 28 sessions with no HOLD or FAIL, and SQ H3 rose from 80 to 85, so the knee has not yet limited anything observable. Other out-of-program limiters compete with it: C2 recovery, the planned calorie deficit and climbing. The goal-feasibility pack also rates the knee-as-binding-constraint evidence as weak. A better wording is 'a plausible but unquantified risk'.

(b) 'No structured pain signal' leaves out the levers REF5 already has. Both are binary and manual:
- §8 trigger 1 (line 375) lets the user choose MICRO for any reason, and the spec names pain explicitly.
- §11.2-11.4 (lines 482-512) let a set stopped by pain be closed as SAFETY, which makes it INVALID.
For the elbow, the spec writes these levers down as guidelines: a weekly pain memo in §19.4 (line 743) and a SAFETY rule in §19.5(b) (line 765). The knee has no equivalent written guideline.
The claim's statement that knee-driven and strength-driven outcomes 'cannot be told apart' is therefore only partly right, and the real problem is two-sided:
- Pain-modulated slowdowns or missed reps logged as CLEAR_SLOWDOWN or FORCE_OR_TECHNIQUE look like strength HOLD/FAIL.
- Sets closed as SAFETY are excluded silently. They lengthen the SQ 6-hard window (already about 24-25 days) and still use up hard density (§11.4). They never trigger stagnation or structureReview, so the engine cannot see a knee-driven stall.

(c) 'Twice-weekly heavy' understates how often the knee is loaded. Every session contains SQ, including MICRO (V 2x5). The observed rate is about 1.75 hard plus V/micro exposures, about 2.9 squat exposures per week. Squatting on consecutive days is allowed: day 2 becomes V, because hard sets need 48 h. The tendon-relevant gap is not the load profile itself. REF5 raises load on performance alone, with no symptom gating. The tendinopathy programs the claim cites as precedent do use symptom gating: Breda 2021 (pain ≤3/10 gate), and Silbernagel 2007 (Am J Sports Med 35(6):897-906, a pain-monitored RCT in Achilles tendinopathy, confirmed; https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0363546506298279; its exact pain thresholds are not in the abstract).

(d) The isometric caveat should be stronger. Clifford et al. 2020 (BMJ Open Sport Exerc Med 6:e000760, confirmed; https://bmjopensem.bmj.com/content/6/1/e000760) is a systematic review of 10 RCTs. It found isometrics no better than isotonic exercise for immediate or short-term pain, and it states that Rio's large effect in 6 volleyball players 'has not been replicated'. The warm-up is a reasonable convenience, not an evidence-based analgesic.

UNVERIFIED
I could not verify the stance memos (7/24 'wider, knees-out'; 10/04 'narrowed slightly'). They are not in the repo or spec, and no web/.env.local exists, so I did not query the database. The comparability point is sound in principle. The spec treats H3/H2/V as the same high-bar squat (§3.1, §4) and has no stance definition, so a change in stance width is invisible to the engine. 'Both lenses agree' is a process statement I cannot check.

RECOMMENDATION CHECK
