The recommendation is compliant (recommendationOk). It is operational, sits outside the judgment system, and changes no protocol element. It mirrors the existing §19.4 elbow-memo pattern. It adds no lift or accessory, no 1RM test, no RIR and no AMRAP, and it does not touch FAIL 0, HOLD≤1 or the volume veto. The clinical assessment is not a protocol change.

Three refinements would make it more useful:
- Take the pre-session score before the isometric warm-up, so any analgesia from the warm-up does not hide the reading.
- Score the morning after every squat session, not only hard days, since V and MICRO also load the knee.
- Pre-register that the pain score never reclassifies an outcome after the fact. Only a stop for pain at the moment of the set, recorded as SAFETY, may exclude an exposure. Otherwise the log could act as a back-door relaxation of the judgment criteria the user has fixed.

## C22 · goal feasibility · unknown · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 doc-or-display

G1 (175 kg high-bar at ~70 kg, 2.5x BW) is a rare, upper-percentile outcome. It needs about +70% on the ~103 kg reference, which is roughly 184-193 kg low-bar-equivalent (2.6-2.75x BW). That sits above the local 74 kg competitor mean and below the drug-tested 90th percentile for young men. It is defensible as a multi-year direction. It cannot be credibly dated, REF cannot confirm attainment, and program cadence is not what limits it. Constant-rate arithmetic (5-10%/yr) gives roughly 5.6-11 years, and real gains usually slow over time. The lenses DISAGREE on confidence: A rates the claim medium and declines to give a timeline; B gives the arithmetic timeline and rates it low. Merged: rarity is moderately well anchored; any timeline is low-confidence.

**근거**

- Gonzalez et al. 2025, Appl Sci 15(6):3143: low-bar 1RM ~7% above high-bar in men. https://doi.org/10.3390/app15063143 . Nuckols (coaching): typically 5-10% more low-bar. https://www.strongerbyscience.com/its-time-to-end-this-nonsense-high-bar-vs-low-bar-squatting/
- Latella et al. 2019, JSCR 33(7S):S103-S112: raw 74 kg class squat means 172.7 kg (local) vs 200.2 kg (international). https://acuresearchbank.acu.edu.au/download/f50fbd80af80bb0182c6d2df3de9874197aaf9db5354cacaff4089cf71d01cde/280151/Latella_2019_Differences_in_Strength_Performance_Between_Novice.pdf
- van den Hoek et al. 2024, J Sci Med Sport 27(10):734-742: drug-tested male 90th-percentile squat is 2.83x BW for young adults. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsams.2024.07.005 . StrengthLog: 95th percentile at 70 kg is 157 kg. https://www.strengthlog.com/squat-strength-standards-kg/
- Latella et al. 2024, Sports Med 54(3):753-774: ~7.5-12.5% in the first competitive year and up to ~20% after 10 years; progress is front-loaded. https://doi.org/10.1007/s40279-023-01962-6
- Nuckols 2022 (coaching analysis): each equally spaced milestone takes about twice as long. https://www.strongerbyscience.com/?p=57447 . Nuckols 2018: median monthly gain fell from 0.78% to 0.26% over ~770 days, and sex, age and strength models explain only ~11% of the variance. https://www.strongerbyscience.com/?p=46506
- Internal computation: 175/103 = 1.70; ln(1.70)/ln(1.10) ≈ 5.6 yr and ln(1.70)/ln(1.05) ≈ 10.9 yr. Spec §20: REF or REF ÷ bodyweight cannot confirm a real 175 kg.

**추론**

Competitor data are selected, mostly low-bar, belted and peaked, and individual variance is large. The athlete's history (1.37x BW after a 1-year plateau on poorly matched programs) could mean faster early gains from unrealized potential. The planned cut and the knee could slow them. The rate of true adaptation, knee tolerance and recovery, not program cadence, will decide the outcome.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: G1 (high-bar 175 kg at about 70 kg, 2.5x bodyweight) is a rare, upper-percentile goal. It needs about +70% over the historical ~103 kg e1RM. Current control REF is about 107, but REF is not a 1RM. High-bar is typically 5-10% below low-bar: two small studies found about 6-7%, and coaching opinion says 5-10%. On that basis, 175 kg high-bar is about 184-193 kg low-bar-equivalent, or 2.6-2.75x bodyweight. That figure is before belt, peaking and depth adjustments, which would push it higher. It sits above the local 74 kg-class competitor mean of 172.7 kg. It sits below the 2.83x 90th percentile for drug-tested, unequipped men aged 18-35. That percentile is pooled across weight classes, and lighter classes are relatively stronger, so the class-specific 90th percentile is likely higher. Overall this is roughly upper-quartile competitive-powerlifter territory. The athlete's age band is not known. The goal is defensible as a multi-year direction. REF cannot confirm attainment (spec §20). The program's progression ceiling at the observed cadence, about 38 kg/yr on H3, exceeds even competitors' best-case squat gains of 20-25 kg/yr, so cadence is not the binding limit. The goal cannot be credibly dated. The cited longitudinal data do not support a sustained 5-10%/yr. Drug-tested raw competitors gain about 7.5-12.5% in year 1 and about 12.5-20% cumulative over 10 years. Median rates fall to about 3%/yr by around year 2. A typical trajectory would therefore not reach +70% within a decade. The 5.6-11-year constant-rate figures are an optimistic bound. They apply only if the athlete's current level is well below his potential, which is plausible given a low baseline (~1.4x bodyweight) and poorly matched past programming, but unproven. Confidence: medium on rarity, low on any timeline.

The rarity part holds. The timeline part goes further than its own sources allow.

WHAT CHECKS OUT
1. Arithmetic. 175/103 = 1.70. Adding 5-10% gives 183.75-192.5 kg, which is 2.63-2.75x at 70 kg. ln1.70/ln1.10 = 5.6 yr and ln1.70/ln1.05 = 10.9 yr.
2. van den Hoek 2024 (JSAMS 27(10):734-742). I read the abstract via the ECU and LIDA repositories: https://ro.ecu.edu.au/ecuworks2022-2026/4617 and https://lida.sport-iat.de/ta/Record/4088979. The data are global drug-tested, unequipped competitions: 809,986 entries, 571,650 of them male. Age groups follow UN categories. The male squat 90th percentile is 2.83x bodyweight for "young adults (18-35)". So "drug-tested 90th percentile for young men" is accurate. Two qualifiers apply. First, the figure is pooled across weight classes, and the abstract says the lightest classes have the highest relative strength. The 90th percentile for a 66-74 kg class is therefore probably above 2.83x, which strengthens "below the 90th percentile". Second, the athlete's age is not given, so the 18-35 band may not be his.
3. Latella 2019, 74 kg local mean 172.7 kg, was confirmed from the full text in the evidence pack. Gonzalez 2025 (men +7.2%, n=9) and Nuckols's 5-10% coaching figure were also confirmed. So "above the local mean, below the 90th percentile" holds.
4. Spec §20, docs/ref5-program-spec.md:797, does say that REF, or REF divided by bodyweight, cannot confirm a real 175 kg.
5. "Cadence is not the limit" is supported. At the observed ~1.75 hard exposures per week, the progression ceiling is about 38 kg/yr on H3. Latella 2022's maximum squat gain is 20-25 kg/yr. The margin is about 1.5-3x, not overwhelming, but the ceiling does not bind.

FIDELITY PROBLEMS
A. The 5-10%/yr band contradicts the claim's own citations for a multi-year horizon.
- Latella 2024: I checked the SportRxiv preprint, https://sportrxiv.org/index.php/server/preprint/download/218/421, and the ECU abstract, https://ro.ecu.edu.au/ecuworks2022-2026/3457. The sample is 9,259 IPF drug-tested raw lifters. Gains were about 7.5-12.5% in year 1 and about 12.5-20% cumulative by 10 years. The authors write: "Rapid strength adaptation occurs within the first year of competition before beginning to plateau." They also tested for survivorship bias and found little.
- Nuckols 2018: the median rate is 0.26%/month, about 3.2%/yr, by around 770 days.
- On these data a typical trajectory gains roughly 20% in a decade, not 70%. Even 3%/yr held constant takes about 18 years. A decaying rate may never get there, and the evidence pack's own 7%/yr rate decaying 15%/yr levels off near 163 kg.
- So 5.6-11 years is an optimistic bound, not a central range. It applies only if the athlete is well below his potential. That is plausible: he squats about 1.4x bodyweight, which is near the bottom of the competitor distribution, and Latella 2020 shows weaker lifters gain faster. But it is unproven.
- Nuckols's 2022 doubling heuristic, also cited by the claim, implies timelines very sensitive to the current milestone time and possibly far beyond 11 years.
- The claim's statements "real gains slow" and "low confidence" are right. The numbers attached to them still anchor on optimism.
B. The conversion to competition terms leaves out belt, peaking and depth. Competition "unequipped" allows belts and sleeves. If the 103 kg was beltless, the competition-equivalent is higher than 184-193 kg, so the goal is, if anything, rarer than stated. I did not quantify this; the size is unverified.
C. The +70% is measured from a historical e1RM estimate. Current control REF is about 107 (85 × 104/82.5), which would mean about +63%, but REF is not a 1RM. This is minor, and it does not change the conclusion.

RECOMMENDATION
The recommendation is a documentation or display change only. It changes no protocol element, adds no 1RM test and relaxes no criterion. It keeps the §20 caveat, so it is within the athlete's fixed principles.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: G1 (175 kg high-bar at ~70 kg) is a rare, upper-percentile outcome. It needs about +70% over the athlete's pre-REF5 estimated 1RM of ~103 kg. That is not control REF, which is now ~107 at H3 85 and is not a 1RM. In low-bar terms that is roughly 184–193 kg (2.6–2.75x BW at 70 kg). This is above the local 74 kg competitor mean (172.7 kg) and below the international mean (200 kg). Among drug-tested competitors it is below the 90th percentile for young adults (2.83x) but above it for middle-aged men (~2.50x). The athlete's age is unrecorded, so the percentile placement is conditional. Among general trained lifters it is beyond the app-data 95th percentile.

G1 is defensible as a multi-year direction, but it cannot be dated. Constant 5–10%/yr gives 5.6–11 years, and that should be read as an optimistic floor. The cited data (≤~20% per decade in competitors; each milestone takes roughly twice as long as the last) are consistent with anything from about 6 years to non-attainment.

REF5's increment cadence (≥~37 kg/yr H3) does not bind. Still unvalidated: the multi-year adequacy of the constrained stimulus during a cut, and how well the stagnation path tracks slow adaptation. REF cannot confirm attainment (spec §20, §22.4 #8). REF may also run ahead of true 1RM once loads near the slowdown threshold, so REF/BW milestones are upward-biased proxies.

What I checked against the repo (read-only): /home/claude/workout-log/docs/ref5-program-spec.md §5–§6, §10, §13, §15, §18–§23 and §26, docs/bodyweight-timeseries-plan.md, and git history. I also re-checked the 2.83x anchor on the Barbell Medicine page.

Confirmed:
(1) Spec §20 (line 797) and §22.4 #8 do say that control REF and REF÷BW cannot confirm a real 175 kg. §6.3 calls REF÷BW only a long-term proxy and forbids describing REF as 1RM or %1RM.
(2) The arithmetic is correct. 175/103 = 1.699. ln(1.70)/ln(1.10) = 5.57 yr and ln(1.70)/ln(1.05) = 10.9 yr. 175×1.05–1.10 = 184–193 kg, which is 2.63–2.75x at 70 kg. 103/75 = 1.37x.
(3) The cadence ceiling is not binding. The observed rate is one 2.5 kg H3 step per ~24.5 days, about 37 kg/yr H3. The rule maximum (≤2 hard starts per 168 h) is about 43 kg/yr. Both are above the confirmed best-case competitor squat gains of 20–25 kg/yr (Latella 2022).
(4) The anchors are cited correctly from the verified pack: Latella 2019 74 kg local mean 172.7, Gonzalez 2025 ~7%, StrengthLog 157 kg, Latella 2024, Nuckols 2018/2022.

Revisions needed:
(a) The 90th-percentile comparison depends on age, and the athlete's age is not recorded anywhere in the repo docs. Barbell Medicine's reading of van den Hoek 2024 gives 2.83x for young adults but about 2.50x in middle age. If the athlete is middle-aged, 2.6–2.75x is above the age-matched drug-tested 90th percentile, so the goal is rarer than the claim states. The page also does not say whether the multiples are pooled across weight classes.
(b) The 5.6–11 yr range is really an optimistic lower bound. The evidence the claim itself cites contradicts holding 5–10%/yr for 6–11 years. Latella 2024 reports up to ~20% over 10 competitive years, and Nuckols 2022 says each milestone takes about twice as long as the last. A decaying-rate model fits these data, and its result swings widely with the inputs. Starting at 7%/yr and shrinking 15%/yr plateaus near 163 kg, so 175 is never reached. Starting at 10%/yr with the same decay reaches 175 in about 10 years. The honest range runs from about 6 years to not attained, though lower-baseline lifters do progress faster (Latella 2020).
(c) "~103 kg reference" should say this is the athlete's pre-REF5 estimated 1RM, not control REF. Current REF at H3 85 is 104×85/82.5 = 107.2.
(d) "Cadence is not limiting" is true for the increment ceiling. It does not show that the program design is not limiting. Two things remain untested over multiple years: the stimulus dose under the fixed constraints (5 lifts, no accessories, non-failure, a cut), and the §15 stagnation sawtooth, which may under-track slow adaptation (§22.4 #3).
(e) Extrapolation: REF uses a fixed 104/82.5 scale. If H3 settles near the 3x3 slowdown threshold (~85–88% of 1RM), REF would run about 5–10% above true 1RM. REF/BW milestones would then overstate how close the athlete is to 2.5x.

Recommendation: it is doc/display only, a single element, and violates no fixed constraint (no tests, RIR, AMRAP or deloads, no criteria relaxed; allowed under §26.1). Most of it is already in the spec: §20 states there is no date and that REF cannot confirm, and §21 already logs REF÷7-day-average BW. Four caveats:
- The 7-day measured BW does not exist yet in practice, because every session logs 75 kg. The body_measurement table exists (commit eb34b30), so this is a data-entry gap.
- "needs C11" collides with the athlete's constraint C11 (no competition).
- REF/BW must not be plotted against the 2.5x target as if it were 1RM (§6.3). Show absolute REF and measured BW separately, so that weight loss alone does not look like strength gain.
- The H3 92.5 HOLD-free milestone is meaningful: it is the old 92.5x3 at RPE 9–10, now done for 3 clean sets. H3 90 alone may only reflect catch-up.

## C23 · goal feasibility · unknown · 확신 low · 변경 등급 observation-only

G6 (first one-arm chin-up) is plausible, but it is limited by bilateral weighted-pull strength relative to bodyweight, not by ladder speed. The athlete's tested pull total was 110 kg at a logged 75 kg (+35 kg, ~47% of BW). Practitioner heuristics put the threshold at a two-arm chin with +60-67% of BW, i.e. ~+45-50 kg added at 75 kg, a gap of ~10-15 kg. Cutting to 70 kg at a constant total raises total/BW from 1.47x to 1.57x, worth ~7.9 kg of added load: about half the gap. Because the logged bodyweight is a default (C11), the real gap is uncertain by several kg. No peer-reviewed data on OAP acquisition exist. Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Willoughby rule, via Legendary Strength 2018 (heuristic, disputed by commenters): one one-arm chin is about a two-arm chin with +2/3 of bodyweight. https://legendarystrength.com/chinups-two-arm-one-arm-weighted/
- J Aust Strength Cond 2019;27(7):62-68, a practitioner 9-step model: 3RM chin-up at +60% BW before one-arm negatives and partials. https://lida.sport-iat.de/ta/Record/4058980?lng=en
- DeMarco (coaching, n=1): ~3 years to the first one-arm chin; begin specific work at about +2/3 BW. leverage.fit says no research-backed bodyweight threshold guarantees an OAP. https://divergentstrengthacademy.com/blog/one-arm-chinups
- Lopez-Rivera 2013 (coaching): lock-off and elbow adaptation takes 'years'. http://en-eva-lopez.blogspot.com/2013/05/lock-off-training-for-climbers-v-goal.html
- Latella et al. 2022: best competitive bench gains are 10.5-12.8 kg/yr, the closest upper-body rate analogue. https://doi.org/10.1249/MSS.0000000000002858
- Internal computation: 75 x 5/3 = 125 total (+15 vs 110); 70 x 5/3 ≈ 117. The ladder minimum is ≥15 PASS per arm (about 10-14 weeks at the observed cadence). Spec §22.4.9: weekly PULL sets dropped from ~8 to ~6 in v1.4.

**추론**

All thresholds are unvalidated coaching heuristics, and no study links weighted pull-up strength to one-arm chin achievement or timing, so confidence is low. The bodyweight cut helps G6 more than any ladder tweak would.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 low · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: G6 (a first one-arm chin-up) is plausible. One practitioner report (JASC 2019) says many elite males weighing 50-80 kg reach it, but gives no outcome data. The ladder's rules are not what limits the timeline: reaching rung 6 takes at least about 15 PASS per arm, roughly 10-14 weeks. Whether the athlete is held back by two-arm pulling strength or by one-arm-specific capacity (asymmetric transfer, lock-off position, anti-rotation) cannot be determined from any evidence. The project's own v1.4 proposal argued for the second explanation, using different heuristics.

Practitioner heuristics are inconsistent and come in two kinds. Thresholds for starting one-arm work run from about +50% BW (r/bodyweightfitness wiki; Antranik 2023) to about +60-67% (JASC 3RM; O'Gallagher 4-5RM; DeMarco). Rules for when a two-arm chin equals one one-arm chin run from +2/3 BW (Willoughby) to about +75-80% (Antranik; O'Gallagher/BarBend). JASC's +60% is a 3RM, which is about +69-76% as a 1RM.

The athlete has one recorded test: a weighted pull-up, grip not specified, 110 kg total at a self-reported 75 kg, so +47% BW. The best ever was +40 kg at 65-75 kg BW, i.e. +53-62%. At 75 kg the athlete is therefore at or near the start thresholds, and about 15-25 kg short of the equivalence heuristics, not a firm 10-15 kg. Dropping to 70 kg at the same total raises total/BW from 1.47 to 1.57, worth about 7.9 kg of added load at 75 kg. That would close roughly 55-80% of a +60-67% gap, but a smaller share of a +75-80% gap.

There is no longitudinal or controlled data on how people acquire the one-arm pull-up. The only peer-reviewed cross-sectional data (Sas-Nowosielski 2022) found that 2 of 24 advanced 65 kg climbers could do 4-5 unassisted one-arm pull-ups. Their mean weighted pull-up was a 5RM at +42% BM, about +65% as a 1RM. So two-arm strength near the heuristic does not reliably mean one-arm capacity.

Evidence-fidelity check. Read-only; sources: spec, v1.4 proposal, the JASC record, the J Hum Kinet full text, and practitioner pages.

What holds:
(a) G6 is plausible. JASC 2019 (https://lida.sport-iat.de/ta/Record/4058980?lng=en) says "many elite male athletes of moderate bodyweight (50 to 80 kg)" achieve it, but reports no counts or success rates.
(b) The ladder rules are not what limits the timeline. At least 15 PASS per arm is a floor. On any equivalence heuristic, the two-arm gap would take 1+ years at the bench rate analogue (Latella 2022, 10.5-12.8 kg/yr). That is extrapolation, but the arithmetic is internally consistent.
(c) The cut arithmetic is correct: 110/75 = 1.467 and 110/70 = 1.571, and 1.571 x 75 - 110 = 7.9 kg. "About half the gap" understates it: at 70 kg the remaining gap is 2-6.7 kg, so the cut closes 55-80%.
(d) Low confidence and "no validated threshold" are correct.

Problems:
1. The threshold mixes two kinds of heuristic and misreads JASC. JASC's criterion is a 3RM chin-up at +60% BW. The record says that at this point "the strength required ... has been established," after which negatives and partials follow. As a 1RM that is about +69-76% BW: Brzycki or Epley on 1.6 x 75 = 120 gives a 127-132 kg total, a gap of 17-22 kg, not 10. DeMarco's +2/3 is a point to begin specific work, not to achieve the chin. Only Willoughby's +2/3 is an equivalence rule.
2. The +60-67% range leaves out heuristics on both sides:
   - r/bodyweightfitness wiki: at least +50% BW before one-arm work.
   - Antranik 2023 (https://antranik.org/weighted-chinups/): 50% is "a very strong base," 75% is "possible can pull off."
   - BarBend/O'Gallagher 2016 (https://barbend.com/2-exercises-thatll-get-one-armed-chin): 4-5 reps at about 60% BW, with one-arm chin about equal to a chin at +80% BW.
   - The project's own docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md §1 used "1.5xBW single / +25-45% BW x 3-5" and concluded the athlete's 1.47xBW was borderline. It placed the bottleneck in skill and one-arm transfer, not pulling strength.
   So the gap is anywhere from about 0 to 25 kg depending on the heuristic. The 10-15 kg figure is not robust.
3. The causal claim "limited by bilateral strength, not ladder speed" is stated as a finding. No source links bilateral load to achieving a one-arm chin; leverage.fit says explicitly that no such threshold exists. Several sources point to large one-arm and task-specific components: specificity work (Kassiano 2025, Spitz 2023, Saeterbakken 2025, the latter on isometric lock-off transfer), bilateral-deficit plasticity (Skarabot 2016), and the JASC sequence, which needs one-arm steps after the strength gate. The athlete's own data are ambiguous: a 4-month stall at rung 2 before v1.4, a demotion to rung 1, and the arms progressing at different rates.
4. Relevant counter-evidence is missing. In Sas-Nowosielski 2022 (J Hum Kinet 84:206-215, https://jhk.termedia.pl/pdf-158770-85024), 24 advanced climbers (BM 65 kg) had a mean 5RM weighted pull-up of +41.9% BM (range 15-70%), about +65% as a 1RM. Only 2 did 4-5 unassisted one-arm pull-ups; the others needed bands. This is cross-sectional, uses a pronated grip and counts 4-5 reps, not 1. Still, it weakly suggests the +60-67% level does not give one-arm capacity on its own.
5. Data handling:
   - The 110 total is a single historical weighted pull-up test at a self-reported 75 kg (spec §19.6.3). Its bodyweight was not the database default.
   - The claim's "C11" is mislabelled; C11 is "no competition."
   - It omits the historical best of +40 kg at 65-75 kg BW (+53-62%).
   - It does not address the grip mismatch: the heuristics are for supinated chin-ups, while the PULL grip orientation is unspecified and only the width is fixed (§19.5.3).

Recommendation: observation-only. It is within the fixed constraints and changes no protocol element, so it is OK, but it is mis-specified. PULL focus is a 3x3 working load at about 81% of REF; for example, 90 kg total / 75 kg = 1.2x, or +20% BW added. Comparing that with 1RM or 3RM heuristics will mislead. Display PULL control REF (or an explicit e1RM) divided by measured BW instead. Show the heuristics as a band, roughly +50% (start) to +67-80% (equivalence), and label JASC as a 3RM. Make clear the marker cannot validate readiness.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: insufficient-evidence · 수정 확신 low · 권고 유지 False

수정 주장: G6 (a first one-arm chin-up) is plausible, but the timeline and the limiting factor cannot be identified from current data or literature. The best known bilateral weighted pull-up total is 110 kg at about 75 kg bodyweight (+35 kg, about 47%), and that bodyweight is unconfirmed.

Bilateral-strength thresholds are unvalidated practitioner heuristics, and they disagree:
- +2/3 of bodyweight for a two-arm chin 1RM (Willoughby): a gap of about 15 kg.
- A 3RM at +60% of bodyweight (JASC 2019), roughly +69-76% as a 1RM: a gap of about 17-22 kg.
- A 1.5× bodyweight single, the heuristic in REF5's own v1.4 proposal: a gap of about 2.5 kg, closed at 70 kg.
- The test was a pull-up and the target is a chin-up, which probably narrows every gap by an unmeasured amount.

Cutting to 70 kg at a constant total raises total/bodyweight from 1.47 to 1.57, worth about 7.9 kg of added load at 75 kg.

The ladder's design minimum (about 15 consecutive PASS exposures per arm, about 10-14 weeks) is not the binding constraint. The observed ladder rate (net 0 or -1 rung in 5 exposures) shows a large one-arm-specific deficit. Whether that deficit is mainly bilateral strength or task-specific factors (asymmetric force transfer, lock-off, anti-rotation, as the v1.4 proposal assumes) cannot be distinguished. No outcome studies on acquiring a one-arm pull-up exist.

Optional display: a PULL REF-based added-load ÷ measured 7-day bodyweight trend next to the rung state, labelled as a proxy rather than a 1RM, with no heuristic readiness line. No protocol change.

The arithmetic is correct. The central causal claim, that G6 is limited by bilateral strength and not by ladder speed, cannot be established. It also conflicts with REF5's own documented premise, and the claim does not address that.

WHAT CHECKS OUT
- 110 kg total at 75 kg is +35 kg added, about 47% of bodyweight. This matches spec §19.6 step 3 and the v1.4 proposal header ("실측 1RM 총 110, 1.47×BW").
- +60-67% of bodyweight at 75 kg is +45-50 kg, a gap of 10-15 kg.
- 110/70 = 1.571. 1.571 × 75 = 117.9, so the cut is worth about +7.9 kg. At 70 kg the gap shrinks to about 2-7 kg, which is "about half" or more of the original gap.
- Ladder minimum: from rung 1, reaching rung 6 needs 15 consecutive-PASS exposures (3 at each of rungs 1-5); achievement needs 18. The right arm, at rung 2, needs 12 to reach rung 6. At the observed OAP cadence (5 exposures from 9/05 to 10/06, about 1.1-1.5 per week; spec line 302 says about 1.5 per week at 3x/week) that is about 10-14 weeks. The §19.5 two-weeks-per-rung guideline rarely binds.
- "No peer-reviewed OAP acquisition data" matches the evidence pack. JASC 2019 is a practitioner model with no outcome data, and Sas-Nowosielski 2022 is an acute study.
- Low confidence is appropriate.

PROBLEMS
1) Contradicts the program's design rationale without engaging it. The v1.4 proposal (docs/ref5-v1.4-oap-proposal.md lines 6-7 and 23) states "근력 ≥ 요구치, 스킬은 초반" (strength meets the requirement; skill is at an early stage). It cites a different heuristic, "통상 1.5×BW 싱글 / +25~45% BW × 3~5렙" (typically a single at 1.5× bodyweight, or +25-45% of bodyweight for 3-5 reps). It concludes that the bottleneck is asymmetric force transfer, elbow-tucked position and anti-rotation, and that "중량풀업 증량으로는 채워지지 않는다" (adding weight to pull-ups will not close it). By that heuristic the gap at 75 kg is about 2.5 kg, and at 70 kg it is already closed. Both positions rest on equally unvalidated coaching heuristics.
   - Neither can be settled from the athlete's data. The pre-v1.4 record shows 4 months stuck at rung 2 doing 1-2 reps, and after the cutover a demotion to rung 1.
   - Confirmed specificity meta-analyses favour the program's premise that unilateral and isometric lock-off capacity is task-specific. These are Kassiano 2025 (unilateral strength favoured by unilateral training, ES -0.65), Spitz 2023, and Saeterbakken 2025 (dynamic-to-isometric transfer SMD 0.42).
   - So "limited by bilateral strength, not ladder speed" is an unsupported causal assertion. The design minimum of the ladder is not binding, but the observed ladder rate is itself the measure of the one-arm-specific deficit. The claim separates the two more cleanly than the data allow.
2) The heuristic bases are mixed:
   - JASC 2019 is a 3RM at +60% of bodyweight, not a 1RM. Converted to a 1RM with Epley or Brzycki it is roughly +69-76% of bodyweight, about +52-57 kg at 75 kg, so the gap is about 17-22 kg.
   - DeMarco's +2/3 of bodyweight is the point at which he says to begin specific work, not an achievement threshold.
   - Across the heuristics in play, the gap runs from about 0 to about 22 kg. The 10-15 kg figure is a selection within that range.
3) Grip mismatch, not mentioned. The tested lift is the REF5 "Weighted Pull-Up" (§19.5.3, shoulder-to-narrow grip). The heuristics, and G6 itself ("첫 목표는 원암 친업", first target: a one-arm chin-up, §7.5.2), are chin-up based. A two-arm chin-up 1RM is plausibly higher than the pull-up 1RM, which would narrow the gap by an amount nobody has measured.
4) Bodyweight is stated as a default, but that is unconfirmed: the facts say "probably a default". The "(C11)" tag does not match the athlete's constraint C11, which is no competition. The 110 kg total is a historical self-test, not the DB-logged figure. The DB default affects in-program PULL totals and the proposed marker, not necessarily the historical 75 kg.

RECOMMENDATION (recommendationOk=false as written)
It is observation-only, changes no protocol element, and violates no fixed constraint. But it is mis-specified:
- "PULL focus total ÷ BW" is the 3×3 working load, about 81% of the control REF (87.5 → REF 108). Comparing it to a heuristic defined on a 1RM or 3RM is a category error, and the gap it shows would be inflated.
- Spec §6.3 forbids describing REF ratios as %1RM and limits REF÷BW to a trend proxy. A heuristic threshold line drawn against the marker would imply that equivalence.
- §16 already displays PULL target total, calculated bodyweight and locked added load, and §21 already records REF÷7-day BW.

An acceptable revision would show a PULL REF-based added-load÷(measured 7-day bodyweight) trend next to the rung state. It would be labelled as a proxy, not a 1RM. The full heuristic range (+50% to about +75%, with basis and grip caveats) would appear only as text, not as a readiness line.

## C24 · comparison · strength · 확신 medium · 변경 등급 none

At the level of principles, REF5 keeps what was useful in the programs the athlete stalled on and addresses each diagnosed failure mode:
- Squat frequency: 5/3/1 Krypteia trains each main lift about once every 2 weeks.
- Per-session volume: nSuns runs 9 main-lift sets including an AMRAP, plus 8 secondary sets, against REF5's 10 sets in total.
- Unnoticed TM drift: Tactical Barbell Operator runs largely open-loop from a TM, with retests every 6-12 weeks.
REF5's session queue, micro valve and no-debt rule fit an irregular schedule: 14 Plan-B sessions at 2.45/wk, median 47 min, three 8-day gaps absorbed without manual action. Its nearest documented relatives are the 'time-poor' minimal-dose approach, GZCLP's 'bar speed slows noticeably' stopping cue, and Sheiko-style submaximal practice. What it gives up is a periodic maximal reality check and finer load tracking (C10); in autoregulation trials that costs little, and the estimate is uncertain. Both lenses agree.

**근거**

- Wendler 2021, Krypteia (2 days/wk): main lifts rotate over 2 weeks. https://www.jimwendler.com/blogs/jimwendler-com/krypteia-2-days-week
- nSuns 5/3/1 LP (Lift Vault, secondary): TM 90%, 9 main sets including an AMRAP plus 8 secondary sets per session, AMRAP-driven weekly TM increases. https://liftvault.com/programs/powerlifting/n-suns-lifting-spreadsheets/
- Tactical Barbell Operator (Liftosaur, secondary): TM 90%, waves of 63-85% 1RM, 'no AMRAP' and 'no built-in auto-regulation', retests every 6-12 weeks. https://www.liftosaur.com/programs/tactical-barbell-operator . Correction from lens A: Tactical Barbell does offer a 2-day template (Fighter); the 'wouldn't recommend' remark was about splitting lifts across 2 sessions.
- Pritchard & Androulakis-Korakakis 2023, SBS 'Effective Strength Training for the Time-Poor' (coaching synthesis): a handful of 1-5-rep sets per lift per week at RPE ~7.5-9.5, SQ/BP 2-3x/wk, DL ~1x/wk. https://www.strongerbyscience.com/training-for-time-poor/
- GZCLP (r/Fitness wiki): AMRAPs stop with ~1-2 RIR 'when bar speed slows noticeably'. https://thefitness.wiki/routines/gzclp/ . JTS Russian seminar: average intensity ~70%, lifters 'rarely ever miss a rep'. https://www.jtsstrength.com/what-i-learned-at-the-russian-strength-seminar/
- Hickmott et al. 2022: autoregulated vs fixed load, 1RM MD +2.07 kg (not significant). https://link.springer.com/article/10.1186/s40798-021-00404-9 . Zhang et al. 2021, Front Physiol 12:651112: pooled ES 0.64, driven by APRE (AMRAP-based); RPE and VBT subgroups not significant. https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/fphys.2021.651112/full . Huang et al. 2025, J Exerc Sci Fit 23(4):360-369: no moderate or large back-squat differences between autoregulation methods and fixed percentages, though APRE ranked first. https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/40791980/
- Fyfe et al. 2022: minimal-dose training may limit negative affective responses that hurt adherence. https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s40279-021-01605-8 . Wendler 2024: 'When there is a doubt, there is no doubt'. https://www.jimwendler.com/blogs/jimwendler-com/building-strength-and-the-training-max
- Internal record: 14 Plan-B sessions over 5.7 weeks; median 47 min from the first squat work set to save; 4 micros, 3 of them automatic.

**추론**

This is a coaching-level, principles-only comparison: none of these programs has controlled trials of its own, and several descriptions come from secondary summaries. The match between REF5's design and the athlete's reported failures is a direct reading of the program documents and history. That it improves outcomes is a hypothesis the next 2-3 months of data, above the June loads, will test (C8). The missing maximal reality check is a consequence of the user's C9/C10, not a design flaw. In autoregulation meta-analyses the AMRAP-based method (APRE) drove most of the benefit, which REF5 forgoes by constraint.

**반박 검토(evidence)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: At the level of principles, REF5 targets three of the athlete's diagnosed failure modes. Each was diagnosed from program documents and history, not from trials.
- Squat frequency: original 5/3/1 squats about once a week and the 2-day Krypteia variant about once every 2 weeks (which variant the athlete ran is unrecorded). REF5 squats every session. Evidence for going from 1 to 2 or more exposures a week is consistent but small, and partly volume-mediated (Pelland 2025; Tao 2026; Grgic 2018).
- Per-session volume: nSuns runs 9 main sets plus 8 secondary sets per session; REF5 runs about 10 sets in total.
- TM drift: Tactical Barbell Operator is largely open-loop, with forced TM increases or a 6-12-week retest.
REF5 does not adopt the athlete's 'insufficient proximity' diagnosis; it stays deliberately short of failure. That is consistent with evidence that proximity matters little for strength once load is controlled (Robinson 2024).
The session queue, the micro valve and the no-debt rule are designed for irregular schedules. Early logs (13 v1.4 sessions, ~2.9/wk, 9/05-10/06) show sessions generated without manual action. The current 8-day gap is not yet resolved.
At the principle level, REF5 resembles minimal-dose 'time-poor' programming. It also shares the no-miss ethos of Sheiko-style submaximal training, though not Sheiko's volume or frequency. Its slowdown cue resembles the r/Fitness wiki's GZCLP advice for ending AMRAP sets.
By constraint (C9, C10), REF5 gives up a periodic maximal or AMRAP reality check and finer RIR-based load tracking. Trial evidence puts that cost at small to moderate and uncertain:
- Hickmott 2022: not significant.
- Huang 2025: no moderate or large squat differences between methods.
- Graham & Cleather 2021: the best-matched RCT (trained men, squat twice a week) found RIR autoregulation beat fixed loading, +10.8% vs +7.1%.
- AMRAP-based APRE ranks highest in pooled analyses, from weak studies.

The principles-level mapping is defensible, and most program descriptions match their sources. Wendler's Krypteia page confirms a 2-week main-lift rotation. Lift Vault confirms nSuns runs 9 main sets plus 8 secondary sets. Liftosaur and Lift Vault confirm Tactical Barbell Operator works from a TM with retests every 6-12 weeks and no AMRAP. The SBS time-poor page is confirmed. The JTS seminar was in fact led by Sheiko and Koklyaev: about 70% average intensity, and lifters "rarely ever miss a rep". Five fidelity problems remain.

(1) 5/3/1 exemplar. Nothing in the athlete record says Krypteia is the 5/3/1 variant they ran; the record only says "5/3/1 Wendler, too little squat frequency". Original 4-day 5/3/1 squats once a week, and Krypteia is the most extreme variant. Krypteia's assistance also includes weekly squat-pattern work such as DB squats. The frequency evidence supports moving to 2 or more exposures a week, but only modestly. Pelland 2025 finds the largest step is from 1 to 2 sessions. Tao 2026 finds |g|=0.17. Grgic 2018 found no effect when volume was equated, and lower-body p=0.07.

(2) "In autoregulation trials that costs little" omits the best-matched trial. Graham & Cleather 2021 tested RIR-autoregulated against fixed loading in trained men squatting twice a week for 12 weeks. Back squat rose +10.8% vs +7.1% (p=0.006), because loads kept pace with strength. This is exactly the finer tracking that C10 gives up. Helms 2018 also found a squat ES of 0.50 favouring RPE (not significant). The trials compare autoregulation against fixed percentages, not against a performance-gated loop like REF5's, so they bound REF5's cost only by assumption. They also do not measure the C9 cost (no periodic maximal check) at all. The closest analogue, AMRAP-based APRE, carries the largest pooled effects: Zhang 2021 ES 0.78; Huang 2025 ranks it first for squat and bench. Those effects rest on weak studies; Mann 2010 is non-randomized with baseline imbalance. The claim's own reasoning admits this, which contradicts the "costs little" headline. Accurate wording: "small to moderate and uncertain".

(3) "Addresses each diagnosed failure mode" overstates. The athlete's Tactical Barbell diagnosis was "insufficient proximity" plus TM drift, and the shared diagnosis was frequency + proximity + recoverable volume. REF5 stays deliberately short of failure (RPE 7-8). So on proximity it departs from the athlete's diagnosis rather than addressing it. The departure is consistent with Robinson 2024: proximity matters little for strength once load is controlled. The 5x5 stall is not mapped.

(4) The internal record conflicts with the harness-verified record. That record shows 13 v1.4 sessions from 9/05 to 10/06 at ~2.9/wk and one 8-day gap still open through 10/14. "14 sessions at 2.45/wk" puts the open gap in the denominator. "Three 8-day gaps absorbed" cannot be checked, and the latest gap has no later session yet to show it was absorbed. The 47-minute median is also unverified.

(5) "Nearest relatives" is looser than stated. The GZCLP bar-speed cue is the r/Fitness wiki's advice for ending AMRAP sets at 1-2 RIR, and the page does not attribute it to LeFever. REF5 instead uses slowdown to end fixed sets and to grade them. The resemblance to Sheiko is only "no misses". Sheiko's athletes train 4-8 times a week at very high volume, which is the opposite of REF5's minimal-volume route. Fyfe 2022's adherence argument comes from general and older populations.

The claim makes no protocol recommendation (changeClass none) and stays within the user's constraints.

**반박 검토(applicability)**: holds-with-revision · 수정 확신 medium · 권고 유지 True

수정 주장: At the level of principles, REF5 answers three of the athlete's diagnoses and deliberately declines the fourth.
- Squat frequency: standard 5/3/1 templates train squat as a main lift about once a week (Krypteia: once every 2 weeks). REF5 squats every session, with hard exposures about 1.75/wk.
- Per-session volume: nSuns runs 9 main-lift sets (including an AMRAP) plus 8 secondary sets per session. REF5 runs about 10–11 work sets in total.
- Unnoticed TM drift: the athlete's own TB Operator log (54 sessions, March–July) shows squat about 3x/wk with forced block increments up to 92.5x3x3 and 97.5x1, with no feedback rule. REF5 instead raises load only after a clean judgment window.
- Insufficient proximity, the athlete's own TB diagnosis: REF5 does not answer it with more effort. It ends sets at the first obvious slowdown at a session RPE of about 7–8, and supplies heavy loading (80% or more on every hard exposure) instead. Evidence that strength is largely insensitive to proximity once load is controlled supports this choice.

REF5's session queue, micro valve and no-make-up rule (§19.2) fit an irregular schedule. Plan B ran 14 sessions from 9/05 to 10/14 (about 2.5/wk; median about 47 min from first squat work set to save). Its 2 micros were both automatic. Its one 8-day gap ended in a NORMAL session with SQ, BP and OHP all PASS. Across all 29 REF5 sessions there were three 8-day gaps: two needed no action, and one was followed by a user-chosen micro, the optional §19.4 step.

The closest documented relatives share single elements rather than the whole design: the 'time-poor' minimal-dose approach (dose and frequency), GZCLP's 'bar speed slows noticeably' cue (stopping rule only), and the non-failure ethos it shares with Sheiko and with TB itself.

What REF5 gives up:
- a periodic maximal reality check (C9 plus no AMRAP);
- finer load tracking (C10 plus no AMRAP or velocity device).

In autoregulation meta-analyses that costs little on average, and the estimate is uncertain. The most applicable squat trial (Graham & Cleather 2021) found a larger benefit when loads keep pace with capacity. That cost could matter most in the current phase, where every window has ended INCREASE.

I checked the claim against the spec, the engine's snapshots and the production DB. I only read the DB (SELECT queries on workout_log, workout_set, generated_session and plan_progress_event for user 6f47ec53). The principle-level comparison mostly holds. Three of its record facts are wrong, it skips one of the athlete's own diagnoses, and it overstates two other points.

What holds:
1. The spec contains every mechanism the claim names: the no-debt rule (§19.2 / line 82 "놓친 세션을 보충하지 않는다"), the session queue (§12) and the automatic micro triggers (§8).
2. The nSuns and TB Operator descriptions match the verified sources.
3. The athlete's real TB log backs up the "unnoticed TM drift" diagnosis. Plan "Tactical Barbell Operator Custom Program" has 54 sessions from 3/08 to 7/17. Squat was trained in every session, about 3x/week at 3 sets each. Top loads rose by forced increments with no feedback rule: 85x3, then 90x1, 90x3, 95x1, 92.5x3x3 and 97.5x1x3. The 92.5x3 @ RPE 9–10 that anchors the athlete's 1RM came from this drift.
4. Plan B ("REF5 Adaptive Strength 프로그램 2") has 14 sessions from 9/05 to 10/14. That is 2.45/wk when counted to 10/15, or 2.51 to 10/14. The median time from first squat work set to save is about 47 min, after excluding a 10/04 entry back-filled 2 days late and a 0.3-min entry on 9/25.
5. The 10/14 session after the gap was NORMAL, and SQ_H3, BP_FOCUS and OHP all came back PASS.

What is wrong or overstated:
(a) Record facts are mixed across two plans.
- Plan B has only ONE 8-day gap (10/06 to 10/14) and 2 micros: 9/11 (NORMAL_SESSION_DENSITY) and 9/25 (CONSECUTIVE_PROGRAM_DAYS + density). Both were automatic.
- "Three 8-day gaps" and "4 micros, 3 automatic" count all 29 REF5 sessions. Two of those gaps (7/26 to 8/03 and 8/13 to 8/21) fall in Plan A, which ran the v1.1–1.3 protocol.
- Not every gap was absorbed without manual action. The first session after the 8/13 to 8/21 gap was a micro with microReasons ["MANUAL"], which is the optional micro in §19.4. On 10/14 the user also previewed a MANUAL micro three times before starting NORMAL.
(b) "Addresses each diagnosed failure mode" leaves out the athlete's main TB diagnosis, insufficient proximity, and the shared diagnosis (frequency + proximity + recoverable volume).
- REF5 deliberately does not raise effort-based proximity: sets stop at the first obvious slowdown and the session target is RPE 7–8.
- What it offers instead is consistent loading at 80% or more on every hard exposure, plus evidence that proximity barely affects strength once load is controlled (Robinson 2024).
- On the athlete's record, TB already gave more squat frequency and volume (9 sets/wk) and heavier top work than REF5's current H3 85.
- So REF5 rejects that diagnosis rather than addressing it, and the claim should say so.
(c) Nothing in the repo or the constraints sheet shows the athlete ran Krypteia; the sheet says only "5/3/1 Wendler". Standard 5/3/1 templates train squat as a main lift about once a week. The frequency diagnosis holds either way, but Krypteia should not be presented as the program the athlete ran.
(d) The "relatives" overreach.
- The GZCLP cue ends AMRAPs at about 1–2 RIR inside an AMRAP-driven system. Only the cue resembles REF5.
- Lens A judged Sheiko's route (high volume at about 70%) to be the opposite of REF5's.
- TB Operator, which the athlete stalled on, shares the same "below fatigue and failure" ethos. Submaximal practice therefore does not set REF5 apart from the failed program, and "both lenses agree" is overstated.
(e) The costs are attributed to the wrong constraints, and "costs little" is too strong for this athlete.
- Losing the maximal reality check comes from C9 plus the no-AMRAP principle, not C10. Coarser load tracking comes from C10 plus no AMRAP or velocity device.
- "Costs little" rests on Hickmott's non-significant +2.07 kg. The most applicable trial, Graham & Cleather 2021 (trained men, squat twice a week, volume-matched), favoured autoregulation +10.8% vs +7.1% and attributed the gap to loads keeping pace with strength.
- This athlete's record (every window INCREASE, zero main-lift HOLD/FAIL) fits loads lagging capacity, which is when that cost would be largest. "Small on average, possibly larger in the current catch-up phase, uncertain" is the defensible wording.

Recommendation check: the claim proposes no protocol change (changeClass none). It describes the costs of C9 and C10 without asking to relax them, so it violates none of the fixed constraints.
